I. On a January evening in the early 1870s, Christine Nilsson was singing *Faust* at the Academy of Music in New York. Though whispers had begun about a grand new opera house rising far uptown—beyond the Forties—a place meant to rival the opulence of Europe’s most celebrated halls—the city’s elite still gathered each winter in the modest, gilded red-and-gold boxes of the old Academy. Conservatives admired its intimacy and discomfort, which kept out the “new people” New York both feared and found irresistible; sentimentalists cherished its history; musicians praised its acoustics, a rare gift in spaces built for music. It was Madame Nilsson’s first performance that season, and what the newspapers now called an “exceptionally brilliant audience” had arrived by private broughams, spacious family landaus, or the more practical but equally respectable Brown coupe. To arrive in a Brown coupe was nearly as dignified as arriving in one’s own carriage—and leaving the same way offered the delightful liberty of slipping into the first available vehicle in line, rather than waiting for one’s own coachman, whose nose, reddened by cold and gin, would soon gleam under the Academy’s portico. The livery-stableman had mastered the American mind: people wanted to leave pleasure faster than they wanted to reach it.

When Newland Archer stepped into the club box from the back, the curtain had just risen on the garden scene. There was no reason he couldn’t have come earlier—he’d dined at seven with his mother and sister, lingered over a cigar in the Gothic library, where black-walnut bookcases and finial-topped chairs allowed smoking, the only room in the house where such indulgence was permitted. But New York was a metropolis, and in metropolises, arriving early at the opera was simply not done. What was or wasn’t “the thing” shaped Newland’s world as decisively as ancestral taboos once ruled his forebears. And then there was his personal preference: he liked to savor anticipation. Especially when the pleasure was refined, as most of his were. This night, the moment he awaited was so rare and exquisite that even if he’d timed his arrival perfectly with the prima donna’s stage manager, he could not have entered at a more perfect instant than just as she sang: “He loves me—*he loves me not*—*he loves me!*”—her voice clear as dew, scattering notes like falling daisy petals. Of course, she sang “*M’ama!*” not “he loves me,” since an unshakable rule dictated that German texts in French operas performed by Swedish singers be translated into Italian for the benefit of English-speaking audiences. It seemed as natural to Newland as other customs that governed his life: using two silver-backed brushes with his monogram in blue enamel to part his hair, or always wearing a flower—preferably a gardenia—in his buttonhole.

“*M’ama… non m’ama…*” she sang, and then, with a triumphant flourish, “*M’ama!*” pressing the disheveled daisy to her lips and lifting her eyes to the small, brown-faced Faust-Capoul, who struggled in a tight purple velvet doublet and plumed cap to appear pure and true, though clearly outmatched by his artless victim.

Newland leaned against the wall at the back of the box and turned from the stage to scan the opposite side of the house. Directly across sat the box of old Mrs. Manson Mingott, whose immense size had long made opera attendance impossible—but whose presence was always ensured by younger relatives on fashionable nights. Tonight, her daughter-in-law, Mrs. Lovell Mingott, and her daughter, Mrs. Welland, filled the front seats. Behind them, slightly withdrawn, sat a young girl in white, her eyes fixed in rapture on the lovers onstage. As Madame Nilsson’s “*M’ama!*” rang through the silent hall—every box hushed during the Daisy Song—a warm pink bloomed on the girl’s cheeks, climbed her brow to the roots of her golden braids, and spread gently over the curve of her breast, where it met a modest tulle tucker fastened with a single gardenia. She lowered her gaze to the bouquet of lilies-of-the-valley resting on her lap, and Newland saw her white-gloved fingers brush the blossoms tenderly.

He exhaled softly, pleased with himself, and returned his attention to the stage. No expense had been spared on the set, acknowledged even by those familiar with Paris and Vienna’s opera houses as remarkably beautiful. The foreground was covered in emerald green cloth. In the distance, symmetrical mounds of moss framed shrubs shaped like orange trees, their branches heavy with large pink and red roses. Gigantic pansies—larger than the roses themselves—sprouted beneath the rose trees, resembling the floral pen-wipers crafted by churchgoers for fashionable clergymen. Here and there, a daisy grafted onto a rose branch bloomed with a lushness that hinted at Mr. Luther Burbank’s future wonders.

At the center of this enchanted garden, Madame Nilsson—dressed in white cashmere slashed with pale blue satin, a reticule dangling from a blue sash, her yellow braids neatly arranged—listened demurely to M. Capoul’s ardent advances, feigning ignorance whenever he suggested, by word or glance, the ground-floor window of the neat brick villa jutting from the right wing.

“The darling!” Newland thought, his gaze drifting back to the girl with the lilies-of-the-valley. “She doesn’t even know what it means.” He watched her absorbed face with a quiet sense of possession—pride in his own understanding mingled with reverence for her innocence. “We’ll read *Faust* together… by the Italian lakes…” he imagined, vaguely blending the setting of his imagined honeymoon with the literary masterpieces he intended to introduce to his bride. That very afternoon, May Welland had let him know she “cared”—New York’s sacred phrase of maidenly confession—and already, his imagination leapt ahead of engagement rings, betrothal kisses, and the march from *Lohengrin*, picturing her beside him in some ancient European charm.

He didn’t want his future wife to be naive. He meant her—through his guidance—to grow into a woman of social grace, quick wit, and charm, able to hold her own among the most admired married women of the “younger set,” where it was customary to attract admiration while playfully rejecting it. If he had looked deeper into his vanity—as he sometimes almost did—he might have admitted he wished her as worldly and eager to please as the married woman who had captivated him for two restless years—without, of course, any trace of the flaw that had nearly destroyed her life and disrupted his plans for an entire winter.

How such a paradox—fire and ice—could endure in a harsh world, he never bothered to consider. But he was content to believe in it, knowing it reflected the views of all the impeccably groomed, waistcoat-clad, flower-adorned gentlemen who occupied the club box, exchanged nods with him, and swept their opera glasses over the circle of ladies—products of the same system.

In matters of intellect and art, Newland felt himself superior to these men. He had likely read more, thought more, seen more of the world than any of them. Individually, they showed their limitations. But together, they represented “New York,” and the instinct of masculine solidarity made him accept their moral code without question. To challenge it would be troublesome—and, indeed, poor form.

“Well—upon my soul!” exclaimed Lawrence Lefferts, turning his opera glass sharply away from the stage.

Lefferts was New York’s foremost authority on “form.” He’d spent more time studying its intricacies than anyone else, yet his mastery seemed innate. One needed only to see him—from the slope of his bald forehead and the curl of his fair mustache to his long patent-leather feet—to understand that knowing how to wear fine clothes carelessly, and carry height with effortless grace, was born, not learned. A young admirer once said: “If anyone can tell you when to wear a black tie with evening clothes and when not to, it’s Larry Lefferts.” His judgment on pumps versus patent-leather Oxfords was never questioned.

“My God,” he murmured, handing his glass to old Sillerton Jackson.

Newland followed Lefferts’s gaze and saw, with surprise, that the cause of his exclamation was a new figure entering Mrs. Mingott’s box. A slim young woman, slightly shorter than May Welland, with brown curls framing her temples and held by a narrow band of diamonds. The look—then called “Josephine”—was echoed in her dark blue velvet gown, dramatically gathered beneath her bosom by a wide, old-fashioned clasp. Unaware of the attention she drew, she paused in the center of the box, debating with Mrs. Welland whether to take her seat in the front right corner. Then, with a slight smile, she yielded and settled beside Mrs. Lovell Mingott, who sat in the opposite corner.

Sillerton Jackson returned the glass to Lefferts. The entire club box turned, expectant. Old Mr. Jackson was as much an authority on “family” as Lefferts was on “form.” He knew every branch of New York’s interwoven kinships—how the Mingotts connected to the Dallases via the Thorleys, or how the Philadelphia Thorleys linked to the Albany Chiverses (never confused with the Manson Chiverses of University Place). He could recite the traits of each family: the notorious stinginess of the Long Island Leffertses, the tendency of the Rushworths to make disastrous matches, the madness that struck every second generation of the Albany Chiverses—whom their New York cousins refused to marry, except for poor Medora Manson, whose mother was a Rushworth.

Beyond genealogy, Jackson carried in his mind a record of the scandals and secrets simmering beneath New York society’s calm surface. He was said to be the only man who could reveal who Julius Beaufort truly was—or what became of handsome Bob Spicer, Mrs. Mingott’s father, who vanished mysteriously after his marriage, taking trust funds with him, on the very day a beautiful Spanish dancer left the old Battery Opera House for Cuba.

But these secrets remained locked in his memory—not only because of honor, but because discretion increased his chances of learning more. So the club waited, breathless, as Jackson handed back the opera glass. For a moment, he studied the audience through his filmy blue eyes, veiled by aged lids. Then he gave his mustache a thoughtful twist and said simply:

“I didn’t think the Mingotts would have tried it on.”

II. Newland Archer found himself caught in a sudden, uncomfortable swirl of emotion as the opera unfolded. It was unsettling—almost absurd—that the box drawing all eyes from the men of New York’s elite should be the very one where his fiancée sat between her mother and aunt. For a moment, he couldn’t place the woman in the elegant Empire-style gown, nor understand why her presence stirred such quiet commotion among those who knew better. Then it clicked—a jolt of realization followed by a flash of irritation. No, surely not the Mingotts! They wouldn’t dare. But they had. There was no mistaking it: the woman was May Welland’s cousin, Ellen Olenska—the one always spoken of with hushed sympathy as “poor Ellen.”

Archer had heard she’d arrived unexpectedly from Europe just days before. He even recalled Miss Welland mentioning she’d visited her cousin, who was staying with old Mrs. Mingott. He admired family loyalty, and respected the Mingotts for standing by their few misfits with unwavering pride. He had no room in his heart for petty judgment; indeed, he approved that May would show kindness to her troubled relative in private. But this—this public appearance at the Opera, seated beside May herself, just weeks before their engagement was to be announced—was something else entirely. It felt reckless, almost scandalous. And yes, he thought much like old Sillerton Jackson: the Mingotts wouldn’t have done this. Or would they?

He knew full well that if any woman dared what society allowed, it was old Mrs. Manson Mingott—the matriarch whose name carried weight even beyond Fifth Avenue. She had risen from modest beginnings—Catherine Spicer of Staten Island, daughter of a man disgraced, without wealth or status enough to erase the stain—and yet married into the Mingott fortune, defied convention, and built a life of bold defiance. Her house, pale cream stone rising like a defiant monument near Central Park when brown sandstone ruled the day, stood as proof of her courage. She lived there, surrounded by pre-Revolutionary furniture and relics from Louis Napoleon’s Tuileries, as though nothing about her life were extraordinary—not the location, not the French windows that opened like doors, not even the fact that her daughters, married to an Italian marquis and an English banker, never returned.

People whispered about her. Some said she lacked beauty—something New York prized above all, a gift that could excuse many flaws. Others claimed, unkindly, that like her imperial namesake, she had risen through sheer willpower and steely resolve. Yet beneath her audacity was a quiet dignity. She had never been touched by scandal. When her husband died young, he left his fortune tightly bound—out of distrust for her family—but she forged ahead anyway. She mingled in foreign courts, entertained opera stars, dined with ambassadors, befriended Mme. Taglioni, and remained untouched by gossip. Sillerton Jackson once declared that the only thing she lacked was the charm of youth—yet she remained unshaken, a force of nature in a world that valued discretion.

Though she had long since freed her fortune, she remained thrifty, refusing to spend extravagantly on food. Her table, simple and often bland, drew criticism from relatives who saw it as a blot on the Mingott name—once synonymous with fine living. But still, people came. Even Lovell Mingott, trying to restore family honor by hiring the city’s best chef, was met with her cheerful retort: “What’s the use of two good cooks in one family, now that I’ve married the girls and can’t eat sauces?”

As Archer reflected on these things, he glanced again toward the Mingott box. He saw Mrs. Welland and her sister-in-law facing the crowd with the calm assurance the Mingotts were known for—unshaken, composed. Only May showed a hint of color, perhaps because she sensed his gaze. And then there was Ellen Olenska—seated gracefully, her eyes fixed on the stage, her posture relaxed, her dress revealing more shoulder and bosom than New York deemed proper for a woman who wished to remain unseen.

To Archer, nothing was more sacred than *Taste*—that invisible code governing every gesture, every word, every choice. Ellen Olenska’s pale face, serious and thoughtful, seemed fitting for her situation. But her dress—without a tucker, sloping freely from her delicate shoulders—felt like a breach of decorum. He couldn’t help but worry about May being exposed to someone so careless of social rules. What kind of influence could she have?

Behind him, voices rose during the Mephistopheles-and-Martha scenes.

“After all,” one young man began, “just *what* happened?”

“Well—she left him. Nobody denies that.”

“He’s an awful brute, isn’t he?” asked Thorley, earnest and eager to defend her.

“The worst,” replied Lefferts. “I knew him in Nice. Half-paralyzed, sneering white fellow—handsome head, but eyes with too many lashes. A collector of china. Paid any price for both women and porcelain.”

A ripple of laughter passed through the audience.

“And then?” Thorley pressed.

“She ran off with his secretary.”

“Oh,” Thorley said, deflated.

“It didn’t last,” Lefferts added. “She was alone in Venice months later. Lovell went to fetch her. Said she was broken-hearted. That’s fine—but bringing her here, to the Opera, with May? That’s another matter.”

“Maybe,” Thorley ventured, “she’s too unhappy to stay home.”

The remark earned a mocking laugh, and Thorley blushed, pretending he meant it as a clever double entendre.

“Still,” someone murmured, glancing toward Archer, “it’s odd to bring Miss Welland at all.”

“Oh, that’s part of the plan,” Lefferts chuckled. “Granny’s orders. When she does something, she does it thoroughly.”

The act ended. The box stirred. And suddenly, Archer felt a surge of purpose. The urge to be the first to enter the Mingott box—to announce his engagement to May, to stand by her through whatever storms might come—overpowered every hesitation. He moved swiftly down the red corridors, arriving just as the curtain fell.

His eyes met May’s. In that instant, they understood each other—no words needed. Their world lived in whispers, in subtle glances, in unspoken meanings. And this silent recognition brought them closer than any declaration ever could. Her eyes said: *You see why Mamma brought me.* His reply: *I would never have let you stay away.*

Mrs. Welland turned to him. “You know my niece, Countess Olenska?”

Archer bowed, as custom dictated, not extending his hand. Ellen Olenska inclined her head slightly, her pale-gloved hands clasped over a fan of eagle feathers. After greeting Mrs. Lovell Mingott—a large blonde woman in creaking satin—Archer sat beside May.

“I hope you’ve told Madame Olenska we’re engaged?” he murmured. “I want everyone to know. I want to announce it tonight at the ball.”

May’s cheeks flushed like dawn. Her eyes sparkled. “If you can convince Mamma,” she said softly. “But why change what’s already settled?”

He didn’t answer. His gaze held hers, warm and sure. Then, smiling more confidently, she added, “Tell her yourself—I give you permission. She says you used to play together when you were children.”

She pulled her chair back, and with deliberate, almost theatrical grace, Archer took the seat beside the Countess.

“We *did* used to play,” she said, turning her grave eyes to his. “You were a horrid boy. You kissed me once behind a door. But it was your cousin Vandie Newland—who never looked at me—that I was in love with.”

Her gaze swept the curved line of boxes. “Ah, how this brings it all back. I see everyone here in knickerbockers and pantalettes.”

Her voice trailed with a faint foreign accent, soft and nostalgic. She smiled gently at him.

And yet, despite the warmth in her tone, Archer felt a pang of discomfort. To speak so lightly of childhood memories, so casually, in front of this assembly—this court of moral judgment—seemed terribly out of place. Misplaced flippancy was worse than anything. He answered stiffly: “Yes. You’ve been away a very long time.”

“Oh, centuries,” she said, laughing softly. “So long that I’m sure I’m dead and buried, and this dear old place is heaven.”

And somehow, though he couldn’t explain why, that struck him as the most disrespectful thing imaginable.

III. It always unfolded the same way. On the evening of her annual ball, Mrs. Julius Beaufort never missed a performance at the Opera. In fact, she timed her grand event precisely to coincide with an opera night—proof, as society saw it, of her effortless grace and supreme command over domestic affairs. Her household ran so flawlessly that she could leave everything in capable hands, trusting her staff to manage every detail while she graced the stage boxes with her presence. The Beauforts’ home was one of the few in New York to boast a dedicated ballroom—one older even than Mrs. Manson Mingott’s or the Headly Chiverses’. At a time when hosting a ball meant hastily covering floors with "crash" paper and hauling furniture upstairs, the Beauforts maintained a room reserved solely for dancing, left dark and still for all but one night a year. Its gilt chairs stood stacked in corners, its chandelier wrapped in cloth—yet this very extravagance was seen as a mark of distinction, a quiet triumph over the modesty others now deemed fashionable.

Mrs. Archer, known for distilling social truths into neat aphorisms, once remarked: “We all have our pet common people.” Though bold, the sentiment resonated quietly in many privileged hearts. But the Beauforts were not merely common—they were something else entirely. Some whispered they were worse. Still, Mrs. Beaufort came from one of America’s most respected lineages. She had been Regina Dallas, a breathtaking beauty from South Carolina, introduced to New York society by her cousin Medora Manson—a woman famed for her reckless kindness. To be linked to the Mansons and Rushworths granted one a *droit de cité* in elite circles, as Mr. Sillerton Jackson once put it, recalling his days at the Tuileries. But did that right vanish upon marrying Julius Beaufort?

Who, then, was Beaufort? He passed as English—charming, handsome, sharp-tongued, generous, and quick-witted. He arrived in America with letters from Mrs. Manson Mingott’s English son-in-law, a banker, and swiftly carved out a powerful role in finance. Yet he lived recklessly, spoke bitterly, and carried secrets like shadows. When Medora announced her cousin’s engagement to him, it was another entry in her long ledger of imprudent acts. But time often vindicates folly. Two years after their marriage, young Mrs. Beaufort presided over what was widely acknowledged as the most celebrated house in New York. No one knew exactly how she achieved it. She seemed idle, passive—some called her dull—but dressed like a goddess, draped in pearls, growing ever more radiant with each passing season, she reigned in her husband’s imposing brownstone, drawing the city’s elite without lifting a finger.

The truth, those in the know suspected, was that Beaufort himself managed everything—the staff, the chef, the gardeners, the guest list, even the punch and the notes his wife sent. But he performed these tasks behind closed doors, presenting instead the image of a careless millionaire strolling into his own drawing-room as if he were a visitor, remarking casually, “My wife’s gloxinias are a marvel, aren’t they? I believe she gets them from Kew.” His secret, everyone agreed, was his flair for making things seem effortless. Rumors swirled—he’d fled England under scandalous circumstances, perhaps aided by the international bank where he’d once worked. But Beaufort swept such whispers aside as lightly as any other. With his charm and confidence, he carried the world along with him. For over two decades, saying “going to the Beauforts” had become as natural as visiting Mrs. Manson Mingott, though with the added pleasure of savoring hot canvas-back ducks and aged wines instead of lukewarm champagne and reheated croquettes.

That evening, Mrs. Beaufort appeared in her box just before the Jewel Song, as she always did. And when, as expected, she rose at the end of the third act, wrapped herself in her opera cloak, and vanished into the wings, New York understood: in half an hour, the ball would begin.

The Beaufort mansion was a source of pride for New Yorkers, especially on this night. They were among the first to own their own red velvet carpet, rolled down the steps by their own footmen beneath their own awning—no longer renting it with supper and chairs. They also pioneered letting ladies remove their cloaks in the hall, rather than shuffling up to the hostess’s bedroom to re-curl their hair under gaslight. Beaufort reportedly said he assumed all his wife’s friends had maids who ensured proper coiffures before leaving home.

Their house was boldly designed around the ballroom, allowing guests to march through a sequence of elegant rooms—the sea-green, the crimson, and the bouton d'or—each leading deeper into opulence. From afar, the polished parquetry reflected the many-candled lustres, and beyond lay a conservatory where camellias and tree-ferns formed a lush canopy over black-and-gold bamboo seats.

Newland Archer arrived somewhat late, as befitted his station. He handed his overcoat to the silk-stockinged footmen—though the stockings were one of Beaufort’s minor vanities—and lingered a moment in the library, lined with Spanish leather and furnished with Buhl and malachite, where men were chatting and adjusting their dancing gloves. Finally, he joined the line of guests being received by Mrs. Beaufort at the threshold of the crimson drawing-room.

Archer felt distinctly uneasy. He hadn’t returned to his club after the opera—as most young men did—but had walked partway up Fifth Avenue before turning back. He feared the Mingotts might have gone too far—perhaps even brought Granny Mingott’s orders to bring the Countess Olenska to the ball. The tone in the club box had made clear how disastrous that would be. Though he remained resolved to “see the thing through,” he no longer felt quite so noble about defending his fiancée’s cousin after their brief exchange at the opera.

He wandered into the bouton d'or drawing-room, where Beaufort had dared hang *Love Victorious*, Bouguereau’s controversial nude. There, he found Mrs. Welland and her daughter near the ballroom door. Couples already glided across the floor beyond—tulle skirts swirling, candlelight catching on delicate blossoms in girls’ hair, on the sparkling aigrettes of newly married women, on polished shirt-fronts and fresh *glacé* gloves.

Miss Welland stood at the threshold, lilies-of-the-valley in hand (she carried nothing else), her face pale, her eyes alight with honest excitement. A group of young people gathered around her, laughing, clasping hands, exchanging teasing remarks. Mrs. Welland stood slightly apart, casting a look of measured approval. It was clear Miss Welland was announcing her engagement, while her mother played the part of reluctant parent, as tradition demanded.

Archer paused. It had been his wish that the announcement be made—but not like this. To declare their happiness in the clamor of a crowded ballroom felt like stripping it of its sacred privacy. His joy was deep enough that the surface mattered little—but he wished the surface had remained untouched. He was pleased to see May shared this feeling. Her eyes met his, pleading: *Remember—we’re doing this because it’s right.* No appeal could stir him more deeply. Yet he wished their reason had been something pure, something ideal—not merely poor Ellen Olenska.

The group parted for him with knowing smiles. After accepting their congratulations, he led his betrothed into the center of the ballroom and slipped his arm around her waist. “Now we won’t have to talk,” he said, smiling into her candid eyes as they drifted to the rhythm of the Blue Danube.

She didn’t speak. Her lips curved into a smile, but her gaze remained distant, fixed on some unseen vision. “Dear,” he whispered, pulling her closer. It struck him then that even in a ballroom, the first hours of engagement held a solemnity, almost a sacrament. What a new life it would be—with this purity, this light, this goodness beside him!

When the dance ended, the couple moved into the conservatory. Seated behind a screen of ferns and camellias, Archer pressed her gloved hand to his lips. “You see,” she said softly, “I did as you asked.”

“Yes,” he replied, smiling. “I couldn’t wait.” After a pause, he added, “Only I wish it hadn’t had to be here—at a ball.”

“Yes, I know,” she answered, meeting his glance with understanding. “But after all—even here, we’re alone, aren’t we?”

“Oh, dearest—always!” he exclaimed. Clearly, she would always understand; she would always say the perfect thing. The realization flooded him with joy. “The worst of it is,” he went on cheerfully, “that I want to kiss you and I can’t.”

As he spoke, he glanced around the conservatory, confirmed their privacy, and drew her close, pressing a fleeting kiss to her lips. To balance the daring, he led her to a bamboo sofa in a less secluded corner and sat beside her, plucking a lily-of-the-valley from her bouquet.

She remained silent. The world stretched out before them like a sunlit valley.

“Did you tell my cousin Ellen?” she asked after a moment, as if speaking through a dream.

He blinked, remembering he hadn’t. An instinctive reluctance to speak of such matters to the foreign woman had kept the words from forming. “No—I didn’t get the chance,” he lied quickly.

“Oh.” She looked disappointed, but resolute. “You must. I didn’t either, and I wouldn’t want her to think—”

“Of course not,” he said. “But shouldn’t you be the one to tell her?”

She considered. “If I’d done it at the right time, yes. But now that there’s been a delay, I think you should explain that I asked you to tell her at the opera—before we told everyone here. Otherwise, she might think I forgot her. You see, she’s family. And she’s been away so long… she’s rather sensitive.”

Archer looked at her with warmth. “Dear and great angel! Of course I’ll tell her.” He glanced toward the crowded ballroom, a hint of unease crossing his face. “But I haven’t seen her yet. Has she come?”

“No,” May replied. “At the last minute, she decided not to.”

“At the last minute?” he echoed, surprised she’d ever considered coming.

“Yes,” she said simply. “She loves dancing. But suddenly she decided her dress wasn’t smart enough for a ball—though we thought it was lovely. So Auntie had to take her home.”

“Oh, well,” Archer said, brushing it off with cheerful indifference. Nothing pleased him more than her steadfast adherence to the ritual of overlooking the “unpleasant”—a habit they both cherished. *She knows the real reason Ellen stayed away,* he thought, *but I’ll never let her see that I’m aware of the shadow hanging over her cousin’s reputation.*

IV. The next day brought the first of the customary betrothal visits, a ritual in New York that followed rules as rigid as they were unspoken. Newland Archer began by accompanying his mother and sister to call on Mrs. Welland, then joined her and May for a visit to old Mrs. Manson Mingott’s home, where they would receive the blessing of the family matriarch—a tradition both solemn and inevitable.

For Newland, visiting Mrs. Mingott was always a peculiar pleasure. Her house stood like a relic of another time, though not quite as ancient as some of the grand residences along University Place or lower Fifth Avenue. Those homes belonged firmly to the 1830s: dark rosewood consoles, fireplaces with black marble mantels, round arches, and towering mahogany bookcases filled with leather-bound volumes. But Mrs. Mingott had built later, and she had long since cast aside the heavy furniture of her youth, replacing it with the lighter, more ornate trappings of the Second Empire—frivolous upholstery, gilded mirrors, and curving lines that seemed almost theatrical.

She herself sat in the window of her ground-floor sitting room, as if waiting patiently for the world to catch up with her. There was no urgency in her gaze, only quiet confidence. She believed—without doubt—that one day the rough streets, wooden greenhouses, and goat-topped rocks surrounding her house would give way to elegant townhouses as stately as her own, perhaps even more so. And the cobblestones, rattled over by clattering omnibuses, would be replaced by smooth Parisian asphalt. Until then, she waited. And because everyone who mattered came to see *her*, she did not feel isolated. Her rooms were always full, her suppers as lavish as those of the Beauforts, without ever needing to add a single course to her menu.

Her body had changed dramatically over the years. Once a plump but active woman with delicate feet and ankles, she had been overwhelmed in middle age by an immense accumulation of flesh—like lava swallowing a city. Now she was vast, almost monumental, a natural force. Yet she accepted this transformation with the same calm resolve she had shown through every trial. In her advanced age, she looked at herself in the mirror and saw a nearly flawless expanse of firm pink and white skin, with just a small face faintly visible beneath—like a fossil awaiting excavation. A cascade of double chins led down to a snowy bosom covered in fine muslin, held in place by a miniature portrait of her late husband. Around her, waves of black silk flowed over the arms of her enormous chair, while two tiny white hands rested atop them, poised like gulls on a sea of fabric.

Long ago, the weight of her frame had made climbing stairs impossible. With characteristic independence, she had moved her reception rooms upstairs and settled herself—defying all social norms—on the ground floor. This meant that anyone seated beside her in the sitting room could glance through an open door and a looped-back damask curtain into her bedroom: a low, wide bed upholstered like a sofa, a vanity with lace trimmings, and a gilt-framed mirror. Visitors were both startled and intrigued by the arrangement—something straight out of French novels, where scandal lived in proximity, where rooms opened onto one another like secrets. It reminded Newland of the love scenes he’d imagined in *Monsieur de Camors*—scenes he now pictured unfolding in this very room. He smiled inwardly, amused by the irony: such a virtuous woman, living in a setting that whispered of forbidden affairs. Still, he thought with respect, if she had wanted a lover, she would have found one. She had the courage for it.

To everyone’s relief, the Countess Olenska was not present when the couple arrived. Mrs. Mingott said she had gone out—an odd choice, given the bright sunlight and the shopping hour. For a woman whose reputation was already fragile, such a public outing seemed indiscreet. But at least it spared the newly engaged pair from any awkwardness, from the shadow their future might cast over Ellen’s past.

The visit unfolded smoothly, as expected. Old Mrs. Mingott was delighted by the engagement—long anticipated, carefully discussed in family councils. When May showed her the ring—a large sapphire set in invisible claws—she admired it warmly. “It’s the new setting,” Mrs. Welland explained gently, glancing at Newland. “Of course it shows the stone beautifully, but it looks a little bare to old-fashioned eyes.”

“Old-fashioned eyes? I hope you don’t mean mine, my dear,” Mrs. Mingott replied, lifting the ring to her clear, glassless eyes. “I like all the novelties.” She returned the jewel. “Very handsome. Very generous. In my day, a cameo set in pearls was enough. But it’s the hand that sets off the ring, isn’t it, Mr. Archer?” She waved one of her tiny hands—nails sharp, wrists encircled by rolls of fat like ivory bracelets. “Mine was modeled in Rome by Ferrigiani. You should have May’s done. No doubt he will. Her hand is large—modern sports spread the joints—but the skin is white.”

Then she turned abruptly to Newland. “When’s the wedding?”

“Oh—” Mrs. Welland murmured, while Newland, smiling at May, answered, “As soon as possible, if you’ll back me up, Mrs. Mingott.”

“We must give them time to get to know each other,” Mrs. Welland interjected, with the proper air of reluctant caution.

“Know each other? Nonsense!” Mrs. Mingott declared. “Everyone in New York has always known everyone. Let the young man have his way. Don’t wait till the bubble’s off the wine. Marry them before Lent. I may catch pneumonia any winter now, and I want to give the wedding breakfast.”

The remarks were met with polite laughter, surprise, and gratitude. The visit was drawing to a close when the door opened—and in walked the Countess Olenska, in bonnet and mantle, followed by the unexpected figure of Julius Beaufort.

A murmur of greeting passed among the women. Mrs. Mingott extended her hand, showing Ferrigiani’s model to the banker. “Ha! Beaufort, this is a rare favor!”

“Thanks,” Beaufort said, his usual arrogance relaxed but present. “I wish it happened more often. I’m generally tied down. But I met the Countess Ellen in Madison Square, and she was kind enough to let me walk home with her.”

“Aha!” Mrs. Mingott exclaimed. “I hope the house will be gayer now that Ellen’s here!” She gestured toward a yellow armchair. “Sit down—sit down, Beaufort. Now I’ve got you, I want a good gossip. I hear your ball was magnificent. And I understand you invited Mrs. Lemuel Struthers?”

“Well, if you and Regina invite her, the thing’s settled,” Mrs. Mingott said, turning her attention fully to Beaufort. “We need new blood, new money—and I hear she’s still very good-looking.”

In the hall, as Mrs. Welland and May put on their furs, Newland noticed Ellen Olenska watching him with a soft, questioning smile.

“Of course you already know,” he said, returning her look with a shy laugh. “About May and me.”

“She scolded me last night at the Opera for not telling you,” he added. “She gave me orders to tell you we were engaged—but I couldn’t, not in that crowd.”

The smile deepened, spreading from her eyes to her lips. She looked younger, more like the bold, brown-eyed Ellen Mingott of his childhood. “Of course I know. Yes. And I’m so glad. But one doesn’t tell such things first in a crowd.”

The ladies were at the door. She reached out her hand. “Good-bye. Come and see me sometime,” she said, still looking at him.

In the carriage, as they rolled down Fifth Avenue, they spoke of Mrs. Mingott—her age, her spirit, her remarkable presence. No one mentioned Ellen. But Newland knew what his mother was thinking: *It’s a mistake for Ellen to be seen, the very day after her return, walking up Fifth Avenue at the busiest hour with Julius Beaufort.* And in his own mind, he added: *And she should know that a man who’s just engaged doesn’t spend his time visiting married women. But then again, maybe in the world she’s come from, that’s how things are done—maybe that’s all they do.*

And despite the cosmopolitan views he prided himself on, he felt a sudden gratitude—to be a New Yorker, to be marrying into his own world, to belong to a society where rules were clear, and honor was measured in silence.

V. The next evening, Mr. Sillerton Jackson came to dine with the Archers. Mrs. Archer was a quiet woman, reserved by nature and somewhat withdrawn from the bustling world of society, yet she had a deep curiosity about its doings. Her old friend, Mr. Jackson, approached the affairs of others with the patience of a scholar and the precision of a naturalist, while his sister, Miss Sophy Jackson, who lived with him and was highly sought after for her company, brought home fragments of gossip that filled in the gaps of his social map. So whenever something stirred interest in Mrs. Archer’s mind—especially if it concerned the upper echelons of New York life—she would invite Mr. Jackson to dinner. Since her invitations were rare and her household charming, he usually accepted personally rather than sending his sister.

If he could have chosen, he might have preferred evenings when Newland was away—not because he disliked the young man (they were quite congenial at their club), but because Newland occasionally displayed an analytical reserve that made conversation less spontaneous. Still, Mr. Jackson, ever the diplomat, would often say to his sister, “I’ve been a little gouty since my last dinner at the Lovell Mingotts’—a visit to Adeline’s will do me good.” And indeed, it did. While the Mingotts dazzled with canvas-back and vintage wines, the Archers offered refined conversation and intellectual companionship, with the added comfort of Madeira that had journeyed around the Cape.

Mrs. Archer, a widow long settled in West Twenty-eighth Street, lived with her son Newland and daughter Janey. An entire floor was set aside for Newland, while the two women shared modest quarters below. Together, they cultivated ferns in glass cases, stitched intricate lace and wool embroidery, collected Revolutionary-era pottery, and read novels by Ouida—particularly those set among Italian peasants, which they found both romantic and morally satisfying. They avoided Dickens, whom they felt failed to portray gentlemen properly, and though they admired Thackeray, they believed Bulwer-Lytton better captured high society—even if he was now considered outdated.

Both mother and daughter loved scenery above all else. On their travels abroad, they sought out landscapes rather than art or architecture, which they regarded as men’s pursuits, especially those of learned men who read Ruskin. Mrs. Archer had been born a Newland, and both she and Janey bore the unmistakable traits of the family: tall, pale, slightly stooped shoulders, long noses, gentle smiles, and a certain melancholy elegance reminiscent of faded Reynolds portraits. Their resemblance was nearly complete—except that age had softened Mrs. Archer’s figure, while Janey’s slender frame remained untouched by time.

Though outwardly alike, their inner lives differed. Years of close companionship had given them identical speech patterns, habits of saying “Mother thinks” or “Janey thinks,” depending on who wished to voice an opinion. But beneath this surface harmony, Janey harbored dreams and impulses that flickered like hidden flames—romantic notions suppressed by decorum, yet never fully extinguished. Mother and daughter adored each other deeply and revered Newland, their only child and brother. In turn, Newland loved them tenderly, though his affection was tinged with guilt over their idealized view of him—and secretly pleased by it. After all, wasn’t it comforting to be respected at home, even if one sometimes doubted the weight of one’s own authority?

That evening, Newland knew Mr. Jackson would have preferred him absent. But he had decided to stay. He understood what was coming: talk of Ellen Olenska. And he knew his mother and sister would want to hear every detail. All three would be subtly uneasy with his presence—now that his engagement to May Welland had become public knowledge. Yet he waited, amused and curious, to see how they would navigate the tension.

They began cautiously, with a mention of Mrs. Lemuel Struthers. “It’s a pity the Beauforts invited her,” Mrs. Archer said gently. “But then Regina always does what he tells her. And _Beaufort_—”
“Certain _nuances_ escape Beaufort,” Mr. Jackson replied, carefully examining the broiled shad, wondering once more why Mrs. Archer’s cook always burned the roe to a crisp. (Newland, who had long shared the same observation, saw the familiar look of quiet dismay cross the older man’s face.)

“Oh, necessarily,” Mrs. Archer agreed. “Beaufort is vulgar. My grandfather Newland used to warn my mother: ‘Whatever you do, don’t let that fellow Beaufort near the girls.’ But at least he’s had the advantage of mixing with gentlemen—over in England, they say. It’s all very mysterious—” She glanced at Janey and paused. Though they both knew the full story, Mrs. Archer still maintained propriety in public.

“But Mrs. Struthers,” she continued, “what did you say _she_ was, Sillerton?”

“Out of a mine—or rather, from the saloon at the top of the pit,” Mr. Jackson replied. “Then with Living Wax-Works, touring New England. After the police shut that down, they say she lived—” He looked toward Janey, whose eyes widened slightly. There were still blanks in Mrs. Struthers’s past that intrigued her.

“Then,” Mr. Jackson went on, “Lemuel Struthers came along. His advertiser used her head for shoe-polish posters—her hair’s intensely black, you know, Egyptian style. Eventually—” He lingered on the word, giving it careful emphasis. “Eventually, he married her.”

There was a wealth of implication in that single syllable.

“Oh well,” Mrs. Archer said lightly. “At the pass we’ve come to nowadays, it doesn’t matter.”

The truth was, they weren’t really interested in Mrs. Struthers. Ellen Olenska was too fresh, too compelling. Mrs. Archer had mentioned her only so she could soon ask: “And Newland’s new cousin—the Countess Olenska? Was _she_ at the ball too?”

There was a hint of irony in her tone, directed at her son, and Newland recognized it. He’d expected it. Even Mrs. Archer, usually composed and unimpressed by worldly events, had been genuinely pleased by his engagement. (“Especially after that silly business with Mrs. Rushworth,” she’d whispered to Janey, referring to a past heartbreak Newland had once thought unbearable.) No match in New York was finer than May Welland—regardless of how one viewed it. Of course, such a marriage was what Newland deserved. But young men were unpredictable, and some women dangerously persuasive. To see her only son safely beyond temptation and into respectable domesticity was nothing short of miraculous.

Yet Mrs. Archer had been unsettled by the timing of the announcement—by the fact that it coincided with Ellen Olenska’s return. And so, feeling a sense of duty and tenderness, Newland had stayed home that night.

“It’s not that I disapprove of the Mingotts’ pride,” Mrs. Archer murmured to Janey, her only confidante in moments of mild rebellion. “But why must Newland’s engagement be tied to that Olenska woman’s comings and goings?”

She had behaved impeccably during the visit to Mrs. Welland—but Newland knew, and May likely guessed, that both women had been on edge, watching for any sign of Madame Olenska’s appearance. When they left, Mrs. Archer had whispered, “I’m thankful Augusta received us alone.”

These small betrayals of anxiety touched Newland deeply, for he too sensed the Mingotts had crossed a line. But neither mother nor son could speak openly of what troubled them. Instead, Newland simply said, “Oh, well—there’s always a phase of family gatherings when one gets engaged. The sooner it’s over, the better.”

His mother merely pursed her lips beneath her lace veil, trimmed with frosted grapes. Her revenge, he knew, would be to draw Mr. Jackson into a full discussion of the Countess Olenska. And since he had already fulfilled his role as a future Mingott, Newland had no objection to hearing about her—except that the subject was beginning to weary him.

Mr. Jackson had taken a slice of the lukewarm filet mignon, handed to him by the solemn butler with a look of silent judgment. He sniffed at the mushroom sauce and rejected it. He looked hungry and baffled, and Newland suspected he would finish his meal with stories of Ellen Olenska.

Leaning back, Mr. Jackson studied the candlelit portraits lining the dark walls—Archers, Newlands, van der Luydens. “Ah, how your grandfather Archer loved a good dinner, my dear Newland!” he said, gazing at a portrait of a robust young man in a blue coat, standing before a stately country house. “Well… well… well… I wonder what he would have said to all these foreign marriages!”

Mrs. Archer ignored the reference to cuisine. Mr. Jackson continued slowly: “No, she was _not_ at the ball.”

“Ah—” Mrs. Archer murmured, relieved. “She had that decency.”

“Perhaps the Beauforts don’t know her,” Janey suggested, her eyes twinkling with innocent malice.

Mr. Jackson took a sip as if sipping invisible Madeira. “Mrs. Beaufort may not—but Beaufort certainly does. She was seen walking up Fifth Avenue this afternoon with him by the whole of New York.”

“Mercy—” Mrs. Archer breathed, realizing the futility of expecting foreigners to follow local codes of conduct.

“I wonder if she wears a round hat or a bonnet in the afternoon,” Janey mused. “At the Opera, I saw her in dark blue velvet—perfectly plain and flat—like a nightgown.”

“Janey!” her mother scolded, and the young woman blushed, trying to appear bold.

“At least,” Mrs. Archer said, “it was in better taste not to attend the ball.”

A spark of defiance lit in Newland’s eyes. “I don’t think it was a matter of taste,” he replied. “May said she meant to go, but changed her mind because the dress wasn’t smart enough.”

Mrs. Archer smiled, satisfied. “Poor Ellen,” she said softly. “We must remember what an eccentric upbringing Medora Manson gave her. What can you expect of a girl who wore black satin at her debut ball?”

“Oh, I remember her in it!” Mr. Jackson exclaimed, then added, “Poor girl!”—a phrase that carried both fondness and foreboding.

“It’s odd,” Janey remarked, “that she kept such an ugly name as Ellen. I’d have changed it to Elaine.”

She glanced around the table, awaiting reactions. Newland laughed.

“Why Elaine?”

“I don’t know—it sounds more… Polish,” she said, blushing.

“It sounds more conspicuous,” Mrs. Archer said coolly. “And that can hardly be what she wants.”

“Why not?” Newland shot back, suddenly animated. “Why shouldn’t she stand out if she chooses? Why should she hide as if she were the one who disgraced herself? She’s poor Ellen, yes—because she married badly. But that doesn’t make her an outcast.”

“That,” Mr. Jackson mused, “is probably the line the Mingotts intend to take.”

Newland flushed. “I didn’t need their cue, if that’s what you mean. Madame Olenska has suffered. That doesn’t justify condemning her.”

“There are rumors,” Mr. Jackson began, glancing at Janey.

“Oh, I know—the secretary,” Newland interrupted. “Nonsense, mother—Janey’s grown-up. They say the secretary helped her escape from her brute of a husband, who held her prisoner? Well, what if he did? I hope there isn’t a man here who wouldn’t have done the same.”

Mr. Jackson turned to the butler. “Perhaps… that sauce… just a little, after all—” He took a portion and said, “I’m told she’s looking for a house. She means to live here.”

“I hear she intends to get a divorce,” Janey said boldly.

“I hope she will!” Newland declared.

The words fell like a thunderclap in the quiet room. Mrs. Archer raised her eyebrows—the subtle signal: “The butler—” And Newland, remembering the impropriety of discussing such matters aloud, quickly shifted to recounting his visit to old Mrs. Mingott.

After dinner, as tradition dictated, Mrs. Archer and Janey ascended to the drawing-room, where they sat beside a Carcel lamp, facing each other across a rosewood work-table, stitching a tapestry band of field-flowers meant for the chair of the future Mrs. Newland Archer.

Below, Newland settled Mr. Jackson into an armchair near the fire in the Gothic library and handed him a cigar. Mr. Jackson sank into the chair with contentment, lit his cigar with confidence (it was Newland who bought them), stretched his thin ankles toward the coals, and said: “You say the secretary merely helped her escape? Well, he was still helping her a year later—for someone met them living together in Lausanne.”

Newland reddened. “Living together? Why not? Who had the right to reshape her life if not she? I’m tired of the hypocrisy that would bury a woman alive just because her husband prefers harlots.”

He stopped, turned away to light his own cigar, and declared, “Women ought to be free—as free as we are.”

He spoke without thinking, consumed by anger and conviction. But he hadn’t realized how profound those words truly were.

Mr. Jackson drew his ankles closer to the fire and emitted a low, sardonic whistle. “Well,” he said after a pause, “apparently Count Olenski takes your view. I’ve never heard of him lifting a finger to bring her back.”

VI. That evening, after Mr. Jackson had departed and the ladies had retired to their softly curtained bedroom, Newland Archer climbed quietly to his study. As always, a careful hand had kept the fire glowing and the lamp trimmed, casting a warm light over the room—its shelves lined with books, its mantelpiece adorned with bronze and steel figures of *The Fencers*, and its surfaces dotted with photographs of famous paintings. It was a place of comfort and quiet dignity, a sanctuary where thought could unfold without interruption.

He sank into his armchair beside the hearth, and his gaze fell upon a large photograph of May Welland, placed prominently on the table. She had given it to him in the early days of their courtship, and now it stood alone among the other portraits, as if claiming exclusive possession of his attention. He looked at her—the clear forehead, the thoughtful eyes, the bright, unguarded smile—and felt a new kind of reverence. This young woman, so full of life yet untouched by experience, was the one whose soul he would soon be entrusted to guard. And yet, looking at her face, he saw not just May, but a symbol: the product of a world that shaped women to know nothing, expect everything, and never question the rules.

It struck him again—marriage wasn’t the safe harbor he’d once believed it to be. It was more like setting sail into unknown waters. The recent stir around Countess Olenska had shaken long-held beliefs, stirring doubts he hadn’t known he carried. His own words—"Women should be free, as free as we are"—now echoed in his mind, not as a bold declaration, but as a challenge to the very fabric of the society he lived in. In his circle, such sentiments were permitted only in conversation, never in practice. "Nice" women, even when wronged, would never demand the freedom he meant. And men like himself, who spoke generously, did so not out of conviction but because it flattered their sense of chivalry. These words were mere gestures, masks for the rigid conventions that bound everyone to tradition.

But here he was, pledged to defend the actions of May’s cousin—actions that, if committed by his own wife, would bring down every social and moral condemnation he knew. Of course, this was hypothetical; he wasn’t a Polish nobleman, nor would he ever be. Yet his imagination refused to let go. What if, in his own marriage, the bond grew strained—not from scandal, but from something quieter, subtler? How much could he truly know of May, when it was his duty to hide parts of himself, and hers to have nothing to hide? What if they grew tired of each other, misunderstood, or simply drifted apart?

He recalled the marriages of his friends—those deemed happy—and found none that matched the deep, tender companionship he dreamed of sharing with May. That dream, he realized, required qualities she had been deliberately denied: independence, depth of experience, the freedom to think and feel without restraint. And with a pang of dread, he saw his future marriage taking shape—not as a union of souls, but as a partnership of appearances, sustained by ignorance and pretense.

Larry Lefferts came to mind. He was the epitome of such a marriage—his wife molded perfectly to his needs, blissfully unaware of his affairs, even laughing at the idea that Julius Beaufort might have “another establishment.” She blushed at the mention of it, as if scandal were a foreign concept. Archer tried to reassure himself that he wasn’t quite so shallow, nor May so naive. But the difference was only one of intelligence, not principle. They all lived in a world of symbols, where truth was hidden behind polite lies. Mrs. Welland, though she knew exactly why Archer had pushed for the engagement announcement at the Beaufort ball, still pretended reluctance—just as primitive brides were dragged screaming from their homes in the stories people read.

And so, the girl at the heart of this elaborate charade remained mysterious, not because she was guarded, but because she was utterly sincere. She was honest because she had nothing to hide, confident because she had no reason to fear. And yet, with this innocence, she was about to step into what was called “the facts of life”—a world she was ill-prepared to face.

Archer loved her deeply, but calmly. He admired her beauty, her vitality, her skill at riding and games, and the gentle curiosity she showed toward books and ideas under his influence. (She had joined him in mocking Tennyson’s *Idyls of the King*, but hadn’t yet grasped the magic of *Ulysses and the Lotus Eaters*.) She was loyal, brave, and possessed a quiet humor—mostly demonstrated by laughing at his jokes. And beneath her calm gaze, he sensed a warmth of feeling, waiting to be awakened.

Yet, despite all this, he felt a growing unease. Was this purity real—or merely manufactured? He believed that untrained human nature wasn’t naturally innocent or frank; it was instinctively guarded, twisted by self-protection. And now, he was surrounded by a creation of artificial virtue—crafted by generations of mothers, aunts, and grandmothers who had shaped girls into perfect wives, not because they were naturally pure, but because society demanded it.

He felt oppressed by it. This ideal of innocence wasn’t freedom—it was performance. And he feared it was designed not to protect, but to allow men like himself to enjoy the thrill of breaking it, like shattering a snow sculpture.

These thoughts were familiar—common enough among young men on the brink of marriage. But usually they came with guilt, with regret. Archer felt none of that. He couldn’t mourn the fact that he didn’t offer his bride a blank page in exchange for hers. If he had been raised as she was, he wouldn’t have been any better equipped to navigate life than a child lost in the woods. And no matter how hard he thought, he couldn *t* find an honest reason—beyond vanity or fleeting desire—why May shouldn’t have the same freedom he claimed for himself.

These questions lingered, sharpened by the arrival of Ellen Olenska. At the moment of his betrothal—a time meant for joy and clarity—he was thrust into a scandal that forced him to confront truths he’d hoped to avoid.

“Damn Ellen Olenska,” he muttered, covering the fire and beginning to undress. He couldn’t see how her fate affected him. Yet deep down, he sensed that he had only just begun to grasp the weight of the role he had accepted.

A few days later, the storm broke. The Lovell Mingotts sent out invitations for a formal dinner—three extra footmen, two dishes per course, Roman punch at the center—and added, “To meet the Countess Olenska,” following the American custom of treating strangers like royalty.

The guest list was carefully chosen, a blend of old families and fashionable young couples—Selfridge Merrys, Beauforts, Sillerton Jackson and his sister Sophy, Lawrence Lefferts, Mrs. Lefferts Rushworth, Harry Thorley, Reggie Chivers, and Morris Dagonet and his wife, a van der Luyden. It was a perfect mix—everyone who moved in the tight-knit inner circle of high society.

But forty-eight hours later, the unthinkable happened: every invitation was declined—except those of the Beauforts and old Mr. Jackson with his sister. Even the Chiverses, part of the Mingott family, refused. And every refusal was worded identically: “Regretted unable to accept,” with no mention of prior engagements. In a society where everyone knew everyone else’s schedule, this was a deliberate, cutting snub.

The Mingotts took it with grace. Mrs. Lovell Mingott confided in Mrs. Welland, who passed it on to Archer. He reacted with outrage, appealing passionately to his mother. After a period of hesitation, she yielded—as she always did—and immediately declared, “I’ll go and see Louisa van der Luyden.”

New York in Archer’s time was a small, slippery pyramid. At its base were the “plain people”—respectable families elevated by marriage to the elite, like the Spicers, Leffertses, and Jacksons. Mrs. Archer often said, “People aren’t as particular as they used to be.” With Catherine Spicer ruling one end of Fifth Avenue and Julius Beaufort the other, the old traditions were fading fast.

Above them was the dominant group—the Mingotts, Newlands, Chiverses, Mansons—who seemed to be at the top. But Mrs. Archer knew better. To the genealogists, only a handful of families truly held aristocratic status: the Dagonets of Washington Square, descendants of English nobility linked to Pitt and Fox; the Lannings, connected to Count de Grasse; and the van der Luydens, direct heirs of Manhattan’s first Dutch governor, tied by marriage to French and British aristocracy.

The Lannings were represented only by two elderly sisters, living among ancestral portraits. The Dagonets were numerous, allied with the best names in Baltimore and Philadelphia. But the van der Luydens stood above all—nearly mythical, existing in a twilight realm from which only two figures emerged: Mr. and Mrs. Henry van der Luyden.

Mrs. van der Luyden had been Louisa Dagonet, granddaughter of Colonel du Lac, a Channel Islander who fought under Cornwallis and settled in Maryland with Lady Angelica Trevenna, daughter of the Earl of St. Austrey. The ties between these families remained strong. The van der Luydens had visited the Duke of St. Austrey in Cornwall and Gloucestershire, and he had promised to return their visit—without the Duchess, who feared the Atlantic.

They divided their time between Trevenna in Maryland, their estate in Skuytercliff on the Hudson—once a colonial grant from the Dutch government—and their rare, solemn house on Madison Avenue, opened only for intimate friends.

“I wish you’d come with me, Newland,” his mother said, pausing at the door of the Brown coupe. “Louisa likes you. And of course, it’s for dear May I’m doing this—and because, if we don’t stand together, there won’t be any Society left.”

VII. Mrs. Henry van der Luyden sat perfectly still, her pale blue eyes fixed on her cousin Mrs. Archer as she spoke. It was one of those moments when silence carried more weight than words—when the air in the room seemed to hold its breath. The Madison Avenue drawing-room, with its high ceilings and white walls, felt like a place untouched by time. The brocade armchairs had been freshly uncovered for the occasion, and delicate gauze still draped the ornate mantel ornaments and the carved frame of Gainsborough’s “Lady Angelica du Lac.” Opposite the painting hung Mrs. van der Luyden’s own portrait by Huntington—her face serene in black velvet and Venetian lace, a likeness so exact it could have passed for a twin sister to the woman now listening in quiet dignity.

She wore the same black velvet and lace today, though she rarely left her home except to receive guests. Her fair hair, unchanged by age, was parted in soft, overlapping waves across her forehead. Her straight nose, sharp yet gentle, framed eyes that were pale and thoughtful. She looked preserved—like a figure from another century, carefully kept in a world untouched by change. Newland Archer had always admired her grace, but he found her presence strangely distant, almost ethereal. She wasn’t harsh or cold, but her kindness felt reserved, as if held back behind a glass door. Unlike his mother’s stern, no-nonsense aunts, who declared their opinions without hesitation, Mrs. van der Luyden never said yes or no outright. Instead, her lips would curve into a faint, hesitant smile, and she’d murmur: “I shall first have to talk this over with my husband.”

It was a phrase repeated so often it had become ritual. Yet today, something shifted. As Mrs. Archer concluded her account of the incident involving Countess Olenska, Mrs. van der Luyden reached out—not toward her tea cup, nor her fan—but to the bell-rope beside her chair.

“I think,” she said quietly, “I should like Henry to hear what you’ve told me.”

A footman appeared instantly. “If Mr. van der Luyden has finished reading the newspaper,” she added, “please ask him to be kind enough to come.”

She said it with such calm authority that it sounded less like a request and more like a summons. Not because she was arrogant, but because years of tradition had made her husband’s actions sacred in the eyes of the world—and even in her own. He was not just her partner; he was part of a legacy, a pillar of society whose decisions carried weight beyond mere personal opinion.

Mrs. Archer and Newland exchanged glances. This was unusual. But before they could react, the double doors opened, and Mr. Henry van der Luyden stepped through—tall, slender, dressed in a dark frock coat, his hair faded silver, his features mirroring his wife’s in every detail. His eyes, pale grey instead of blue, held the same quiet intensity, the same sense of measured restraint.

He greeted Mrs. Archer with polite warmth, offered Newland low-voiced congratulations—words as carefully chosen as a diplomat’s—then settled into one of the brocade chairs with the ease of a man accustomed to being obeyed.

“I had just finished reading the Times,” he said, folding his long fingers together. “In town, my mornings are so full I find it more convenient to read after luncheon.”

“Ah, there’s much to be said for that,” Mrs. Archer replied. “Uncle Egmont used to say it was less agitating to wait until dinner.”

“Yes,” Mr. van der Luyden agreed slowly. “My father abhorred hurry. But now we live in constant rush.”

His wife interjected gently, “But I hope you *had* finished your reading, Henry?”

“Quite—quite,” he reassured her.

Then she turned to Mrs. Archer. “I should like Adeline to tell you—”

“Oh, it’s really Newland’s story,” his mother smiled, and began again the tale of the affront to Mrs. Lovell Mingott—how the Leffertses had refused to attend her dinner, how the Countess Olenska had been excluded, and how the whole affair threatened to shake the delicate balance of New York society.

“And of course,” Mrs. Archer concluded, “Augusta Welland and Mary Mingott both felt that, especially given Newland’s engagement, you two ought to know.”

Mr. van der Luyden exhaled slowly. A long silence followed. The clock on the mantelpiece ticked with a sound like a drumbeat. Archer watched them—their stillness, their shared understanding. They were not merely a couple; they were an institution. Two figures standing at the threshold between tradition and change, bound by duty and love, yet longing for peace in the quiet of Skuytercliff, where they might play Patience beneath the oaks and tend their gardens in silence.

At last, Mr. van der Luyden spoke. “You really believe this is due to Lawrence Lefferts?” he asked, turning to Newland.

“I’m certain of it, sir,” Archer said. “Larry’s been acting rather rashly lately—having some sort of trouble with the postmaster’s wife in his village. Whenever Gertrude suspects anything, he starts making noise about morality, about people not knowing who belongs where. He’s using Madame Olenska as a lightning rod—just like he’s done before.”

“The *Leffertses*!” Mrs. van der Luyden murmured.

“The *Leffertses*!” echoed Mrs. Archer.

“What would Uncle Egmont have said if Lawrence Lefferts dared pronounce on someone’s social standing? It shows how far Society has fallen.”

“We’ll hope it hasn’t quite come to that,” Mr. van der Luyden said firmly.

“Oh, if only you and Louisa went out more!” Mrs. Archer sighed.

She immediately regretted it. The van der Luydens were deeply sensitive to any suggestion that their seclusion was a flaw. They were the final arbiters of taste, the ultimate court of appeal. Everyone knew it. And yet they lived in near isolation, retreating to Skuytercliff whenever possible, declining invitations under the pretense of Mrs. van der Luyden’s health.

Newland stepped in. “Everyone knows what you and cousin Louisa represent. That’s why Mrs. Mingott felt she couldn’t let this pass without consulting you.”

The two glanced at each other—pale eyes meeting in silent communion.

“It is the principle I dislike,” Mr. van der Luyden said. “As long as a member of a respected family is supported by that family, it should be considered… final.”

“That seems right to me,” Mrs. van der Luyden said softly, as if revealing a truth long hidden.

“I had no idea,” Mr. van der Luyden continued, “that things had come to such a pass.” He paused, then added, “It occurs to me, my dear, that the Countess Olenska is already somewhat related—through Medora Manson’s first husband. At any rate, she will be when Newland marries.”

He turned to the young man. “Have you read this morning’s Times, Newland?”

“Yes, sir,” Archer replied, thinking of the papers he usually devoured with coffee.

The couple exchanged another look—long, deliberate, meaningful. Then a soft smile touched Mrs. van der Luyden’s lips. She understood. She approved.

Mr. van der Luyden turned to Mrs. Archer. “If Louisa’s health allowed her to dine out—I wish you would tell Mrs. Lovell Mingott—that she and I would have been happy to… er… fill the places of the Lawrence Leffertses at her dinner.”

He paused, letting the irony hang in the air. “As you know, this is impossible.”

Mrs. Archer nodded sympathetically.

“But Newland tells me he’s read the Times,” Mr. van der Luyden continued. “So he may have seen that Louisa’s cousin, the Duke of St. Austrey, arrives next week on the Russia. He’s coming to enter his sloop, the Guinevere, in the International Cup Race—and to do a little canvasback shooting at Trevenna.”

He paused again, then spoke with growing warmth. “Before taking him down to Maryland, we’re inviting a few friends to meet him here—just a small dinner, with a reception afterward. I’m sure Louisa will be delighted if Countess Olenska joins us among our guests.”

He rose, bowed stiffly but kindly to his cousin, and added, “I think I have Louisa’s authority to say she will personally deliver the invitation when she drives out shortly—with our cards, of course—with our cards.”

Mrs. Archer rose quickly, murmuring thanks.

Mrs. van der Luyden smiled warmly—like Esther pleading with Ahasuerus. But her husband raised a hand. “There is nothing to thank me for, dear Adeline. Nothing at all. This kind of thing must not happen in New York. It shall not—so long as I can help it.”

And with that, he guided his cousins to the door.

Two hours later, the news spread like wildfire. The great C-spring barouche—always seen gliding through the streets—had been spotted at old Mrs. Mingott’s house. A large square envelope had been delivered. That evening, at the Opera, Mr. Sillerton Jackson announced with satisfaction that it contained an invitation to the Countess Olenska for the van der Luydens’ dinner the following week—honoring the Duke of St. Austrey.

In the club box, a few younger men exchanged smiles. One glanced sideways at Lawrence Lefferts, who sat casually in the front row, twirling his moustache. As the soprano paused mid-aria, he declared with absolute certainty: “No one but Patti ought to attempt the Sonnambula.”

VIII. In New York, it was widely believed that the Countess Olenska had lost her beauty. Yet when Newland Archer saw her enter the van der Luyden drawing-room on that significant evening, he found himself quietly rejecting that verdict.

She had first come to his attention as a child—bright-eyed and lively, barely ten years old, with cheeks like ripe apples and curls so dark they seemed to shimmer in the light. People said she should be painted, not just because of her looks, but because of the energy that danced in her gaze. Her parents had been restless souls, drifting across Europe, and when both passed away, she was taken in by her aunt, Medora Manson—a woman equally prone to wanderings, though more than a little eccentric. Medora, who’d married and buried several husbands, always returned to New York claiming she would “settle down,” only to leave again after a few months, usually having sold her house at a loss. Though her lineage traced back to the Rushworths and her last marriage tied her to the reclusive Chiverses, New York forgave her quirks. But when she arrived with young Ellen Mingott—her orphaned niece—the city hesitated. The child’s bright clothes and bold spirit seemed out of place for someone still mourning her parents. Medora, ever defiant, ignored mourning customs: her veil was shorter than those of her sisters-in-law, and Ellen wore crimson merino and amber beads like a street performer from the south of France.

Still, Ellen charmed everyone. She was fearless, curious, and delightfully unguarded. She asked questions no one expected from a child, sang Neapolitan songs with a guitar, and danced with a Spanish shawl as if born to it. Under Medora’s direction—whose real name was Mrs. Thorley Chivers, but who now called herself the Marchioness Manson (a title she’d adopted after a Papal honor)—Ellen received an education far ahead of its time: drawing from live models, playing piano in quintets with professionals. It was all considered improper, and soon enough, tragedy struck. Chivers died in an asylum, and Medora fled once more, taking Ellen with her. For years, nothing was heard.

Then came word: Ellen had married a wealthy Polish nobleman—legendary in European circles—who owned estates in Paris, Florence, and Nice, a yacht in Cowes, and vast hunting grounds in Transylvania. She vanished into a world of grandeur and mystery. When Medora returned to New York again, poorer and subdued, people wondered why her rich niece hadn’t helped her. Then came the news: Ellen’s marriage had failed. She was coming home—not to reclaim glory, but to find peace among her kin.

That evening, as Newland watched her arrive at the van der Luydens’ dinner, he felt a quiet stir within him. She entered late, one hand bare, adjusting a bracelet. Yet she moved without haste or tension, stepping into the room where New York’s most refined society stood poised like figures in a portrait. She paused in the center, eyes scanning the crowd with calm grace. Her face was no longer radiant—her cheeks were pale, her frame thin, her features sharper than before. She looked older than her nearly thirty years. But something else lingered: a quiet power, a stillness in her presence that spoke of depth and experience.

Her manner was simpler than the others’. She didn’t wear jewels with ostentation, nor did she speak with the polished artifice of the city’s elite. And yet, there was a strength in her silence, a dignity that made her stand out even amid such formality. New York had expected flamboyance—something dramatic, perhaps scandalous—from a woman with such a past. Instead, she offered restraint, thoughtfulness, and a kind of weary honesty.

The dinner itself was a solemn affair. To dine with the van der Luydens was already an event; to do so with their cousin, the Duke of St. Austrey, elevated it to near-religious significance. Only a true New Yorker could appreciate the subtle distinction between being merely a duke and being *their* duke. Noblemen were tolerated in New York, sometimes even met with polite skepticism—but this one carried a weight of tradition that earned him genuine warmth, not just social obligation.

The table sparkled with heirlooms: Sevres porcelain, George II silver, Lowestoft china from the East India Company, Crown Derby from Dagonet. Mrs. van der Luyden glowed like a painting by Cabanel; Mrs. Archer, in her grandmother’s pearls and emeralds, looked like a miniature by Isabey. Jewels glittered, but mostly in heavy, old-fashioned settings—old Miss Lanning even wore her mother’s cameos and a Spanish blonde shawl.

And yet, standing among them, Ellen Olenska seemed the most mature. As Archer glanced around at the plump, smooth-faced ladies adorned with diamonds and feathers, he felt a pang—what must have shaped those eyes? What storms had passed behind that quiet gaze?

The Duke sat beside the hostess, a figure of modest stature, sunburnt, with a thick beard and small eyes. He wore clothes that looked too large, too worn, as if borrowed from another man. He rarely spoke, and when he did, his voice was so low it was lost beneath the hushed anticipation of the room.

After dinner, the men joined the women. The Duke immediately approached the Countess and settled into conversation with her—ignoring protocol entirely. No one remarked upon it. They simply watched, intrigued.

For nearly twenty minutes, they talked. Then, without warning, the Countess rose and walked across the room—alone—to sit beside Newland Archer.

It was unusual. In New York, a lady did not rise from one gentleman to seek another. She remained seated, a statue of decorum, while men took turns at her side. But Ellen didn’t seem aware of any rule being broken. She sat easily, comfortably, and looked at him with gentle eyes.

“I want you to talk to me about May,” she said.

Instead of answering, he asked, “You knew the Duke before?”

“Oh, yes—we used to see him every winter at Nice. He loves gambling—he often came to our house.”

She said it plainly, as if discussing a favorite flower. Then, after a pause, she added honestly, “I think he’s the dullest man I’ve ever met.”

Archer laughed—surprised, delighted. It was thrilling to meet someone who could say such a thing about the van der Luydens’ Duke and mean it.

He wanted to ask more—about her life, her past, the world she’d left behind—but hesitated. Then she returned to her original subject.

“May is a darling. I’ve seen no young girl in New York so beautiful and intelligent. Are you very much in love with her?”

Archer flushed. “As much as a man can be.”

She studied him carefully. “Do you think there’s a limit?”

“To being in love? If there is, I haven’t found it.”

She smiled, touched by his sincerity. “Ah—so it’s truly a romance?”

“The most romantic of romances.”

“How delightful! And you discovered it all yourselves? Nothing was arranged?”

He looked at her, startled. “Have you forgotten,” he said gently, “that here we don’t arrange marriages?”

A faint blush crept up her cheek. “Yes—I forgot. Please forgive me. Sometimes I forget that what was wrong where I came from is now right here.”

She looked down at her fan—feathers of eagle, delicate and exotic—and her lips trembled slightly.

“I’m sorry,” he murmured, “but you *are* among friends here.”

“Yes,” she said softly. “I know. That’s why I came back. I want to forget everything else. To become a complete American again—like the Mingotts, the Wellands, you, your mother… all of you tonight.” She paused. “Ah—here comes May. You’ll want to go to her.”

But she didn’t move. Her eyes stayed fixed on his.

The drawing-room filled with guests. Through her glance, Archer saw May Welland entering with her mother—dressed in white and silver, a wreath of silver blossoms in her hair. She looked like a goddess returning from the hunt.

“Oh,” Archer said, “I have so many rivals. She’s already surrounded. Look—there’s the Duke being introduced.”

“Then stay with me a little longer,” she whispered, brushing his knee lightly with her fan.

It was a feather-light touch, but it sent a jolt through him—like a secret shared.

“Yes,” he replied, almost without thinking, “let me stay.”

Just then, Mr. van der Luyden approached, followed by old Mr. Urban Dagonet. The Countess greeted them with her quiet smile, and Archer, sensing his host’s disapproving glance, rose and stepped aside.

She held out her hand, as if bidding farewell. “Tomorrow, then—after five. I shall expect you.”

And then she turned, making room for Mr. Dagonet.

“Tomorrow…” Archer repeated under his breath, though no promise had been made, no invitation extended. Still, the words hung in the air.

As he walked away, he saw Lawrence Lefferts, tall and dazzling, leading his wife forward. Gertrude Lefferts beamed at the Countess. “But I think we used to go to dancing school together when we were children…”

Behind them, Archer noticed several couples who had refused to attend Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s earlier gathering. Now, they stood in line, eager to introduce themselves. As Mrs. Archer had said: when the van der Luydens chose to teach a lesson, they knew how to do it. The wonder was that they so rarely did.

A hand touched his arm. Mrs. van der Luyden looked down at him from her height of black velvet and family diamonds. “It was kind of you, dear Newland, to devote yourself so unselfishly to Madame Olenska. I told your cousin Henry he must come to the rescue.”

He smiled vaguely. She added, as if understanding his shyness: “I’ve never seen May looking lovelier. The Duke thinks her the handsomest girl in the room.”

And so, in that moment, Newland stood caught between two worlds—one of duty and expectation, the other of quiet longing and unexpected connection.

IX. The Countess Olenska had said “after five”; and at half past the hour, Newland Archer rang the bell of a modest house on West Twenty-third Street—its stucco peeling, its cast-iron balcony strangled by a wild wisteria vine.
