It was an odd place to settle in, far removed from the polished avenues of society. Small dressmakers, taxidermists, and writers—people who lived quietly, almost invisibly—were her neighbors. Down the untidy street, Archer recognized a weathered wooden cottage where Winsett, a journalist he sometimes met, once claimed to live. Winsett never invited guests, but once, during a late-night walk, he’d pointed it out with pride. Archer had shivered then, wondering if this was how the intellectual world lived elsewhere. Madame Olenska’s home was no grander than the rest—only slightly better kept, with a bit more paint around the windows. As he stood before it, he couldn’t help thinking that the Polish count must have stripped her not just of fortune, but of illusion as well.

The day had been heavy. He’d lunched with the Wellands, hoping afterward to take May for a quiet stroll in the park. He wanted to be alone with her—to tell her how radiant she’d looked the night before, how proud he was of her, and how he wished they could marry sooner. But Mrs. Welland had reminded him firmly that family visits were only halfway done. When he suggested moving up the wedding date, she’d raised her eyebrows and sighed: “Twelve dozen of everything—hand-embroidered—” They’d rolled through one household after another in the family landau, like exhibits in a polite parade. By the time they parted, Archer felt like a specimen on display—caged, admired, and passed along. He supposed his anthropology readings made him see things too plainly, but when he thought about the wedding being delayed until autumn, a cold weight settled in his chest. “Tomorrow,” Mrs. Welland called after him, “we’ll do the Chiverses and the Dallases.” He realized then that their social rounds were being followed alphabetically—and they were barely through the first quarter.

He’d meant to tell May about the Countess’s request—that she’d actually *commanded* him to call. But in their brief moments together, other things had mattered more. Besides, it seemed absurd to bring it up. May had always wanted him to be kind to her cousin; hadn’t that very wish helped hasten their engagement? It struck him now that, without Ellen Olenska’s arrival, he might still have been free—or at least less bound. But May had chosen this path, and somehow, that freed him from further obligation. So here he was, standing at her door, unannounced, uninvited into her world.

Curiosity gripped him. The way she’d summoned him—so casually, so directly—suggested a mind sharper than her gentle demeanor implied. The door opened to a dark-haired maid, thick-boned and foreign-looking, wearing a bright scarf over her neck. She smiled broadly, showing white teeth, and gestured toward the inside with a shrug. Her eyes didn’t meet his, nor did she understand his questions. She led him through a narrow hall into a low-ceilinged drawing room lit only by a fire. The room was empty. She left him there, and for a moment he wondered if she’d gone to fetch the Countess—or if she thought he’d come to wind the clock, which sat stopped on the mantel.

He remembered reading that southern people communicated in silence, in gesture and expression. But her movements—her nods, her smiles—meant nothing to him. He felt foolish. Then she returned with a lamp. He managed to cobble together a phrase from Dante and Petrarch, and she responded: “La signora e fuori; ma verra subito.” He took it to mean: “She’s out—but she’ll come soon.”

With the light now, he saw the room clearly. It was unlike any space he’d ever entered. Faded, yes, but full of quiet charm. The Countess had brought fragments of her old life—“bits of wreckage,” she’d called them. A few slender dark tables, a delicate Greek bronze on the mantle, a stretch of red damask nailed over stained wallpaper behind two Italian-style paintings in aged frames. Archer prided himself on knowing art. He’d read Ruskin as a boy, Symonds, Vernon Lee, Hamerton, Pater’s *The Renaissance*. He spoke of Botticelli with ease, mentioned Fra Angelico with mild condescension. Yet these pictures unsettled him. They weren’t like anything he’d seen in Italy—perhaps because he wasn’t seeing them with the eyes of a traveler, but of a man caught in a strange house, waiting for a woman who might not come.

He regretted not telling May. What if she came? What would she think of him sitting alone in the dusk, as if already at home? But he’d come. He meant to wait.

He sank into a chair, stretched his feet toward the logs. The atmosphere was unlike any he’d known—quiet, intimate, charged with something foreign. This shabby rented house, surrounded by pampas grass and Rogers statuettes, had been transformed by touch and taste. He tried to decipher the magic: the way chairs were grouped, the single pair of Jacqueminot roses in a thin vase—never bought singly, yet here, just two. And the scent—something beyond perfume, like a distant bazaar, Turkish coffee, ambergris, dried roses.

His thoughts drifted to May’s future home. Mr. Welland had already picked a new house on East Thirty-ninth Street—greenish-yellow stone, modern, built by young architects rebelling against brownstone. The plumbing was perfect. Archer had hoped to travel, delay the decision. But the Wellands approved of a long European honeymoon—maybe even Egypt—but insisted on a house for the couple upon return. His fate was sealed. Every evening, he’d climb the iron railings, step into a Pompeian vestibule, pass through yellow wood paneling into a hall. Above, a bay window in the drawing room—he couldn’t picture what May would do with it. She accepted the purple satin and gold tufting of her parents’ room, the fake Buhl tables, the gilt cabinets filled with Saxe china. He imagined she’d want the same. His only comfort: she’d probably let him arrange his library—plain bookcases, no glass doors, “sincere” Eastlake furniture.

The maid returned, drew the curtains, pushed a log, and said softly: “Verra—verra.” Then she left.

Archer rose and began pacing. Should he stay? His presence felt increasingly absurd. Had he misunderstood? Was she not expecting him?

A sound echoed down the street—hooves on cobblestones. A carriage stopped. He pulled back the curtain. In the dim glow of a streetlamp, he saw Julius Beaufort’s compact English brougham, drawn by a roan horse. The banker stepped out, helping a woman from the vehicle. It was the Countess.

Beaufort stood, hat in hand, speaking. She shook her head. They exchanged a handshake. He climbed back into the carriage. She walked up the steps.

When she entered, she showed no surprise at finding him there. Surprise was not part of her nature.

“How do you like my funny house?” she asked.

“To me, it’s like heaven,” he replied.

She untied her velvet bonnet, tossed it aside with her cloak, and looked at him with thoughtful eyes. “You’ve arranged it delightfully,” he added, aware of how flat it sounded—but trapped by convention, desperate to say something true.

“Oh, it’s a poor little place. My relations despise it. But at least it’s less gloomy than the van der Luydens’.”

The words stunned him. Few dared speak ill of the van der Luydens’ stately home. Those privileged to enter called it “handsome”—though they trembled within. But suddenly, he was glad she’d said it. She’d named the fear everyone felt.

“It’s delicious—what you’ve done here,” he repeated.

“I like the little house,” she admitted. “But I suppose what I really love is the blessedness of being here—in my own country, my own town—and being alone.”

Her voice was so soft he barely heard the last part. But awkwardly, he picked it up.

“You like being alone?”

“Yes. As long as my friends keep me from feeling lonely.”

She sat near the fire. “Nastasia will bring tea soon,” she said, gesturing for him to return to his chair. “I see you’ve already chosen your corner.”

Leaning back, she folded her arms behind her head, gazing into the flames beneath lowered lids. “This is the hour I like best—don’t you?”

A sense of propriety made him answer: “I was afraid you’d forgotten the hour. Beaufort must have been very engrossing.”

She smiled. “Why—have you waited long? Mr. Beaufort took me to see several houses—since it seems I’m not allowed to stay in this one.”

She dismissed both men—Beaufort and him—and continued: “I’ve never been in a city where there’s such a resistance to living in _des quartiers excentriques_. What does it matter where one lives? I’m told this street is respectable.”

“It’s not fashionable.”

“Fashionable! Do you all care so much about that? Why not make your own fashions? But I suppose I’ve lived too independently. At any rate, I want to do what you all do—I want to feel cared for, safe.”

He was touched—as he had been the night before when she spoke of needing guidance. “That’s what your friends want,” he said. “New York’s an awfully safe place,” he added, with a flash of irony.

“Yes, isn’t it? One feels that,” she exclaimed, missing the sarcasm. “Being here is like—like being taken on a holiday when one has been a good little girl and done all one’s lessons.”

The comparison was meant kindly, but it grated. He didn’t mind mocking New York himself—but hearing others treat it lightly annoyed him. He wondered if she truly grasped how powerful it was, how nearly it had crushed her. The Lovell Mingotts’ dinner, hastily patched together from scraps of society, should have shown her how close she’d come to disaster. But either she hadn’t realized it, or had forgotten in the triumph of the van der Luyden evening. Archer leaned toward the first. He thought her vision of New York still undifferentiated—and that thought irritated him.

“Last night,” he said, “New York laid itself out for you. The van der Luydens do nothing by halves.”

“No—how kind they are! It was such a nice party. Everyone seems to hold them in such high esteem.”

Her words were too mild. She might have spoken of a tea party at the dear Miss Lannings’.

“The van der Luydens,” Archer said, feeling pompous, “are the most powerful influence in New York society. Unfortunately—because of her health—they receive very seldom.”

She unclasped her hands, looked at him thoughtfully. “Isn’t that perhaps the reason?”

“The reason—?”

“For their great influence. That they make themselves so rare.”

He flushed, stared—then understood. With one sentence, she’d undone their power. He laughed, and let them fall.

Nastasia brought tea—Japanese cups without handles, small covered dishes—placing the tray on a low table.

“But you’ll explain these things to me,” Madame Olenska continued, leaning forward to hand him his cup. “You’ll tell me all I ought to know.”

“It’s you who are teaching me,” he said. “Opening my eyes to things I’ve looked at so long I’ve ceased to see them.”

She detached a small gold cigarette case from her bracelet, offered it to him, then lit one herself. On the mantle were long spills for lighting.

“Ah—then we can both help each other. But I need help so much more. You must tell me exactly what to do.”

It was on the tip of his tongue to say: “Don’t be seen driving with Beaufort—” But he was too deep in the room’s spell, in her world. To give such advice would be like warning someone bargaining for attar of roses in Samarkand to pack arctics for winter. New York seemed farther away than Samarkand. And if they were to help each other, she was already doing the first service—making him see his own city from the outside.

Through the wrong end of a telescope, it looked small, distant. But from Samarkand, it would look the same.

A flame leapt from the logs. She bent over the fire, stretching her thin hands close—until a faint halo glowed around her nails. The light touched the dark rings of hair escaping her braids, made her pale face paler.

“There are plenty of people to tell you what to do,” Archer said, vaguely envious.

“Oh—all my aunts? And my dear old Granny?”

She considered it calmly. “They’re all a little vexed with me for setting up on my own—poor Granny especially. She wanted to keep me with her. But I had to be free—”

He was moved by the casual way she spoke of Catherine Mingott—the formidable matriarch. He wondered what had driven her to crave even the loneliest freedom.

Still, he said, “Your family can advise you. Explain differences. Show you the way.”

She lifted her brows. “Is New York such a labyrinth? I thought it so straight up and down—like Fifth Avenue. And with all the cross streets numbered!”

She sensed his disapproval and added, with a rare smile that lit her whole face: “If you knew how I like it for just _that_—the straight-up-and-downness, and the big honest labels on everything!”

He saw his chance. “Everything may be labeled—but everybody is not.”

“Perhaps. I may simplify too much—but you’ll warn me if I do.”

She turned from the fire to look at him. “There are only two people here who make me feel as if they understand what I mean and can explain things: you and Mr. Beaufort.”

Archer winced. Then, quickly adjusting, he understood—sympathized, even pitied. She’d lived so close to darkness that she still breathed easier in its air. But since she believed he understood, his duty was to show her Beaufort as he truly was—with all he represented—and make her abhor it.

“I understand,” he said gently. “But at first, don’t let go of your older friends’ hands—your Granny Mingott, Mrs. Welland, Mrs. van der Luyden. They admire you. They want to help.”

She shook her head, sighed. “Oh, I know—I know! But only on condition that they don’t hear anything unpleasant. Aunt Welland put it exactly that way when I tried... Does no one want to know the truth here, Mr. Archer? The real loneliness is living among kind people who only ask you to pretend!”

She raised her hands to her face. He saw her shoulders shake. “Madame Olenska!” he cried, rising, bending over her. He took one of her hands, clasping it like a child’s, murmuring reassurances. But in a moment, she freed herself, looked up, wet lashes gleaming.

“Does no one cry here, either?” she said, laughing as she straightened her braids. “I suppose there’s no need to, in heaven.”

It was burned into his mind: he had called her “Ellen”—twice—and she hadn’t noticed.

Far down the inverted telescope, he saw the faint white figure of May Welland—here, in New York.

Then Nastasia appeared, saying something in rich Italian. Madame Olenska, touching her hair, flashed: “Gia—gia.” And the Duke of St. Austrey entered, leading a magnificent woman in black wig and crimson plume, swathed in fur.

“My dear Countess,” the Duke announced, “I’ve brought an old friend—Mrs. Struthers. She wasn’t invited to last night’s party, and she wants to know you.”

The Duke beamed. Madame Olenska advanced with a murmur of welcome.

“Of course I want to know you, my dear!” Mrs. Struthers boomed, her voice matching her feathers and wig. “I want to know everyone who’s young, interesting, charming. And the Duke tells me you like music—didn’t you, Duke? You’re a pianist, I believe? Well, do you want to hear Sarasate tomorrow? I have something going on every Sunday—when New York doesn’t know what to do with itself, I say: ‘Come and be amused.’ And the Duke thought you’d be tempted.”

Madame Olenska’s face lit up. “How kind! How good of the Duke to think of me!”

She pushed a chair forward. Mrs. Struthers sank into it with pleasure. “Of course I shall be happy to come.”

“That’s all right, my dear. And bring your young gentleman with you.”

She extended a bold hand to Archer. “I can’t place your name—but I’m sure I’ve met you. I’ve met everybody—here, Paris, London. Aren’t you in diplomacy? All the diplomatists come to me. You like music too? Duke, you must be sure to bring him.”

The Duke rumbled “Rather” from his beard. Archer bowed stiffly, feeling like a schoolboy among careless elders.

He wasn’t sorry the visit ended—only that it had lasted so long, wasting emotion.

As he stepped out into the winter night, New York surged back—vast, immediate. May Welland was again the loveliest woman in it.

He turned into his florist’s to send her the daily box of lilies-of-the-valley—only to realize he’d forgotten them that morning.

As he wrote a note, he glanced around the shop—and saw a cluster of yellow roses. Sun-golden, fiery, unlike any he’d seen. For a moment, he thought of sending them to May instead.

But they didn’t look like her. Too rich, too strong.

In a sudden shift of mood, almost without thought, he signaled the florist to pack the roses in another box. He slipped his card into a second envelope—wrote “Countess Olenska” on it.

Then, just as he turned to leave, he pulled the card out again—and left the envelope blank.

“They’ll go at once?” he asked, pointing to the roses.

The florist nodded.

X. The next day, he persuaded May to slip away for a walk in the Park after luncheon. As was customary among old-fashioned Episcopalian families in New York, she usually attended church with her parents on Sunday afternoons. But Mrs. Welland had quietly allowed this small rebellion—having that very morning convinced her daughter of the necessity of a long engagement, so they might have time to prepare a hand-embroidered trousseau with all the proper number of dozens.

The day was radiant. The bare arches of trees lining the Mall were set against a sky like lapis lazuli, and snow glittered beneath them like shattered crystal. It was the kind of weather that brought out May’s natural glow, and she shone like a young maple touched by frost. Archer felt proud under the admiring glances they drew, and the simple joy of being with her washed over his deeper doubts.

“It’s so delicious—waking every morning to smell lilies-of-the-valley in my room!” she said, smiling. “Yesterday they came late. I didn’t have time in the morning—”

“But your remembering each day to send them makes me love them more than if you’d just given a standing order,” he replied, “like one’s music teacher—just as I know Gertrude Lefferts’s did when she and Lawrence were engaged.”

“Oh, they would!” Archer laughed, amused by her sharpness. He glanced sideways at her rosy cheek and, feeling rich and secure, added: “When I sent your lilies yesterday afternoon, I saw some magnificent yellow roses and sent them off to Madame Olenska. Was that right?”

“How sweet of you! She adores flowers like that. It’s odd she didn’t mention it—she lunched with us today and spoke of Mr. Beaufort sending her orchids, and cousin Henry van der Luyden a whole hamper of carnations from Skuytercliff. She seems so surprised to receive them. Don’t people send flowers in Europe? She thinks it such a lovely custom.”

“Oh, well, no wonder mine were overshadowed by Beaufort’s,” Archer muttered, irritated. Then he remembered—he hadn’t included a card. He regretted speaking of the roses at all. He wanted to say, *I called on your cousin yesterday*, but hesitated. If Madame Olenska hadn’t mentioned it, it might seem awkward for him to bring it up. Yet not saying anything gave the whole matter an air of secrecy he disliked. To distract himself, he turned to their future—plans, engagements, Mrs. Welland’s insistence on a long courtship.

“If you call it long!” she teased. “Isabel Chivers and Reggie were engaged two years. Grace and Thorley nearly a year and a half. Why aren’t we doing quite well as we are?”

It was the classic maidenly retort, and he felt embarrassed at how childish it sounded. No doubt she’d simply repeated what she’d heard—but she was nearing twenty-two, and he wondered when “nice” women began to think for themselves.

“Never, if we won’t let them,” he mused, recalling his impulsive words to Mr. Sillerton Jackson: *Women ought to be as free as we are—* Soon, he would have to lift the veil from this young woman’s eyes and let her see the world. But how many generations of women had been buried blindfolded in family vaults? He shivered, thinking of the scientific theories he’d read—the Kentucky cave-fish, born without eyes because they never needed them. What if, when he asked May to open hers, they could only stare into emptiness?

“We might be better off,” he said. “We might be together—travel.”

Her face lit up. “That would be lovely,” she admitted. She’d love to travel. But her mother wouldn’t understand wanting to do things differently.

“As if the mere ‘differently’ didn’t explain everything!” he insisted.

“Newland! You’re so original!” she exclaimed, delighted.

His heart sank. He realized he was saying exactly what every young man in his position was expected to say—and she was responding exactly as tradition dictated—even calling him original.

“Original?” he said, stopping and facing her. “We’re all cut from the same paper, like those dolls folded from the same sheet. We’re patterns stenciled on a wall. Can’t you and I break free, May?”

She looked at him with bright, unclouded admiration.

“Mercy—shall we elope?” she laughed.

“If you would—”

“You *do* love me, Newland. I’m so happy.”

“But then—why not be happier?”

“We can’t behave like characters in novels, can we?” she said gently.

“Why not—why not—why not?” he pressed.

She looked slightly bored. She knew they couldn’t—she just couldn’t come up with a reason. “I’m not clever enough to argue with you,” she said, relieved to find a word that would end the conversation. “But that kind of thing is rather… vulgar, isn’t it?”

“Are you so afraid of being vulgar?” he asked sharply.

She blinked. “Of course I would hate it—so would you.”

He stood silent, tapping his stick against his boot-top. She seemed to sense she’d found the perfect way to close the subject and continued cheerfully: “Oh, did I tell you I showed Ellen my ring? She thought it the most beautiful setting she’d ever seen. Nothing like it in the rue de la Paix, she said. I love you, Newland, for being so artistic!”

The next afternoon, before dinner, Archer sat smoking sullenly in his study. He hadn’t stopped at his club on the way home from the office—where he practiced law in the unhurried manner typical of wealthy New Yorkers. He was in low spirits, restless, haunted by the dread of repeating the same routine every day at the same hour.

“Sameness—sameness!” he muttered, the word echoing like a tuneless refrain. He imagined the familiar figures in tall hats lounging behind the plate-glass windows of the club. He knew precisely what they’d be discussing—probably the Duke, or the golden-haired lady who’d appeared in Fifth Avenue in a canary-yellow brougham drawn by black cobs (widely believed to be Beaufort’s doing). Such women—called “women” in society circles—were rare in New York; even rarer were those who drove their own carriages. Miss Fanny Ring’s sudden appearance had caused a stir. Only the day before, her carriage had passed Mrs. Lovell Mingott’s, and the latter had immediately rung the bell and ordered her coachman to take her home.

“What if it had happened to Mrs. van der Luyden?” people whispered, horrified.

Archer could already hear Lawrence Lefferts ranting about the collapse of society. He raised his head irritably as his sister Janey entered, then quickly bent over his book—Swinburne’s *Chastelard*, newly published—as if he hadn’t noticed her.

She glanced at the table piled with books, opened a volume of the *Contes Drolatiques*, made a face at the old French, and sighed: “What learned things you read!”

“Well—?” he asked, watching her hover like Cassandra.

“Mother’s furious.”

“Furious? With whom? About what?”

“Miss Sophy Jackson was here. She said her brother would come after dinner—she couldn’t say much, because he forbade her. He wants to give all the details himself. He’s with cousin Louisa van der Luyden now.”

“For heaven’s sake, try a fresh start,” he groaned. “It would take an omniscient God to make sense of this.”

“It’s not the time to be profane, Newland,” she said, eyes wide. “Mother feels badly enough about your not going to church…”

With a sigh, he returned to his book.

“Newland! Listen. Your friend Madame Olenska was at Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s party last night. She went there with the Duke and Mr. Beaufort.”

At the last part, a wave of irrational anger surged through him. He laughed to cover it.

“Well, what of it? I knew she meant to.”

Janey paled. “You knew—and you didn’t try to stop her? Warn her?”

“Stop her? Warn her?” He laughed again. “I’m not engaged to the Countess Olenska!”

The words sounded absurd even to himself.

“You’re marrying into her family.”

“Oh, family—family!” he sneered.

“Newland—don’t you care about Family?”

“Not a brass farthing.”

“Nor about what cousin Louisa van der Luyden will think?”

“Not half a one—if she thinks such old-maid nonsense.”

“Mother is not an old maid,” Janey said, lips pinched.

He wanted to shout: *Yes, she is—and so are the van der Luydens—and so are we all, when it comes to touching reality.* But he saw her face begin to crumple, and felt ashamed.

“Hang Countess Olenska! Don’t be a goose, Janey—I’m not her keeper.”

“No,” she said, “but you *did* ask the Wellands to announce your engagement sooner so we could all support her. And if it hadn’t been for that, cousin Louisa would never have invited her to the dinner for the Duke.”

“Well—what harm was there in inviting her? She was the most beautiful woman in the room. She made the dinner less funereal than usual.”

“You know cousin Henry asked her to please you. He persuaded cousin Louisa. Now they’re so upset, they’re leaving for Skuytercliff tomorrow. I think, Newland, you’d better go down. You don’t seem to understand how mother feels.”

In the drawing-room, he found his mother. She looked up from her embroidery. “Has Janey told you?”

“Yes.” He tried to keep his tone calm. “But I can’t take it seriously.”

“Not the fact that we’ve offended cousin Louisa and cousin Henry?”

“The fact that they can be offended by something as trivial as Countess Olenska attending a party at a woman they consider common.”

“Consider—!”

“Well, who is—but who has good music and entertains people on Sundays, when the whole city is dying of boredom?”

“Good music? All I know is, there was a woman who stood on a table and sang songs like they sing in Paris. There was smoking and champagne.”

“Well—that happens elsewhere. The world still turns.”

“I don’t suppose you’re really defending the French Sunday?”

“I’ve heard you complain often enough about the English Sunday when we were in London.”

“New York is neither Paris nor London.”

“Oh, no, it’s not!” he groaned. “You mean society here isn’t as brilliant? Probably true—but we belong here. People should respect our customs when they come among us. Especially Ellen Olenska—she came back to escape the kind of life they lead in brilliant societies.”

He said nothing.

After a pause, his mother ventured: “I was going to put on my bonnet and ask you to take me to see cousin Louisa before dinner.”

He frowned.

“I thought you might explain to her what you just said—about how society abroad is different, how people aren’t as particular, and that Madame Olenska may not have realized how we feel. It would be, you know, dear,” she added with innocent tact, “in Madame Olenska’s interest if you did.”

“Dearest mother, I really don’t see how we’re involved. The Duke took Madame Olenska to Mrs. Struthers’s—in fact, he brought Mrs. Struthers to call on her. I was there when they arrived. If the van der Luydens want to quarrel with someone, the real culprit is under their own roof.”

“Quarrel? Newland, have you ever known cousin Henry to quarrel? Besides, the Duke is his guest—and a stranger. Strangers don’t discriminate. How should they? Countess Olenska is a New Yorker—she should have respected New York’s feelings.”

“Well, then, if they must have a victim, you have my permission to throw Madame Olenska to them,” he snapped. “I don’t see myself—or you—offering up to expiate her sins.”

“Oh, of course you see only the Mingott side,” his mother said, voice tinged with hurt.

The butler drew back the portières and announced: “Mr. Henry van der Luyden.”

Mrs. Archer dropped her needle and pushed her chair back. “Another lamp,” she called to the servant, while Janey adjusted her mother’s cap.

Mr. van der Luyden stepped into the room, and Archer moved forward.

“We were just talking about you, sir,” he said.

Mr. van der Luyden seemed taken aback. He removed his glove to shake hands, smoothed his hat shyly, and Janey offered him a chair.

“And the Countess Olenska,” Archer added.

Mrs. Archer paled.

“Oh—a charming woman,” Mr. van der Luyden said, regaining composure. “I just visited her. She has a real gift for arranging flowers. I sent her carnations from Skuytercliff, and I was astonished. Instead of grouping them in big bunches like our gardener does, she scattered them loosely—here and there. I can’t describe it. The Duke told me: ‘Go see how cleverly she’s arranged her drawing-room.’ And she has. I’d really like to take Louisa to see her—if the neighborhood weren’t so… unpleasant.”

A silence followed.

Mrs. Archer pulled her embroidery from the basket, and Archer leaned against the fireplace, twisting a hummingbird-feather screen in his hand. Janey’s face lit up as the second lamp was lit.

“The fact is,” Mr. van der Luyden continued, stroking his grey leg with a pale hand heavy with the Patroon’s signet ring, “the fact is, I came to thank her for her lovely note about my flowers—and also—to give her a friendly warning about letting the Duke carry her off to parties. I don’t know if you’ve heard—”

Mrs. Archer smiled indulgently. “Has the Duke been carrying her off to parties?”

“You know what these English grandees are. They’re all alike. Louisa and I adore our cousin—but it’s hopeless to expect people used to European courts to care about our little republican distinctions. The Duke goes where he’s amused.”

He paused. No one spoke.

“Yes—it seems he took her to Mrs. Struthers’s last night. Sillerton Jackson just came to us with the story, and Louisa was rather troubled. So I thought the best way was to go straight to Countess Olenska and explain—by the merest hint, you understand—how we feel in New York about certain matters. I felt I might, without impropriety, because the evening she dined with us, she rather suggested… she let me see that she would appreciate guidance. And she *did*.”

Mr. van der Luyden looked around the room with a mild benevolence that Mrs. Archer dutifully mirrored.

“How kind you both are, dear Henry—always! Newland will especially appreciate what you’ve done, for dear May and their new relations.”

She shot her son a warning glance. He said: “Immensely, sir. But I was sure you’d like Madame Olenska.”

Mr. van der Luyden looked at him gently. “I never invite anyone to my house, my dear Newland,” he said, “whom I do not like. And so I’ve just told Sillerton Jackson.”

He glanced at the clock. “But Louisa will be waiting. We’re dining early to take the Duke to the Opera.”

After the portières closed behind him, silence fell.

“Gracious—how romantic!” Janey burst out.

No one knew what she meant. Her family had long since stopped trying.

Mrs. Archer shook her head. “Provided it all turns out for the best,” she said, as if knowing it wouldn’t.

“Newland, you must stay and see Sillerton Jackson when he comes tonight. I really don’t know what to say to him.”

“Poor mother! But he won’t come—” her son laughed, bending to kiss her frown away.

XI. Some two weeks later, Newland Archer sat in quiet contemplation within the private corner of the office of Letterblair, Lamson and Low—attorneys at law—when he was summoned by the firm’s senior partner. Old Mr. Letterblair, a man whose name had long stood for legal wisdom among three generations of New York's most distinguished families, sat behind his massive mahogany desk, looking troubled. As he smoothed his neatly trimmed white whiskers and ran a hand through his tousled gray hair above his prominent brows, Archer couldn’t help but think how much he resembled a family physician exasperated by a patient whose illness defied diagnosis.

“My dear sir,” Letterblair began—always addressing Archer with formal respect—“I have asked you to come in to discuss a matter of some delicacy—one I prefer not yet to share with Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood.” The men mentioned were the other senior partners, though in truth, all the names listed on the firm’s letterhead belonged to deceased predecessors. Letterblair himself was, in professional terms, the grandson of the original founder. He leaned back, frowning slightly. “For family reasons—” he paused, then smiled faintly—“the Mingott family.”

Archer looked up sharply.

“The Countess Olenska,” said Letterblair, “wishes to sue her husband for divorce. Mrs. Manson Mingott called me yesterday. Certain documents have been entrusted to me.”

He paused, tapping his fingers lightly on the desk. “Given your impending marriage into the family, I thought it wise to consult you first—before any further action is taken.”

A sudden warmth surged through Archer’s temples. He had seen the Countess only once since their last meeting—briefly, at the opera, seated in the Mingott box. In that time, she had receded from his thoughts, replaced by May Welland’s radiant presence, which had slowly restored the world to its familiar order. He had dismissed talk of a divorce as idle gossip, something whispered by Janey Mingott and no more than that. Divorce, after all, was nearly as repugnant to him as it was to his mother. And now, here was Letterblair—clearly acting under pressure from old Catherine Mingott—trying to draw him into the affair. Why must it be *him*? There were plenty of Mingott men who could handle such matters. He wasn’t even officially part of the family yet.

Still, he waited quietly for Letterblair to continue.

The older man unlocked a drawer and pulled out a sealed packet. “If you would kindly review these papers—”

Archer frowned. “I beg your pardon, sir—but precisely because of this prospective connection, I should prefer you consult either Mr. Skipworth or Mr. Redwood.”

Letterblair blinked, surprised. It was rare for a junior partner to decline such an opportunity. He inclined his head. “I respect your scruples, sir—but in this case, I believe true discretion demands otherwise. This request does not come from me, but from Mrs. Manson Mingott herself—and her son. I’ve spoken with Lovell Mingott, and also with Mr. Welland. They both named you.”

Archer felt a flicker of irritation. For the past fortnight, he’d drifted along, allowing May’s charm and grace to gently erase the lingering tension stirred by the Mingotts. But now, this summons from the matriarch awakened a sense of obligation—of being expected to serve the family’s interests before his own. “Her uncles should deal with this,” he said firmly.

“They have,” Letterblair replied. “The family has discussed it. They oppose the idea. But the Countess is resolute. She insists on a legal opinion.”

Archer remained silent. He hadn’t opened the packet yet.

“Does she intend to remarry?” he asked finally.

“I believe it’s been suggested—but she denies it.”

“Then—”

“Would you do me the favor, Mr. Archer, of reviewing these papers first? Afterward, we can discuss them thoroughly. Then I’ll give you my view.”

Archer left reluctantly, clutching the documents like a burden. Since their last encounter, he had unconsciously allowed life to move forward without him—letting go of the emotional weight the Countess had placed upon his heart. That solitary evening by the firelight had brought them close, but the arrival of the Duke of St. Austrey and Mrs. Struthers had shattered the moment, and her warm greeting of them had restored social equilibrium. Two days later, he had watched her re-enter society with ease and dignity, and he had told himself, with a touch of bitterness, that a woman who could so skillfully charm powerful men did not need the private comfort—or public defense—of a young man like him.

That realization had cleared his mind. He could not imagine May Welland ever seeking counsel from strangers in moments of crisis. She seemed purer, stronger, more admirable than ever. He had even agreed to her wish for a longer engagement, moved by her simple reply when he pressed for haste: “You know, when it comes down to it, your parents have always let you have your way since you were a little girl.” And she had answered, with a clarity that touched him deeply: “Yes—and that’s what makes it so hard to refuse the very last thing they’ll ever ask of me as a little girl.”

That was the voice of old New York—the kind of sentiment he longed to hear from his future wife. To breathe that air was to feel clean, clear, untouched by shadows.

But the papers he now read offered no such clarity. They were mostly correspondence between Count Olenski’s lawyers and a French firm handling the Countess’s financial affairs. There was also a brief letter from the Count to his wife. When Archer finished reading it, he stood abruptly, stuffed the documents back into their envelope, and returned to Letterblair’s office.

“Here are the letters, sir,” he said, voice strained. “If you wish, I’ll see Madame Olenska myself.”

“Thank you, Mr. Archer,” Letterblair replied, relieved. “Come to dinner tonight, if you’re free. We’ll discuss it afterward. If you wish, you may call on our client tomorrow.”

Archer walked home that afternoon. The winter evening was crisp and bright, the moon rising innocent and pale over the rooftops. He wanted nothing more than to fill his lungs with the pure air, to remain silent until he could speak freely with Letterblair after dinner. He knew his decision was inevitable—he would meet the Countess himself. Not because he wished to interfere, but because he could not bear the thought of her secrets being exposed to others.

A wave of compassion washed over him, erasing his earlier indifference. She stood before him now as a vulnerable figure, wounded and defiant, fighting against forces too great for her to control. He remembered her words about Mrs. Welland’s plea to spare her from anything “unpleasant”—and suddenly understood the cost of such purity. Was their society merely a group of Pharisees, pretending innocence while hiding behind rigid rules?

He wondered, painfully, whether he could reconcile his natural revulsion at moral failure with his deep sympathy for human weakness. For the first time, he saw how shallow his own principles had always been. He was known as a man unafraid of risk—his secret romance with Mrs. Thorley Rushworth had earned him a reputation for daring. But now he realized: Mrs. Rushworth was “that kind of woman”—frivolous, vain, drawn more to secrecy than to love. The affair had been, in truth, just another episode common among young men of his class—a momentary lapse that ended without consequence, leaving them with clean consciences and a firm belief in the sacred divide between respectable women and those meant only to be enjoyed and pitied.

This illusion was carefully nurtured by mothers, aunts, and grandmothers—women like Mrs. Archer—who believed that when such things happened, the man was foolish, but the woman was always guilty. Any woman who loved recklessly was seen as scheming, dangerous, capable of ensnaring a good man. The solution? Marry early, marry well, and trust your wife to keep you safe.

But Archer began to suspect that in the complex, ancient societies of Europe, love was not so easily categorized. Among the idle, wealthy, and isolated, such dilemmas might arise naturally. Perhaps there were cases where a woman—sensitive, refined, alone—was pushed into a bond that conventional morality condemned, not from malice, but from desperation.

At home, he wrote a note to the Countess, asking when she might receive him. A messenger returned with word: she would be at Skuytercliff, visiting the van der Luydens, but would be alone that evening after dinner. The note was written on a half-sheet, untidy, undated—yet her handwriting was strong, confident. He smiled at the image of her staying at Skuytercliff, that vast, silent estate. But then he thought: nowhere else would she feel the coldness of minds so determined to ignore the “unpleasant.”

He arrived at Letterblair’s house at seven sharp, eager to excuse himself soon after dinner. He already had his own opinion formed from the papers—but he didn’t want to debate it with his senior partner. Letterblair was a widower, and they dined alone in a dim, shabby room adorned with yellowing prints of “The Death of Chatham” and “The Coronation of Napoleon.” On the sideboard stood a decanter of Haut Brion and another of old Lanning port—a gift from a client, Tom Lanning, who had sold off his cellar before dying mysteriously in San Francisco. The incident had been less scandalous than the sale itself.

They ate slowly—velvety oyster soup, shad and cucumbers, broiled turkey with corn fritters, canvas-back duck with currant jelly, and celery mayonnaise. Letterblair, who lunched on tea and a sandwich, dined with solemn deliberation, urging Archer to do the same. Finally, after dessert, cigars were lit, and Letterblair, leaning back in his chair and pushing the port glass toward the fire, said:

“The whole family opposes the divorce. And rightly so.”

Archer felt himself shift instantly to the opposite side. “But why, sir? If there ever was a case—”

“Well—what’s the use? _She’s_ here. _He’s_ there. The Atlantic is between them. She won suffice beyond what he’s already returned. Their marriage contracts protect that. As things stand over there, Olenski’s behaved generously—he might have left her penniless.”

Archer knew this. He said nothing.

“But,” Letterblair continued, “I understand she doesn’t care about the money. So, as the family says—why not let well enough alone?”

Archer had entered the house fully agreeing with Letterblair’s stance. But now, hearing it voiced by this comfortable, self-satisfied old man, it sounded hollow—like the sterile logic of a society devoted solely to shielding itself from discomfort.

“I think that’s for her to decide,” he said quietly.

“H’m. Have you considered the consequences if she chooses divorce?”

“You mean the threat in his letter? What weight would that carry? It’s nothing more than the rant of an angry man.”

“Yes—but if he actually defends the suit, it might stir some unpleasant talk.”

“Unpleasant—!” Archer burst out.

Letterblair raised an eyebrow, studying him. The young man sensed the futility of trying to explain what stirred in him. He bowed instead, letting the elder man continue.

“Divorce is always unpleasant,” Letterblair repeated.

“You agree with me?” he asked, after a pause.

“Naturally,” Archer said.

“Then I may count on you? The Mingotts may count on you—to use your influence against the idea?”

Archer hesitated. “I can’t promise until I’ve spoken with the Countess.”

Mr. Letterblair looked alarmed. “Mr. Archer, I don’t understand. Do you wish to marry into a family with a scandalous divorce hanging over it?”

“I don’t think that changes the matter,” Archer replied.

Letterblair set down his glass, fixing him with a wary gaze. Archer realized he might lose his mandate—and for some reason, he didn’t want that. Now that the task had been thrust upon him, he refused to abandon it. He needed to reassure the unimaginative old man who served as the Mingotts’ legal conscience.

“You may be certain, sir,” he said, “that I will not commit myself until I report to you. I simply meant—I’d rather not form an opinion until I’ve heard her side.”

Letterblair nodded, satisfied. Such caution was the hallmark of the best New York tradition. Archer glanced at his watch, excused himself with an engagement, and took his leave.

XII. In the old-fashioned world of New York, dinner was served at seven, and the custom of after-dinner calls—though mocked by some in Newland Archer’s circle—still held sway across much of society. As he walked up Fifth Avenue from Waverley Place, the wide street lay quiet and nearly empty, save for a cluster of carriages drawn up before the Reggie Chiverses’ house, where a dinner was being given for the Duke. Occasionally, an elderly gentleman wrapped in a heavy overcoat and muffler would appear, climb the steps of a brownstone, and vanish into a gas-lit foyer. Passing through Washington Square, Archer noticed old Mr. du Lac visiting his cousins, the Dagonets; further along, near West Tenth Street, he saw Mr. Skipworth, a partner in his own firm, clearly on his way to call on the Miss Lannings. A little higher up, Beaufort emerged from his home, silhouetted sharply against the glow of his doorway, descended to his private brougham, and drove off into the night toward a destination that seemed secretive, perhaps even scandalous. It wasn’t Opera night, nor was anyone hosting a soirée—so Beaufort’s outing must have been something more private, more intimate.

Archer couldn’t help but connect it with a small house beyond Lexington Avenue, recently spruced up with bright window curtains and flower boxes. The canary-yellow brougham of Miss Fanny Ring often waited there, suggesting a certain romantic intrigue. Beyond the polished, predictable sphere of Mrs. Archer’s social world lay another realm—the uncharted territory of artists, musicians, and “people who wrote.” These individuals had never sought inclusion in the rigid structure of high society. Though they might be considered odd or unpredictable, they were generally respectable in their own way. They preferred solitude, keeping to themselves and their craft.

Once, Medora Manson had attempted to create a literary salon, but it fizzled out quickly—no one in the literary world wanted to attend. Others tried too, most notably the Blenkers: a loud, intense mother and her three flamboyant daughters, all mimicking her mannerisms. There, one could meet Edwin Booth, Patti, William Winter, George Rignold, magazine editors, and critics. But Mrs. Archer and her friends viewed such people with cautious unease. They were strange, uncertain, carrying secrets in their pasts that no one quite understood. Literature and art were respected in their circles—Mrs. Archer often lamented how much richer society had once been when it included figures like Washington Irving, Fitz-Greene Halleck, and the poet of *The Culprit Fay*. Those men had been gentlemen. But the new generation of writers—unknown, unconventional, often linked to the stage or opera—defied the old standards. Their appearance, their lifestyles, their very hair made them impossible to judge by the old rules.

“Back when I was young,” Mrs. Archer used to say, “we knew everyone between the Battery and Canal Street. Only those we knew had carriages. You could always place someone. Now? No one is what they seem. And I’d rather not try.”

Only old Catherine Mingott, with her lack of moral judgment and her almost vulgar disregard for fine distinctions, might have bridged that gap. But she never read a book or admired a painting—music mattered only because it reminded her of gala nights at the Italiens during her glory days at the Tuileries. Perhaps Beaufort, bold and daring as she was, could have brought about a fusion—but his grand mansion and silk-stockinged footmen stood in the way of anything informal. Besides, he was as illiterate as Mrs. Mingott and saw “fellows who wrote” as mere entertainers, hired to amuse the rich. No one wealthy enough to challenge this view ever had.

Newland Archer had known all this since childhood. He accepted it as part of the world he lived in. Yet he also knew other places—where painters, poets, novelists, scientists, and great actors were as admired as dukes. He often dreamed of living in rooms filled with conversation from Mérimée, Thackeray, Browning, or William Morris. But such dreams were impossible in New York. They unsettled him just to think of.

He knew many of these artists and writers—he met them at the Century Club or at the emerging musical and theatrical societies. He enjoyed them there, but found them tedious at the Blenkers’, where they were paraded like exotic curiosities among fervent, dowdy women. Even after stimulating conversations with Ned Winsett, he always left feeling that both worlds were narrow—and that the only way to expand either was to reach a point where they naturally merged.

This thought returned to him now as he approached Madame Olenska’s door, wondering what kind of society she had lived in, suffered in, and perhaps—secretly—enjoyed. He remembered her amusement when she told him how her grandmother Mingott and the Wellands objected to her living in a “Bohemian” quarter full of “people who wrote.” It wasn’t the danger they feared—it was the poverty. But she hadn’t understood that nuance; she assumed they thought literature itself was compromising. She herself had no such fear. Her drawing-room, where books usually belonged only in libraries, was scattered with novels by Paul Bourget, Huysmans, and the Goncourt brothers—names unfamiliar yet intriguing to Archer.

As he neared her door, he felt again the peculiar way she turned his values upside down. To truly help her now, he had to imagine a life utterly different from his own.

Nastasia opened the door with a mysterious smile. On the hall bench lay a sable-lined overcoat, a folded opera hat of dull silk bearing a gold monogram—J.B.—and a white silk muffler. There was no mistaking whose belongings they were: Julius Beaufort’s.

Archer felt a surge of anger—so strong he nearly scribbled a note on his card and walked away. Then he remembered: in his letter to Madame Olenska, he had held back from saying he wished to speak privately. So if she received others, it was his own discretion that had left her open to visitors. He stepped inside, determined to make Beaufort feel unwelcome—and to stay longer than him.

Beaufort leaned against the mantelshelf, draped with antique embroidery held by brass candelabras filled with yellow church candles. His chest thrust forward, shoulders braced, one large patent-leather shoe planted firmly. He smiled down at Madame Olenska, who sat half-reclined on a sofa angled toward the fireplace. Behind her, a table overflowed with flowers—orchids and azaleas, clearly gifts from Beaufort’s greenhouses. Her head rested on one hand, her sleeve wide open, leaving her arm bare to the elbow.

Evening guests typically wore “simple dinner dresses”—tight, whalebone-silk gowns with lace ruffles at the neck and tight sleeves flaring slightly to reveal a bracelet or velvet band. But Madame Olenska ignored tradition. She wore a long red velvet robe trimmed with glossy black fur around her chin and down the front. Archer recalled seeing a portrait by Carolus Duran in Paris—a bold, sheath-like gown with fur framing the face. It was oddly defiant to wear fur indoors in winter, especially with a muffled throat and bare arms. Yet the effect was striking.

“Lord love us—three whole days at Skuytercliff!” Beaufort boomed, sneering. “You’d better take your furs and a hot-water bottle.”

“Why? Is the house so cold?” she asked, holding out her left hand to Archer in a gesture that subtly invited a kiss.

“No,” Beaufort replied, nodding casually to the young man. “But the missus is.”

She smiled faintly. “I thought her so kind. She came herself to invite me. Granny says I must go.”

“Granny would, of course,” Beaufort said. “And I say it’s a shame you’re missing the oyster supper I’ve planned for you at Delmonico’s next Sunday—with Campanini, Scalchi, and a lot of jolly people.”

She hesitated, glancing between them. “Ah—that does tempt me! Except for the other evening at Mrs. Struthers’s, I haven’t met a single artist since I’ve been here.”

“What kind of artists?” Archer asked boldly. “I know a few painters—very good fellows—I could bring if you’d allow me.”

“Painters?” Beaufort scoffed. “Are there painters in New York?”—as if their existence depended solely on whether he bought their work.

Madame Olenska turned to Archer, her eyes soft. “That would be charming. But I was thinking of dramatic artists—singers, actors, musicians. My husband’s house was always full of them.”

She said “my husband” without hesitation, almost wistfully, as if recalling lost pleasures.

Archer looked at her, puzzled. Was it lightness—or deception—that let her speak so freely of the past while risking everything to escape it?

“I do think,” she continued, addressing both men, “that the *imprévu* adds to one’s enjoyment. It’s perhaps a mistake to see the same people every day.”

“It’s confoundedly dull,” Beaufort grumbled. “New York is dying of dullness. And when I try to liven it up, you go back on me. Come—think better of it! Sunday is your last chance. Campanini leaves next week for Baltimore and Philadelphia. I’ve got a private room, a Steinway, and they’ll sing all night.”

“How delicious! May I think it over and write to you tomorrow morning?”

Her tone was polite, but carried the faintest hint of dismissal. Beaufort sensed it. Unused to being sent away, he stared at her, a stubborn line forming between his brows.

“Why not now?”

“It’s too serious a question to decide at this hour.”

“Do you call it late?” she countered coolly.

“Yes,” he said. “Because I still have business to discuss with Mr. Archer.”

“Ah,” Beaufort snapped.

There was no appeal. With a slight shrug, he regained composure, took her hand, kissed it with practiced ease, and called out from the doorway: “I say, Newland—if you can persuade the Countess to stay in town, you’re included in the supper.” Then he left, his heavy footsteps echoing down the hall.

For a moment, Archer thought Letterblair must have warned her of his coming. But her next words proved otherwise.

“You know painters, then? You live in their milieu?”

Her eyes sparkled with interest.

“Oh, not exactly,” he said. “I don’t think any art has a true milieu here. They’re more like a thinly settled outpost.”

“But you care for such things?”

“Immensely. When I’m in Paris or London, I never miss an exhibition. I try to keep up.”

She looked down at the tip of her satin boot peeking from beneath her robes. “I used to care immensely too. My life was full of such things. But now… I want to try not to.”

“You want to try not to?”

“Yes. I want to cast off my old life. To become just like everyone else here.”

Archer reddened. “You’ll never be like everyone else.”

She raised her eyebrows slightly. “Ah, don’t say that. If you knew how much I hate being different!”

Her face darkened, like a tragic mask. She leaned forward, clasping her knee, gazing into distant shadows. “I want to get away from it all.”

He cleared his throat. “I know. Mr. Letterblair told me.”

“Ah?”

“That’s why I’ve come. He asked me to—well, you see, I’m in the firm.”

She looked surprised, then her eyes brightened. “You mean you can manage it for me? I can talk to you instead of Mr. Letterblair? Oh, that will be so much easier!”

Her tone touched him. His confidence swelled. He realized she had mentioned business to Beaufort only to dismiss him—and to have driven Beaufort away felt like a small victory.

“I’m here to talk about it,” he repeated.

She sat silent, her head resting on her arm. Her face looked pale, dimmed by the deep red of her dress. Suddenly, she seemed fragile, almost pitiful.

“Now we’re coming to hard facts,” he thought, recognizing the same instinctive recoil he’d criticized in his mother and her generation. How little practice he had in dealing with such situations! The language itself felt foreign—like something from fiction or the theater. Facing what was to come, he felt as awkward as a boy.

After a pause, she spoke with sudden intensity: “I want to be free. I want to wipe out the past.”

“I understand.”

Her face warmed. “Then you’ll help me?”

“First—perhaps I should know more.”

She seemed surprised. “You know about my husband—my life with him?”

He nodded.

“Well—then—what more is there? In this country, are such things tolerated? I’m a Protestant—our church doesn’t forbid divorce in such cases.”

“Certainly not.”

They fell silent again. The specter of Count Olenski’s letter loomed between them. It filled only half a page—just as Archer had described it to Letterblair: a vague, angry accusation. But how much truth lay behind it? Only his wife could know.

“I’ve reviewed the papers you gave to Mr. Letterblair,” Archer said at length. “Well—can there be anything more abominable?”

“No.”

She shifted slightly, shielding her eyes with her hand. “Of course you know,” he continued, “that if your husband chooses to fight the case—as he threatens to—”

“Yes?”

“He can say things—things that might be… disagreeable to you. Say them publicly. They’d spread, and harm you—even if—”

“If—?”

“I mean: no matter how unfounded.”

She paused. So long that Archer studied the shape of her other hand, resting on her knee—the rings on her fourth and fifth fingers, none of which was a wedding band.

“What harm could such accusations do me here—even if he made them public?”

He almost cried out: “My poor child—far more harm than anywhere else!” Instead, he echoed Mr. Letterblair’s voice: “New York society is a very small world compared to the one you’ve known. And it’s ruled, despite appearances, by a few people with… rather old-fashioned ideas.”

She said nothing.

“Our ideas about marriage and divorce are particularly old-fashioned,” he went on. “Our laws favor divorce—our customs don’t.”

“Never?”

“Well—not if the woman, however injured, however innocent, has any appearance against her. If she’s exposed herself to offensive insinuations…”

She lowered her head slightly. He waited, hoping for outrage, a denial. Nothing came.

A tiny clock ticked beside her. A log cracked, sending sparks upward. The room seemed to hold its breath with him.

“Yes,” she murmured. “That’s what my family tells me.”

He winced. “It’s not unnatural—”

“Our family,” she corrected gently. “For you’ll soon be my cousin.”

“I hope so.”

“And you take their view?”

He rose, wandered across the room, stared blankly at a painting, then returned to her side. How could he say: *Yes—if what your husband hints is true, or if you can’t disprove it?*

“Sincerely,” she interjected.

He looked into the fire. “Sincerely, then—what would you gain that would compensate for the certainty of terrible talk?”

“But my freedom—is that nothing?”

At that moment, it flashed across him: the charge in the letter was true. She hoped to marry the man involved. How could he tell her that, if she meant to do that, the law of the state would stop her? The mere suspicion made him impatient.

“But aren’t you already free?” he said. “Who can touch you? Mr. Letterblair says the financial matter is settled—”

“Oh, yes,” she said indifferently.

“Well, then—is it worth risking pain and disgrace? Think of the newspapers—their cruelty! It’s all stupid and narrow and unjust—but one can’t change society.”

“No,” she whispered, her voice faint and broken.

He felt a pang of remorse. “The individual is often sacrificed,” he said, pouring out stock phrases, eager to cover the silence. “People cling to conventions—to protect families, children. It’s not personal. It’s collective.”

“It’s my duty,” he added, “to show you how those who love you see this. The Mingotts, the Wellands, the van der Luydens—all your friends. If I didn’t show you honestly how they judge such matters, wouldn’t that be unfair?”

He spoke urgently, pleading with her, desperate to avoid the abyss of her silence.

“No,” she said slowly. “It wouldn’t be fair.”

The fire had died to ash. One lamp gurgled softly. She rose, wound it up, and returned to the hearth—without sitting. Standing was a signal: there was nothing more to say.

Archer stood too.

“Very well,” she said abruptly. “I will do what you wish.”

Blood rushed to his forehead. Caught off guard, he grasped her hands clumsily. “I—I do want to help you.”

“You do help me. Good night, my cousin.”

He bent and kissed her hands—cold, lifeless. She pulled away. He turned, found his coat and hat under the dim gaslight, and stepped out into the winter night, bursting with words he could not speak.

XIII. It was a crowded night at Wallack’s Theatre. The play, *The Shaughraun*, starring Dion Boucicault in the title role and featuring Harry Montague and Ada Dyas as the lovers, had drawn a full house. The English company was at the height of its fame, and this particular production always filled every seat. In the galleries, audiences were swept up in fervent delight; in the stalls and boxes, people smiled gently at the familiar sentiments and theatrical clichés—yet still found themselves captivated by the performance.

One scene, in particular, held the entire theatre spellbound. After a quiet, almost wordless farewell between Montague and Miss Dyas, he turned to leave. She stood near the mantelpiece, gazing into the fire, dressed in a simple gray cashmere gown that clung to her tall figure and fell in long, graceful lines to the floor. Around her neck was a narrow black velvet ribbon, its ends trailing down her back. As he stepped away, she leaned her arms on the mantel and buried her face in her hands. He paused at the threshold, then quietly returned, lifted one end of the ribbon, kissed it softly—and slipped out without a sound, leaving her motionless and unaware.

The curtain fell just as the silence deepened. That moment—so understated, so full of unspoken sorrow—was the reason Newland Archer came to see *The Shaughraun*. He considered the parting of Montague and Dyas among the most moving scenes he had ever witnessed, rivaling even the finest performances he’d seen in Paris or London. Its restraint, its quiet ache, touched him more deeply than any grand gesture or dramatic cry ever could.

On this evening, however, the scene stirred something deeper than usual. Without knowing why, it reminded him of his own recent farewell with Madame Olenska—a conversation they’d shared a week or two before, after their private talk in her drawing room. There was no real similarity between the two moments: Montague was young, handsome, and heartbroken; Archer was neither. Miss Dyas was a striking red-haired woman of imposing presence, while Ellen Olenska was delicate, radiant, and profoundly different in every way. And yet, as the curtain closed, Archer felt a strange tremor in his chest. Why did this silent departure echo so vividly in his mind?

It was not the situation, but the feeling—the sense of something hidden, something tragic just beneath the surface. Madame Olenska had an uncanny way of making ordinary moments feel charged with drama, of suggesting depths beyond everyday life. She never spoke of them directly, but they were there, woven into her very being—perhaps from her mysterious past, perhaps from some inner intensity that made her seem destined for extraordinary things, even when she tried hardest to avoid them.

Archer had always believed that people weren’t shaped so much by chance as by their own nature—their innate capacity to draw events toward them. From the first moment he met her, he’d sensed that about Ellen Olenska. She seemed quiet, passive, even fragile—but somehow, she carried within her a storm that refused to be contained. Her calm demeanor only heightened the impression that she had lived through tempests others couldn’t imagine.

And now, after their last meeting, he was certain of what he’d suspected: Count Olenski’s accusations were not entirely baseless. The man who had once been known only as “the secretary” in her husband’s past—someone who had helped her escape—had likely earned her gratitude. She had fled a life so unbearable, so cruel, that it was natural she would be thankful to anyone who saved her. But that same gratitude, in the eyes of society and the law, made her appear complicit. It placed her on equal footing with her despicable husband.

He had told her this, as he had to. He had also made her understand that kind-hearted, conventional New York—where she might have hoped for mercy—would be the least forgiving place of all. To explain this to her, and to see her accept it with such quiet resignation, had wounded him deeply. A strange mix of pity and jealousy stirred within him—like a secret burden she had laid upon him, humbling her, yet making her more compelling.

He was glad it had been him she confided in, not Mr. Letterblair’s cold judgment, or the awkward glances of her family. Immediately, he took it upon himself to reassure them all that she had abandoned her plan to seek a divorce. He framed it as her own realization of its futility, and with great relief, everyone moved on—turning away from the "unpleasantness" she had spared them.

“I knew Newland would handle it,” Mrs. Welland said proudly of her future son-in-law. And old Mrs. Mingott, summoning him for a private chat, had praised his cleverness, then added impatiently, “Silly goose! I told her myself it was nonsense. Wanting to pass herself off as Ellen Mingott, a plain old maid, when she’s lucky enough to be a married woman and a countess!”

These words, these gestures—everything that followed—had made the memory of that final conversation with Ellen burn brighter in Archer’s mind. So when the curtain fell on the stage, he found himself blinking back tears. He rose to leave, turning slightly toward the side of the house—and froze.

There, in a box, sat Madame Olenska, surrounded by the Beauforts, Lawrence Lefferts, and several others. He hadn’t spoken with her alone since that evening, and had deliberately avoided being near her in public. But now their eyes met.

Mrs. Beaufort recognized him at once and gave her languid little wave—an invitation he could not refuse. Beaufort and Lefferts made room, and after a few polite exchanges with Mrs. Beaufort—whose chief concern was maintaining her flawless appearance—Archer seated himself behind Madame Olenska.

Only Mr. Sillerton Jackson remained in the box, murmuring to Mrs. Beaufort about Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s recent Sunday reception, where rumors claimed dancing had taken place. Under the cover of this trivial gossip, Madame Olenska turned to him and whispered, her voice barely audible.

“Do you think,” she asked, glancing toward the stage, “he’ll send her yellow roses tomorrow morning?”

Archer flushed. His heart leapt. He had visited her twice since their talk—each time sending a box of yellow roses, each time without a note. He had assumed she didn’t know who sent them. Now, here she was, mentioning them—not with suspicion, but with a quiet recognition that stirred something fierce and tender inside him.

“I was thinking of that too,” he replied, his voice low. “I was going to leave just to take the picture with me.”

To his surprise, her cheeks darkened slightly, as if she were embarrassed by her own boldness. She looked down at the mother-of-pearl opera glass in her gloved hands and said, after a pause, “What do you do while May is away?”

He frowned faintly. “I stick to my work,” he answered, annoyed by the question.

It was true. The Wellands had left the previous week for St. Augustine, where Mr. Welland always spent the winter—out of concern for his bronchial health. He was a gentle man, soft-spoken, deeply rooted in routine. His wife and daughter always traveled with him, not out of obligation, but out of love. The family adored him, and he was their anchor. His sons, both lawyers like Archer, joined him for Easter and returned together. It was unthinkable for him to go alone.

Archer had wanted to go too—to spend a few weeks in sunshine, rowing boats with May, enjoying the warmth of southern skies. But professional duty kept him bound. To ask for leave in mid-winter would have been seen as frivolous by the Mingott clan. He accepted May’s absence with the resignation he now saw would be a constant companion in marriage.

He felt Madame Olenska watching him, her gaze soft beneath lowered lashes.

“I’ve done what you advised,” she said suddenly.

He blinked. “Ah—I’m glad,” he managed, uncomfortable that she brought it up now.

She went on, breathless: “I understand… you were right. But sometimes life is difficult… perplexing…”

“I know,” he said quietly.

“And I wanted to tell you,” she continued, lifting her opera glass quickly as the box door opened and Beaufort’s voice boomed in, “that I *do* feel you were right—and I’m grateful to you.”

Archer stood up. He left the box, and soon the theatre.

Earlier that day, he had received a letter from May. With her characteristic honesty, she had written: “Please be kind to Ellen while we’re gone. She likes you and admires you so much. And though she doesn’t show it, she’s still very lonely and unhappy. Granny and Uncle Lovell don’t understand her—they think she’s more worldly, more fond of society than she really is. I can see that New York must seem dull to her, even if the family won’t admit it. She’s used to wonderful music, art, brilliant people—artists, authors, the kinds of things you care about. Granny only sees dinners and dresses, but I can see that you’re almost the only one who can talk to her about what matters.”

Her wisdom—her insight—had touched him deeply. He had loved her for that letter. But he hadn’t meant to act on it. He was busy. And as an engaged man, he didn’t want to be seen as overly involved in Ellen’s affairs. He thought she could protect herself better than May imagined—she had Beaufort’s devotion, van der Luyden’s protection, and countless others waiting in the wings.

Yet every time he saw her, every time he spoke to her—even briefly—he felt a quiet certainty: May’s simplicity wasn’t foolishness. It was intuition. And she was right.

Ellen Olenska was lonely. And she was unhappy.

XIV. As Archer stepped into the lobby, he collided with Ned Winsett, the only one among the so-called "clever people" Janey mentioned whom he truly valued for more than idle chatter over cigars or at a club dinner. He’d caught sight of Winsett’s hunched back earlier across the hall and noticed how his gaze had lingered on the Beaufort box. They exchanged greetings, and Winsett suggested a bock at a small German eatery just around the corner. But Archer, not in the mood for their usual round of half-serious banter, declined, citing work waiting at home. Winsett laughed, “Oh, well, so have I—guess I’ll be the Industrious Apprentice too.” They walked together, side by side, until Winsett broke the silence: “Look, I’m really after something—what’s the name of that dark-haired woman in your box? With the Beauforts, wasn’t she? The one Lefferts can’t seem to take his eyes off.”

Archer felt an odd pang—why did Winsett care about Ellen Olenska? And why bring up Lefferts? It wasn’t like him to pry. Still, Archer remembered: Winsett was a journalist. “Not for a story, I hope?” he asked, smiling.

“Not for the press,” Winsett replied. “Just for myself. She lives near me—strange neighborhood for someone so striking—and she was kind to my boy. He tumbled down her front steps chasing his kitten, cut his knee badly. She came rushing out bareheaded, carrying him, bandage already wrapped tight around his leg. So calm, so beautiful—my wife was so overwhelmed, she never thought to ask her name.”

A warmth spread through Archer’s chest. The gesture itself wasn’t rare—any decent woman might’ve done it—but it *was* Ellen. That sudden, unguarded rush, the quiet grace, the way she’d silenced Mrs. Winsett with her presence—it was all unmistakably her.

“It’s Countess Olenska,” he said softly. “Granddaughter of old Mrs. Mingott.”

“Whew—Countess!” Winsett whistled. “Well, I didn’t know they were this neighborly. Mingotts aren’t.”

“They would be,” Archer said, “if you let them.”

They both knew the argument was old and pointless—the endless debate about whether men of talent and taste could ever belong in high society. There was no point in continuing.

“I wonder,” Winsett mused, “how a Countess ends up in our little slum?”

“Because she doesn’t care where she lives—or what we think of it,” Archer answered, proud of the image he painted. “She sees no meaning in our petty rules.”

“H’m,” Winsett muttered. “Been in bigger places, I suppose.” Then he turned toward Broadway. “Here’s my corner.” He disappeared into the street, and Archer stood watching him go, lost in thought.

Winsett had flashes of insight—rare moments when he saw deeper than most. That was what made him compelling. Yet why did such a man accept failure so quietly, at a time when others still fought? Archer knew Winsett had a wife and child, though he’d never met them. Their meetings always happened at the Century Club or in dim corners of restaurants frequented by writers and actors—places where Winsett often proposed a bock. Once, he’d mentioned his wife was an invalid—perhaps true, or perhaps just a polite excuse for not attending parties. Winsett despised social rituals. Archer, who dressed at night because it felt cleaner and easier, saw it as part of a tired Bohemian act—a pose that made fashionable life seem simpler, less pretentious. But despite that, Archer always found himself drawn to Winsett. Whenever he saw that lean face, those melancholy eyes, he’d pull him aside for hours of talk.

Winsett wasn’t a journalist by choice. He was a writer—pure, serious, born too late for a world that no longer needed literature. After publishing a slim volume of literary essays—only 120 sold, thirty given away, the rest destroyed by publishers to make room for fashion spreads and temperance ads—he gave up. He took a sub-editor job on *Hearth-fires*, a weekly filled with paper patterns, sentimental love stories, and moralizing ads. He could talk endlessly about it, but beneath the humor lay a bitterness—of youth wasted, talent unappreciated. His conversations stirred Archer, made him measure his own life and feel its emptiness. But Winsett’s life, in truth, held even less. Still, their shared curiosity kept their talks alive—though always within the bounds of thoughtful dilettantism.

“The fact is,” Winsett once said, “life isn’t much suited for either of us. I’m down and out—nothing to do about it. I’ve got one thing to offer, and no market for it. But you—you’re free, you’ve got means. Why don’t *you* get involved? The only way is politics.”

Archer laughed. That was the gap—between Winsett and men like him. Everyone knew in polite circles: “a gentleman couldn’t go into politics.” But he couldn’t say that aloud. Instead, he evaded: “Look at honest men in American politics! They don’t want us.”

“Who’s ‘they’?” Winsett shot back. “Why don’t you all become ‘they’?”

Archer smiled, condescendingly. It was useless. Everyone knew the fate of gentlemen who entered politics—ruined reputations, ruined clothes. The era was over. The city belonged to bosses and immigrants. Decent people retreated to sport or culture.

“Culture!” Winsett scoffed. “If we had any. Just scattered patches—dying out from lack of care. The last bits of Europe’s tradition your ancestors brought. But you’re a tiny minority—no center, no audience, no competition. You’re like portraits in an empty house: ‘The Portrait of a Gentleman.’ You’ll never matter till you roll up your sleeves and dive into the mud. Or emigrate… God, if I could…”

Archer shook his head silently and steered the conversation to books—where Winsett, uncertain but passionate, was always worth listening to.

Emigrate? As if a gentleman could abandon his country! One couldn’t roll up sleeves and get dirty. A gentleman stayed home and abstained. But you couldn’t make Winsett see that. That was why New York—its literary clubs, its exotic restaurants—seemed dazzling at first, then revealed itself as a smaller, duller box than Fifth Avenue’s polished façade.

The next morning, Archer searched the city for yellow roses—vainly. He arrived late at the office, realized no one noticed, and felt a surge of frustration. Why wasn’t he right now on the beach with May Welland? No one believed in his pretense of work. In firms like Mr. Letterblair’s—focused on estates and conservative investments—there were always young men, comfortably off, doing nothing much. They sat at desks, read papers, performed trivial tasks. The law was acceptable because it was a profession—not business. But none of them hoped to rise. The green mold of indifference had already begun to creep over some. Archer shivered—could it be spreading over him too?

He had other interests—travel, art, the clever set May admired. He tried to “keep up,” as he’d told Madame Olenska. But what would happen when he married? Would this narrow space—his real life—shrink further? He’d seen others dream the same dream, then slowly sink into the comfortable routine of their elders.

From the office, he sent a note to Madame Olenska, asking if he might visit that afternoon, and requested she reply at his club. But there was no message. No letter the next day. The silence stung worse than he expected. Even seeing a perfect bouquet of yellow roses in a shop window the following morning, he walked past without a glance.

Only on the third day did a letter arrive—postmarked Skuytercliff, where the van der Luydens had fled after sending the Duke off to sea.

“I ran away,” it began abruptly, skipping all formalities. “The day after I saw you at the play. These kind friends took me in. I wanted quiet—to think. You were right to say how kind they are. I feel safe here. I wish you were with us.”

It ended with a cold “Yours sincerely”—no mention of when she’d return.

Archer was startled. What was she running from? Why did she need safety? At first, he imagined some threat from abroad. Then he reminded himself—he didn’t know her writing style. Women exaggerated. And she wasn’t fully fluent in English—often speaking as if translating from French. *Je me suis évadée*—that phrase alone suggested she might simply have fled boredom, a tiresome schedule. Likely enough—she seemed capricious, easily weary of the moment’s pleasures.

He chuckled at the idea of the van der Luydens whisking her off to Skuytercliff—again, and for no fixed time. The estate rarely opened its doors; even privileged guests received only chilly weekends. But Archer remembered seeing *Le Voyage de M. Perrichon* in Paris—the stubborn, tender devotion of a man rescuing another from a glacier. The van der Luydens had saved Madame Olenska from a fate nearly as frozen. And though many reasons drew him to her, beneath them all was the quiet, persistent urge to keep saving her.

He felt a pang of disappointment. And then, instantly, he recalled: the day before, he’d refused an invitation to spend Sunday with the Reggie Chiverses at Highbank, just below Skuytercliff. He’d grown tired of their noisy parties—coasting, ice-boating, sleigh rides, snow tramps, light flirtations, mild jokes. He’d just received a new batch of books from London and preferred a quiet day at home.

But now, he went straight to the club’s writing room, scribbled a telegram, and instructed the servant to send it immediately. He knew Mrs. Reggie wouldn’t mind a change of plans. There was always room in her generous house.

XV. Newland Archer arrived at the Chiverses’ on Friday evening, and on Saturday he dutifully observed every custom expected of a guest spending a weekend at Highbank. In the morning, he joined his hostess and a few hardy guests for a ride in an ice-boat, cutting through the frosty air with exhilarating speed. Later, he accompanied Reggie on a tour of the farm, where they lingered in the ornate stables listening to lengthy, reverent discussions about horses—each detail treated as if it carried the weight of history.
