After tea, he found himself engaged in quiet conversation with a young woman who had once confessed heartbreak over his engagement, but now spoke animatedly of her own romantic prospects. By midnight, he was caught up in the evening’s merriment: helping place a goldfish in one visitor’s bed, dressing a burglar in the bathroom of a nervous aunt, and joining a spirited pillow fight that spread from the nursery to the basement. Yet by Sunday afternoon, after lunch, he borrowed a cutter and drove across the snow-covered roads to Skuytercliff.

It was said that the house at Skuytercliff resembled an Italian villa. Those who’d never seen Italy believed it; some who had still did. Built in the youth of Mr. van der Luyden during his return from the grand tour, and in anticipation of his marriage to Miss Louisa Dagonet, it stood as a grand wooden structure—painted pale green and white, with a Corinthian portico and fluted pilasters framing each window. From its elevated position, terraces lined with balustrades and urns descended like a steel engraving toward a small, irregular lake edged in asphalt and shaded by rare weeping conifers. On either side, immaculate lawns dotted with “specimen” trees—each unique—stretched into the distance, ending in elaborate iron ornaments. Below, nestled in a hollow, lay the original four-room stone house built by the first Patroon in 1612.

Against the blanketing snow and the dull winter sky, the villa loomed stiff and imposing—more mausoleum than home. Even in summer, it remained distant, aloof, as though no garden ever dared approach within thirty feet of its stern façade. Now, as Archer rang the bell, the long chime echoed through silence so deep it felt like calling from a tomb. The butler who finally answered seemed startled, as if awakened from a long sleep. But Archer, being family, was spared reproach. He was told that the Countess Olenska had already left—driving to afternoon service with Mrs. van der Luyden just three-quarters of an hour earlier.

“Mr. van der Luyden is in, sir,” the butler continued, “but I believe he’s either finishing his nap or reading yesterday’s Evening Post. I heard him say this morning, upon returning from church, that he meant to look through it after luncheon. If you’d like, sir, I might go to the library door and listen—”

Archer thanked him but declined, saying he would meet the ladies. The butler closed the door with solemn dignity, and a groom took the cutter to the stables. Archer walked through the park toward the high road. Though Skuytercliff village was only a mile and a half away, he knew Mrs. van der Luyden never walked, so he stayed on the path, waiting for her carriage.

Then, descending a footpath that crossed the highway, he saw a slight figure in a red cloak, a large dog running ahead. He quickened his pace. Madame Olenska stopped short, smiling. “Ah, you’ve come!” she said, drawing her hand from her muff.

The red cloak made her glow—vivid, alive—as if she were the Ellen Mingott of old. Archer laughed, taking her hand. “I came to see what you were running from.”

Her smile faded. “Ah, well—you’ll see soon enough.”

He frowned. “Do you mean someone’s been chasing you?”

She shrugged—just like Nastasia used to—and replied lightly, “Shall we walk? I’m freezing after the sermon. And what does it matter now you’re here to protect me?”

Heat surged through Archer’s face. He gripped the fold of her cloak. “Ellen—what is it? You must tell me.”

“Oh, later—let’s race first! My feet are frozen to the ground,” she said, laughing, and darted across the snow, the dog barking at her heels.

For a moment, Archer watched—enchanted by the flash of red against the white. Then he followed, catching up at a wicket leading into the park. They met, breathless and laughing, beneath the bare trees.

“You knew I’d come,” he said.

“That shows you wanted me to,” she replied, her voice bright with mischief.

The trees sparkled under the weak winter sun, their branches glittering like crystal. As they walked, the snow seemed to sing beneath their feet.

“Where did you come from?” she asked.

He told her. “I got your note.”

After a pause, she said, her tone slightly cool, “May asked you to take care of me.”

“I didn’t need asking.”

“You mean—I’m so helpless? What a poor thing you all must think me! But women here seem not to feel the need—any more than the blessed in heaven.”

He lowered his voice. “What kind of need?”

“Don’t ask me! I don’t speak your language,” she snapped, almost petulantly.

The words struck him like a blow. He stopped, staring down at her.

“What did I come for, then—if I don’t speak yours?”

“Oh, my friend—” She laid a light hand on his arm. He pleaded earnestly. “Ellen—why won’t you tell me what’s wrong?”

She shrugged again. “Does anything ever happen in heaven?”

They walked on in silence for a while. Then she said, “I will tell you—but where? Where can one be alone in such a house? Every door is open, servants bring tea, logs, newspapers—always watching. It feels like a convent—or a stage, before an audience that never applauds.”

“You don’t like us,” Archer said.

They passed the old Patroon’s house—its squat walls, square windows clustered around a central chimney. The shutters stood wide. Through a freshly washed pane, Archer saw the glow of a fire.

“The house is open!” he said.

“No—only today,” she replied. “I wanted to see it. Mr. van der Luyden lit the fire and opened the windows so we could stop here on the way back from church.” She ran up the steps and tried the door. “It’s still unlocked—how lucky! Come in. We can talk quietly. Mrs. van der Luyden has gone to Rhinebeck to visit her aunts. We won’t be missed for another hour.”

He followed her into the narrow passage. His spirits, dampened by her earlier words, lifted suddenly. The little house seemed to glow with warmth—the panels polished, brass fittings gleaming in the firelight. A bed of embers still glowed beneath an iron pot hanging from an ancient crane. Rush-bottom chairs faced each other across the tiled hearth, and rows of Delft plates lined the shelves.

Archer added a log to the fire. Madame Olenska dropped her cloak and sat. He leaned against the chimney, watching her.

“You’re smiling now,” he said. “But when you wrote, you were unhappy.”

“Yes.” She paused. “But I can’t feel unhappy when you’re here.”

“I won’t be long,” he said, his lips tightening. “I have to go back.”

“No,” she said softly. “But I’m imprudent—I live in the moment when I’m happy.”

The words stirred something deep inside him—a temptation he fought to ignore. He turned away, gazing out at the black tree trunks against the snow. Yet even as he looked, he still saw her—between himself and the trees—leaning over the fire, smiling with languid grace.

His heart pounded. What if she had fled *from* him? What if she had waited until they were here, alone, to tell him so?

“Ellen,” he said, without turning, “if I truly help you—if you really wanted me to come—then tell me what’s wrong. Tell me what you’re running from.”

He stood there, eyes fixed on the snow beyond the window, the space between them vast and charged. For a long moment, silence. And in that silence, he imagined her stepping behind him, arms slipping around his neck.

Then, through the window, he saw a man approaching along the path—Julius Beaufort.

“Ah—!” Archer burst out, laughing.

Madame Olenska had risen and slipped her hand into his. But when she glanced at the window, her face paled. She stepped back.

“So that was it?” Archer said, mockingly.

“I didn’t know he was coming,” she whispered, her fingers still clutching his.

He pulled away, walked into the passage, and threw open the door. “Hallo, Beaufort—this way! Madame Olenska was expecting you.”

Beaufort entered with his usual ease, though a line of tension marked his brow. He couldn’t hide his annoyance. The ostensible reason for his visit was a “perfect little house”—not on the market—that he claimed was exactly right for her. If she didn’t take it immediately, it would vanish. He railed mockingly at her for running off just when he’d found it.

“If only this new wire-talk had been a bit closer to perfection,” he grumbled, “I could’ve told you from town and been warming my toes by the club fire instead of tramping through the snow.”

At this, Madame Olenska shifted the conversation to the fantastical idea of talking from street to street, or even town to town—dreams that brought out references to Poe and Verne, and platitudes about progress. The talk carried them safely back to the main house.

Mrs. van der Luyden hadn’t returned yet. Archer took his leave and walked to fetch the cutter. Beaufort followed the Countess indoors.

It was unlikely he’d be invited to stay overnight—too much luggage, too little cordiality. But he had come a long way for a simple dinner, and that said everything. Beaufort was pursuing her. And Beaufort pursued women only for one reason.

His life at home was dull, childless, stagnant. He craved excitement, especially among the elite. This was the man from whom Madame Olenska was fleeing. But why? Was it because she disliked him—or because she feared herself? Or was it all a ruse?

Archer didn’t believe it was a trick. Though he’d seen little of her, he thought he could read her—her face, her voice. Both betrayed dismay at Beaufort’s arrival.

Yet if she had fled *because* of him, wasn’t that worse than if she’d gone to meet him willingly? If she’d sought him out, she’d become entangled with a man whose reputation was irredeemable. But if she fled *from* him, yet still felt drawn—drawn by his worldliness, his connections, his contempt for convention—then she was caught in a far more dangerous trap.

Beaufort was vulgar, uneducated, proud of his wealth. But he had lived in two worlds, moved among artists and actors, spoken freely of things others avoided. He understood her language better than anyone else could. And that, perhaps, was what frightened her most.

That night, Archer unpacked books from London. Among them was a slim volume of poetry—*The House of Life*. He opened it and was swept into a world unlike any he’d known—warm, rich, tender. All night, he read, imagining a woman with Ellen’s face. When he woke, the city outside looked cold and ordinary. His desk, his office, his pew in Grace Church—all seemed miles away.

“Mercy, how pale you look, Newland!” Janey said at breakfast.

“And you’ve been coughing,” his mother added. “Are you overworking?”

He smiled, letting them believe the worst.

The next few days dragged. Everything tasted flat. He felt buried alive. No word from Ellen. No news of the perfect little house. He saw Beaufort at the club—they nodded, nothing more.

Then, on the fourth evening, he found a note on his table:

Only those words. He tucked it away, amused by the Frenchness of “to you.” That night, after dinner and a play, he returned home past midnight and read it again—slowly, over and over.

There were many ways to reply. But by morning, he had decided. He packed a few clothes into a suitcase and boarded a boat bound for St. Augustine—leaving that very afternoon.

XVI. When Archer walked down the sandy main street of St. Augustine toward the house that had been pointed out to him as Mr. Welland’s, he felt a quiet certainty settle over him. The air was warm and still, thick with the scent of magnolias and sea salt, and when he saw May standing beneath one of the great trees—her hair catching the sunlight like spun gold—he understood why he had waited so long to come. This was not just a visit; it was home. This was where he belonged. And yet, he thought, how foolish he’d been, afraid to leave his desk, to break free from duty, all because of what people might say. He had called himself bold, unbound by convention—but in truth, he had trembled at the idea of stepping outside the rules.

She turned at his approach, her face brightening. “Newland—has anything happened?” she asked, her voice light but edged with curiosity.

He smiled. It would have been more natural, perhaps, if she had read the truth in his eyes—the urgency, the longing—but instead, she looked surprised, then delighted, when he replied simply: “Yes. I found I had to see you.”

Her cheeks flushed, soft and warm, and for a moment the tension between them melted into something tender, almost familiar. He knew then that forgiveness would come easily, that even Mr. Letterblair’s mild disapproval would fade before the kindness of her family.

But the street was too public, too full of polite glances and idle gossip. He longed to be alone with her, to speak freely, to tell her everything—the ache in his chest, the way her presence made the world feel alive again. Still, it was early, and breakfast was not due for another hour. Instead of inviting him inside, she suggested they walk to an old orange grove beyond the town. She had just returned from rowing on the river, and the sun, dancing across the water like scattered coins, seemed to have caught her in its glow.

As they walked beside each other, her stride long and easy, her hair fluttering in the breeze, she looked like a statue carved from youth and light. Her face held a calm serenity, untouched by worry or desire—a purity that calmed Archer’s restless soul. They sat on a wooden bench beneath the citrus trees, and he reached for her, pulling her close, kissing her gently.

It was like drinking from a spring touched by sunlight—refreshing, pure, intoxicating. But perhaps he held her too tightly, for her breath hitched and she pulled back, startled.

“What is it?” he asked, smiling softly.

“Nothing,” she whispered, though her eyes held a flicker of confusion.

A silence fell between them, fragile and sudden. Her hand slipped from his, and he realized this was only the second time he’d kissed her on the lips—the first being their brief embrace in the Beaufort conservatory. And now, he saw, she was shaken, pulled from her usual composure.

To ease the moment, he leaned back, crossing his arms behind his head, shielding his eyes from the glare. “Tell me what you do all day,” he said, letting his voice fall into a relaxed rhythm.

She began to speak—of swimming, sailing, riding, of dances at the inn when a warship docked. A few guests from Philadelphia and Baltimore were staying nearby, and the Selfridge Merrys had come down because Kate Merry had fallen ill. They were planning a tennis court on the beach, though only Kate and May owned racquets, and most of the others hadn’t even heard of the game.

All of it kept her busy, and she confessed she hadn’t had time to read the little vellum book he’d sent her—the *Sonnets from the Portuguese*. But she had memorized “How they brought the Good News from Ghent to Aix,” because it was one of the first poems he’d ever read aloud to her. And she found amusement in telling him that Kate Merry had never heard of Robert Browning.

Then she glanced at her watch and gasped. “We’ll be late for breakfast!” she exclaimed, jumping up.

They hurried back to the modest house with its crooked porch and overgrown garden of plumbago and geraniums. The place was a makeshift refuge, arranged each winter by Mrs. Welland with painstaking care. Mr. Welland, delicate and sensitive, could not endure the discomforts of southern hotels, so year after year, at great expense and effort, his wife created a semblance of home—using disgruntled New York servants and local help alike.

“The doctors insist my husband must feel he’s at home,” Mrs. Welland explained each season to visiting guests, “or the climate won’t help him at all.” And sure enough, at breakfast, Mr. Welland sat beaming, surrounded by delicacies that seemed impossible in such a remote place. “We’re camping,” he declared, piling his plate with golden griddle-cakes drowned in syrup. “I want to teach my wife and May how to rough it.”

They were surprised by Archer’s sudden arrival, but he had cleverly claimed he was recovering from a cold—just beginning to take hold. That excuse, Mr. Welland declared, was perfectly reasonable. “You can’t be too careful, especially in spring,” he said. “If I’d been as prudent at your age, May would be dancing at Assemblies now, instead of spending winters in this wilderness with an old invalid.”

“Oh, but I love it here, Papa,” May replied, her voice warm. “If only Newland could stay, I’d like it a thousand times better than New York.”

“And he shall,” Mrs. Welland said kindly. “Until he’s completely recovered.”

Archer laughed, though he already knew he would manage to extend his illness for a week—long enough to delay his return. And it was ironic, he thought, that Mr. Letterblair’s indulgence was partly due to the fact that Archer had recently resolved the Olenski divorce case with skill and discretion. The firm had praised him highly, and even old Mrs. Manson Mingott had expressed approval. One day, while May was out with her father, Mrs. Welland took the chance to speak of Ellen Olenska.

“I’m afraid Ellen’s ideas are very different from ours,” she said. “She was barely eighteen when Medora Manson took her to Europe—do you remember the stir when she appeared in black at her coming-out ball? Another of Medora’s whims, really. And since then, Ellen has never returned to America. No wonder she’s become so European.”

“But European society doesn’t approve of divorce,” Archer said quietly. “Countess Olenska believed she was conforming to American ideals by seeking freedom.”

It was the first time he had spoken her name since leaving Skuytercliff, and he felt heat rise to his cheeks.

Mrs. Welland smiled gently. “That’s just the kind of thing foreigners imagine about us. They think we dine at two o’clock and accept divorce! That’s why I don’t understand why we invite them to New York. They accept our hospitality—and then go home and repeat these silly stories.”

Archer said nothing. He knew she meant well, but the words stung. He wanted to say that Ellen wasn’t merely a foreigner, but someone who had chosen a different path—one that required courage, not scandal. Yet he remained silent.

Mrs. Welland continued. “But we are truly grateful for your influence in persuading Ellen to abandon her plans. Her grandmother and Uncle Lovell wrote that her change of heart was entirely due to you. In fact, she told her grandmother so herself. She has great admiration for you. Poor Ellen—she was always a difficult child. I wonder what her future holds?”

Archer nearly answered: “What we’ve all made it.” He imagined saying, “If you’d rather she become Beaufort’s mistress than a decent man’s wife, you’ve done exactly the right thing.” But he didn’t speak. He wondered what Mrs. Welland would do if he did—how her composed face would crumble under the weight of truth. There were traces of beauty still in her features, like May’s, and he couldn’t help thinking: Would May grow into that same image of perfect, unyielding innocence?

No, he thought fiercely. Not May. He didn’t want her to carry that kind of innocence—the kind that shuts out imagination, that seals the heart against real feeling.

Later, he hoped to talk to Mrs. Welland about advancing their wedding date. But when the moment came, he found no words strong enough to move her. Relief washed over him when he saw May and her father returning. His only hope now was to plead with May directly.

On the eve of his departure, he walked with her to the ruins of the Spanish Mission. The crumbling walls and arches evoked visions of distant lands—Granada, the Alhambra. May, wearing a wide-brimmed hat that shadowed her clear eyes, lit up at the mention of Spain.

“We might see it all this spring—even the Easter ceremonies in Seville,” he urged, exaggerating his hopes in the hope of gaining more.

“Easter in Seville?” she laughed. “And it’s Lent next week!”

“Why shouldn’t we marry in Lent?” he replied.

She looked shocked. “Oh, Newland—I didn’t mean that. But soon after Easter—so we could sail at the end of April. I know I could arrange it at the office.”

She smiled dreamily, as if picturing a future that was already written in poetry. But he saw it clearly—she was content to dream, not to act. It was like reading beautiful verses that could never come true.

“Oh, go on,” she said. “I love your descriptions.”

“But why should they be only descriptions?” he asked. “Why can’t they be real?”

“We shall, dearest,” she murmured. “Next year.”

Her voice lingered on the word, as if it carried the weight of a promise.

“Don’t you want them to be real sooner?” he pressed. “Can’t I persuade you to break away now?”

She bowed her head, disappearing beneath the brim of her hat. “Why should we waste another year? Look at me, dear. Don’t you understand how much I want you?”

For a moment, she stood motionless. Then she raised her eyes—clear, desperate, unguarded—and he felt his grip loosen slightly.

Suddenly, her expression shifted. “I’m not sure if I _do_ understand,” she said softly. “Is it… is it because you’re not certain of continuing to care for me?”

Archer stood up abruptly. “My God—perhaps,” he said, anger flashing in his voice. “I don’t know.”

May rose too, and as they faced each other, she seemed to grow taller, more dignified. Silence stretched between them—uncomfortable, charged.

“If that’s it,” she said quietly, “is there someone else?”

“Someone else—between you and me?” he echoed, as if the words were unfamiliar.

She nodded. “Let’s talk frankly, Newland. Sometimes I’ve noticed a difference in you—especially since our engagement was announced.”

“Dear—what madness!” he protested.

She smiled faintly. “If it is, it won’t hurt us to talk about it. Or even if it’s true—why shouldn’t we speak of it? You might have made a mistake.”

He stared at the pattern of leaves on the path beneath their feet. “Mistakes are easy to make,” he said. “But if I had made one like the kind you suggest, do you think I’d be begging you to hurry our marriage?”

She looked down, her sunshade trembling slightly in her hands. “Yes,” she said slowly. “You might want—to settle it once and for all. That’s one way.”

The clarity of her voice startled him, but he saw the fear beneath it—the pallor of her profile, the slight quiver of her nostril.

“Well?” he asked, sitting down, trying to lighten the mood with a frown.

She joined him. “You mustn’t think girls know nothing,” she said. “We hear things. We notice. We have feelings. And long before you told me you cared for me, I knew there was someone else you were interested in. Everyone talked about it two years ago at Newport. Once I saw you sitting together on a verandah at a dance. When she came back into the house, her face was sad. I felt sorry for her. I remembered it later—when we were engaged.”

Her voice dropped to a whisper. She clasped and unclasped her hands around her sunshade.

Archer laid his hand over hers. “My dear child—was that it? If you only knew the truth.”

She lifted her head. “Then there’s a truth I don’t know?”

He kept his hand on hers. “I meant—the truth about that old story.”

“But that’s what I want to know,” she said. “What I ought to know. I couldn’t build my happiness on something unfair to someone else. And I believe you’d feel the same. What kind of life could we have on such foundations?”

Her face filled with a tragic courage that humbled him. He felt as if he should kneel before her.

“I’ve wanted to say this for a long time,” she went on. “When two people truly love each other, I understand there may be situations where it’s right to go against public opinion. And if you feel pledged—somehow—to the woman we’ve spoken of—if there’s any way you can fulfill that pledge—even through divorce—Newland, don’t give her up because of me.”

He was stunned. That she feared his past—his fleeting affair with Mrs. Thorley Rushworth—was shocking. But her generosity, her willingness to let him choose his own path, was almost unbearable. It was a kind of heroism he had never expected.

He swallowed hard. “There is no pledge,” he said. “No obligation. Such cases aren’t always simple. But that’s not important. I admire your generosity—because I feel the same way. Each situation must be judged on its own merits—without stupid conventions. I mean—each woman’s right to her liberty—”

He stopped himself, startled by the direction of his thoughts. Then he smiled. “Since you understand so much, dearest—can’t you understand the uselessness of submitting to another form of the same foolish conventions? If there’s no one and nothing between us—doesn’t that argue for marrying quickly, rather than waiting longer?”

She flushed, joy lighting her face. She looked up at him, tears glistening in her eyes.

But then, as if the moment had passed, she sank back into her usual timidity. The courage she showed for others vanished, replaced by the helpless girlhood he knew so well. He realized her strength was reserved for others—not for herself.

He had no heart left to press her further. The woman who had looked at him with such depth and honesty was gone, replaced by the May he had always known—sweet, obedient, afraid to step beyond the lines.

She seemed to sense his disappointment, but didn’t know how to mend it. They stood up and walked silently home, the warmth of the afternoon fading into the quiet of separation.

XVII. "Your cousin the Countess called on mother while you were away," Janey Archer announced to her brother one evening after his return from St. Augustine. The young man, dining quietly with his mother and sister, looked up in surprise. Mrs. Archer’s eyes remained demurely fixed on her plate, as if she had already anticipated the news—and perhaps resented that he should be taken aback by it.

"She wore a black velvet polonaise with jet buttons and carried a tiny green monkey muff—never have I seen her so elegantly dressed," Janey continued, her voice tinged with admiration. "She came alone, early Sunday afternoon. Luckily, the fire was lit in the drawing-room. She had one of those new card-cases, too. Said she wanted to know us because you'd been so kind to her."

Newland smiled. "Madame Olenska always speaks like that about her friends. She's delighted to be back among her own people again."

"Yes," said Mrs. Archer, her tone measured. "She told us so. And I must say, she seems truly grateful to be here."

"I hope you liked her, mother," Newland ventured gently.

Mrs. Archer pressed her lips together. "She certainly makes an effort to please—even when calling upon an old woman."

"Mother doesn’t think her simple," Janey interjected, watching her brother closely.

"It's just my old-fashioned way," Mrs. Archer replied, almost apologetically. "Dear May is my ideal."

Ah," Newland murmured. "They're not alike."

Archer had left St. Augustine burdened with messages for Old Mrs. Mingott, and a few days after returning to town, he paid her a visit. The elderly lady greeted him with unusual warmth. She was deeply thankful that he had persuaded the Countess Olenska to abandon the idea of divorce. When he confessed that he had left his office without permission—rushing down to Florida simply to see May—she let out a soft, hearty chuckle and patted his knee with her plump hand.

"Ah, ah—so you kicked over the traces, did you? And I suppose Augusta and Welland looked as though the world had ended? But little May—she knew better, I'll wager?"

"I hoped she did," he said, "though she wouldn't agree to what I went down to ask."

"And what was that?" she asked, eyes gleaming.

"I wanted her to promise we’d marry in April. What good is another year of waiting?"

Mrs. Manson Mingott twisted her mouth into a mock-prudish grimace and twinkled at him through half-lidded eyes. "'Ask Mamma,' I suppose—the usual story. Ah, these Mingotts—all alike! Born in a rut and can't be pulled out. When I built this house, you’d have thought I was moving to California! Nobody ever _had_ built above Fortieth Street—no, says I, nor above the Battery either, before Columbus discovered America. No, no; not one of them wants to be different. They’re scared of it like smallpox. Ah, my dear Mr. Archer, I thank my stars I’m nothing but a vulgar Spicer. But only one of my children takes after me—my little Ellen."

She paused, still smiling, then added casually: "Now, why in the world didn’t you marry my little Ellen?"

Archer laughed. "For one thing, she wasn’t there to be married."

"No—of course not. More’s the pity. And now it’s too late. Her life is finished."

She spoke with the cold certainty of age, casting earth onto buried dreams. A chill passed through the young man. He hurried to change the subject. "Can’t I persuade you to use your influence with the Wellands, Mrs. Mingott? I wasn’t made for long engagements."

Old Catherine beamed. "No, I can see that. You’ve got a quick eye. When you were a boy, I’m sure you liked being helped first." She threw back her head and laughed, making her chins ripple like waves. "Ah, here’s my Ellen now!"

The portières parted, and Madame Olenska stepped forward, smiling. Her face glowed with quiet joy. She held out her hand to Archer, bowing slightly as she kissed her grandmother’s cheek. "I was just saying to him," Mrs. Mingott declared, "‘Now, why didn’t you marry my little Ellen?’"

Madame Olenska turned to Archer, still smiling. "And what did he answer?"

"Oh, my darling, I leave you to find that out!" the old lady teased. "He’s been down to Florida to see his sweetheart."

"Yes, I know," she replied softly, her gaze lingering on him. "I went to see your mother to ask where you’d gone. I sent a note you never answered. I feared you were ill."

He mumbled something about leaving suddenly, in haste, and intending to write from St. Augustine.

"And once you were there, you never thought of me again!" she said lightly, though her smile seemed carefully composed.

*If she still needs me,* he thought, stung by her tone, *she’s determined not to show it.* He wanted to thank her for visiting his mother—but under the old woman’s sharp, amused stare, he felt unable to speak.

"Look at him—in such a rush to marry he took French leave and ran off to beg the silly girl on his knees! That’s how a lover behaves—that’s how handsome Bob Spicer swept off my poor mother, then grew tired of her before I was weaned—though they only waited eight months for me! But there—you’re not a Spicer, young man. Luckily for you and for May. Only poor Ellen carries any of their wicked blood. The rest are all model Mingotts," she declared scornfully.

Archer noticed that Madame Olenska, seated beside her grandmother, was studying him with quiet intensity. The sparkle had faded from her eyes. "Surely, Granny," she said gently, "we can persuade them between us to do as he wishes."

He rose to leave. As their hands touched, he sensed she was waiting for him to mention her unanswered letter.

"When can I see you?" he asked as she walked him to the door.

"Whenever you like," she said. "But soon—if you want to see the little house again. I’m moving next week."

A pang struck him—the memory of those dim, intimate evenings in her low-ceilinged drawing room. Though brief, they were filled with meaning. "Tomorrow evening?"

She nodded. "Tomorrow—yes, but early. I’m going out."

It was Sunday. If she was going out on a Sunday evening, it could only mean Mrs. Lemuel Struthers’s. He felt a flicker of annoyance—not at her going there (he actually approved of her independence, even against the van der Luydens), but because she would surely meet Beaufort there—someone she must have known she’d encounter. Perhaps she was going precisely for that reason.

"Very well," he repeated, resolving inwardly to arrive late—either to stop her from leaving or to find her already gone. That would be simpler, he decided.

Yet when he arrived at half-past eight beneath the wisteria, he was earlier than planned—though not by much. A restless impulse had driven him there sooner than intended. He reminded himself that Mrs. Struthers’s gatherings weren’t grand affairs; guests often arrived early to minimize their transgression.

But what he hadn’t expected was finding hats and overcoats in the hall. Why had she invited him early if she was hosting dinner?

Closer inspection revealed two peculiar garments. One was a shaggy yellow ulster, cut wide and low—"reach-me-down" style. The other was a battered, rust-colored cloak with a cape—something resembling a "Macfarlane"—clearly worn by someone of immense size. It reeked faintly of damp sawdust, as if it had spent years leaning against barroom walls. On top lay a ragged grey scarf and a semiclerical felt hat.

Archer raised an eyebrow at Nastasia, who met his look with a fatalistic “Gia!” as she opened the drawing-room door.

Inside, his hostess was absent. But standing by the fireplace was another woman—tall, thin, loosely formed, dressed in intricate loops and fringes, with plaids and stripes arranged in a chaotic pattern. Her hair, fading to white, was adorned with a Spanish comb and black lace scarf. Silk mittens, visibly darned, covered her arthritic hands.

Beside her stood two men, enveloped in cigar smoke. One, Archer recognized with surprise, was Ned Winsett. The other, older and enormous, wore morning clothes untouched since morning. His head was crumpled and leonine, his arms moved in broad, sweeping gestures, as if blessing a crowd.

All three stared at an enormous bouquet of crimson roses, anchored by a knot of purple pansies, resting on the sofa where Madame Olenska usually sat.

"What they must have cost at this season—though, of course, it’s the sentiment that matters!" the woman sighed, speaking in short, breathy bursts.

They turned as Archer entered. She stepped forward, offering her hand. "Dear Mr. Archer—almost my cousin Newland!" she said. "I am the Marchioness Manson."

He bowed. "Pleased to meet you."

"My Ellen has taken me in for a few days. I came from Cuba, where I’ve wintered with Spanish friends—delightful, distinguished people: the highest nobility of old Castile. I wish you could know them! But I was summoned by our dear friend here, Dr. Carver. You don’t know Dr. Agathon Carver, founder of the Valley of Love Community?"

Dr. Carver inclined his head gravely. The Marchioness continued: "Ah, New York—New York—how little the life of the spirit has reached it! But I see you do know Mr. Winsett."

"Oh, yes—I reached him some time ago," Winsett said dryly, "but not by that route."

The Marchioness shook her head reprovingly. "How do you know, Mr. Winsett? The spirit bloweth where it listeth."

"List—oh, list!" Dr. Carver rumbled in a deep murmur.

"But do sit down, Mr. Archer. We four have enjoyed a delightful dinner. My child has gone upstairs to dress. She expects you—she’ll be down in a moment. We were admiring these marvelous flowers, which will surprise her when she returns."

Winsett remained standing. "I fear I must go. Please tell Madame Olenska we shall all feel lost when she leaves our street. This house has been an oasis."

"Ah, but she won’t abandon you," the Marchioness said. "Poetry and art are the breath of life to her. Is it poetry you write, Mr. Winsett?"

"Well, no—but I sometimes read it," Winsett replied, nodding at the group before slipping out.

"A caustic spirit—_un peu sauvage_. But so witty! Dr. Carver, you _do_ think him witty?"

"I never think of wit," Dr. Carver declared severely.

"Ah—ah—how merciless he is to us weak mortals, Mr. Archer! But he lives only in the life of the spirit. Tonight, he’s preparing his lecture at Mrs. Blenker’s. Dr. Carver, would there be time before you depart to explain your illuminating discovery of the Direct Contact?"

"No," the doctor said, checking his massive gold watch against Madame Olenska’s tiny traveling clock. "It’s nearly nine. We shouldn’t detain you."

He gathered himself slowly, preparing to leave. "I shall see you later, dear friend?"

"As soon as Ellen’s carriage arrives, I’ll join you," the Marchioness promised. "I hope the lecture hasn’t begun."

Dr. Carver looked at Archer thoughtfully. "Perhaps, if this young gentleman is interested in my experiences, Mrs. Blenker might allow you to come with me?"

"Oh, dear friend—if possible, I’m sure she’d be delighted. But I fear my Ellen counts on Mr. Archer herself."

"That," said Dr. Carver, "is unfortunate—but here is my card."

He handed it over. Archer read the Gothic script:

/c +-----------------------+
| Agathon Carver |
| The Valley of Love |
| Kittasquattamy, N. Y. |
+-----------------------+
c/

Dr. Carver bowed and departed. The Marchioness, sighing—perhaps in relief—motioned Archer to sit.

"Ellen will be down in a moment. Before she comes, I’m so glad of this quiet moment with you."

He murmured a polite response. She continued, her voice low and breathless: "I know everything, dear Mr. Archer. My child has told me all you’ve done for her. Your wise advice, your courageous firmness—thank heaven it wasn’t too late!"

He listened, uneasy. Had anyone else heard of his intervention in her private life?

"Madame Olenska exaggerates," he said. "I simply gave her a legal opinion, as she asked."

"Ah, but in doing so—you became the unconscious instrument of… of… what word do we moderns use for Providence, Mr. Archer?" she asked, tilting her head and lowering her lids mysteriously.

"Little did you know," she whispered, glancing over her shoulder as if fearful of eavesdroppers, "that at that very moment, I was being appealed to—from across the Atlantic!"

She leaned forward, tapping her chest with a delicate finger. "By the Count himself—my poor, mad, foolish Olenski—who asks only to take her back on her own terms."

"Good God!" Archer exclaimed, rising.

"You’re horrified? Of course. I understand. I don’t defend poor Stanislas—he’s always called me his best friend. He doesn’t defend himself—he casts himself at her feet—in my person."

She placed a purple finger to her lips. "I have his letter here."

"A letter?" Archer stammered. "Has Madame Olenska seen it?"

The Marchioness shook her head gently. "Time—time. I must have time. I know my Ellen—haughty, intractable—shall I say, just a shade unforgiving?"

"But, heavens," he protested, "to forgive is one thing—to return to that hell—"

"Ah, yes," she agreed. "She calls it that—my sensitive child. But consider the material side, if one may stoop to such things. Do you know what she’s giving up? Those roses there—acres like them, under glass and open sky, in his terraced gardens at Nice! Jewels—historic pearls, the Sobieski emeralds, sables—but she cares nothing for them. Art and beauty—those she lives for, as I always have. And those surrounded her. Paintings, priceless furniture, music, brilliant conversation—ah, that, my dear young man, is what you’ve no conception of here! And she had it all—and the devotion of an adoring husband."

As she spoke, her face softened into ecstatic remembrance. Archer would have laughed—had he not been stunned. He’d never imagined meeting Medora Manson as a messenger of temptation. Yet now, she seemed to rise from the very depths from which Ellen had escaped.

"She knows nothing yet?" he asked sharply.

The Marchioness pressed a finger to her lips. "Nothing directly—but does she suspect? Who can say? The truth is, Mr. Archer, I’ve been waiting to see you. From the moment I heard of your stand, and your influence over her, I hoped you might support me—convince you…"

"That she ought to go back?" he interrupted fiercely. "I’d rather see her dead!"

"Ah," she murmured, unflinching.

For a moment, silence. Then she lifted her head, listening.

"Here she comes," she whispered, pointing to the bouquet. "Am I to understand that you prefer _that_, Mr. Archer? After all, marriage is marriage… and my niece is still a wife…"

XVIII. "What are you two plotting together, Aunt Medora?" Madame Olenska called out as she swept into the room, radiant and poised as if stepping from a dream. She was dressed for an evening of splendor—her gown shimmered like moonlight on water, and her posture carried the quiet defiance of a woman who knew she commanded attention. “We were just saying,” Mrs. Manson replied, rising with a playful gesture toward the bouquet on the table, “that here’s something lovely to surprise you with.”

Madame Olenska paused, her eyes narrowing slightly as they fell upon the flowers. Her expression didn’t shift—no flicker of joy or warmth—but a pale, electric tension passed over her face, like lightning across a still sky. “Ah,” she said sharply, her voice thin and bright, unlike any tone Archer had ever heard from her. “Who is foolish enough to send me a bouquet? Why a bouquet? And why tonight of all nights? I’m not going to a ball. I’m not a girl being betrothed. Some people simply can’t resist making themselves ridiculous.”

She turned abruptly toward the door, flung it open, and called, “Nastasia!” The maid appeared instantly. Madame Olenska spoke in deliberate Italian, clearly meant for Archer to understand: “Here—throw this into the dustbin!” Then, seeing Nastasia hesitate, she added, “No, no—it’s not the flowers’ fault. Tell the boy to take them to the house three doors down—the one belonging to Mr. Winsett, the dark gentleman who dined here. His wife is ill; perhaps it will cheer her. But the boy’s gone? Then you go yourself. Here—take my cloak and run. I want it out of the house at once. And mind—you’re not to say they came from me!”

She draped her velvet opera cloak over the maid’s shoulders and stepped back inside, closing the door firmly behind her. For a moment, Archer thought he saw tears forming beneath her lashes—her chest rose and fell rapidly under the lace of her dress. But then she laughed, low and sudden, and looked from the Marchioness to him. “And you two—have you become friends?”

“It’s for Mr. Archer to say,” Mrs. Manson replied with a teasing smile. “He’s been very patient while you were dressing.”

“Yes, I gave him plenty of time—my hair wouldn’t behave,” Madame Olenska said, lifting a hand to her elaborate chignon. “But that reminds me—Dr. Carver has left, and you’ll be late at the Blenkers’. Mr. Archer, would you see my aunt into the carriage?”

She followed her aunt into the hall, helped her into a chaotic pile of shawls and overshoes, and called from the doorway: “Mind—the carriage must return for me by ten!” Then she returned to the drawing-room, where Archer found her standing before the mirror, studying her reflection.

It wasn’t customary in New York society for a lady to call her maid “my dear one” or send her out wrapped in her own cloak. Yet here was Madame Olenska doing both—her actions swift, decisive, charged with emotion. Archer felt a strange thrill—not of scandal, but of witnessing something real, unfiltered. In this world, feeling moved swiftly to action.

She didn’t turn when he approached. Their eyes met briefly in the glass, then she spun around, sank onto the sofa, and sighed, “There’s time for a cigarette.” He handed her the box and lit the match. As the flame danced up, illuminating her face, she glanced at him with laughter in her eyes. “What do you think of me when I’m angry?”

He hesitated. Then, with unexpected clarity, he said: “It makes me understand what your aunt said about you.”

“I knew she’d been talking about me. Well?”

“She said you were used to grand things—splendors, amusements, excitement—things we could never give you here.”

Madame Olenska smiled faintly, exhaling a ring of smoke. “Medora is terribly romantic. It’s made up for so many things.”

Archer paused again, bracing himself. “Is her romanticism always accurate?”

“You mean—does she speak the truth?”

She considered. “In most things, yes—something true, something false. But why ask? What has she told you?”

He looked into the fire, then back at her—her presence glowing like a beacon. A tightness gripped his chest. This was their last night together, and soon the carriage would come to take her away. “She says—she pretends—that Count Olenski has asked her to persuade you to return to him.”

Madame Olenska didn’t answer. She sat perfectly still, cigarette held aloft, her face unreadable. Archer remembered how she seemed immune to surprise.

“You knew?” he burst out.

She remained silent so long that the ash dropped from her cigarette. She brushed it aside without looking. “She hinted about a letter—poor darling. Medora’s hints…”

“Did your husband send her here?”

She pondered. “Again—one can’t tell. She claimed a ‘spiritual summons’ from Dr. Carver. I fear she may marry him… poor Medora—there’s always someone she wants to marry. Perhaps the people in Cuba finally tired of her. Maybe she was just a paid companion. Honestly, I don’t know why she came.”

“But you believe she has a letter from your husband?”

After a long silence, she said, “After all—it was expected.”

Archer walked to the fireplace, restless, unable to speak. The minutes were slipping away. At any second, he might hear the wheels of the returning carriage.

“You know your aunt believes you’ll go back?”

She raised her head quickly. A flush spread across her cheeks and neck—a rare, painful blush, as though it burned her. “Many cruel things have been said about me,” she murmured.

“Oh, Ellen—forgive me. I’m a fool, a brute.”

She smiled faintly. “You’re terribly nervous. You have your own troubles. I know you think the Wellands are unreasonable about your marriage. Of course, I agree. In Europe, people don’t understand our long American engagements. They’re not as calm as we are.”

She said “we” with a subtle irony. Archer caught it, but dared not respond. Perhaps she’d steered the conversation away from herself deliberately. After the pain his words had caused, he could only follow her lead. But the ticking clock pressed on him. He couldn’t let another barrier fall between them.

“Yes,” he said suddenly. “I went south to ask May to marry me after Easter. There’s no reason we shouldn’t be married then.”

“And May adores you—and yet you couldn’t convince her? I thought she was too intelligent to be ruled by such absurd superstitions.”

“She *is* intelligent—she’s not their slave.”

Madame Olenska studied him. “Then why don’t you understand?”

Archer reddened and rushed on. “We had a frank talk—almost the first. She thinks my impatience is a bad sign.”

“A bad sign?”

“She thinks it means I can’t trust myself to care for her. That I want to marry her quickly to escape someone I care for more.”

Madame Olenska looked at him curiously. “But if she thinks that—why isn’t she in a hurry too?”

“Because she’s not like that. She’s nobler. She insists even more on the long engagement—to give me time—”

“To give her up for the other woman?”

“If I want to.”

She leaned forward, staring into the flames. Down the street, he heard the clip-clop of horses approaching.

“That *is* noble,” she whispered, her voice breaking slightly. “Yes. But it’s ridiculous.”

“Ridiculous because I don’t care for anyone else?”

“Because you don’t mean to marry anyone else.”

“Ah.”

Silence stretched. Then she looked up. “This other woman—does she love you?”

“Oh, there’s no other woman. The person May imagined—was never—”

“So why, after all, are you so eager?”

“There’s your carriage,” he said.

She half-risen, her eyes distant. Her fan and gloves lay beside her. She picked them up mechanically. “Yes. I suppose I must go.”

“You’re going to Mrs. Struthers’s?”

“Yes.” She smiled. “I must go where I’m invited, or I’d be too lonely. Why not come with me?”

Archer felt a desperate need to keep her near. He ignored her question, leaning against the mantel, watching her hands as she held her gloves and fan. As if he could make her drop them.

“May guessed the truth,” he said. “There *is* another woman—but not the one she thinks.”

Ellen Olenska said nothing. Did not move.

After a moment, he sat beside her, took her hand gently, and unclasped it. The gloves and fan fell between them.

She jumped up, freeing herself, and moved to the other side of the hearth. “Ah, don’t make love to me! Too many people have done that,” she said, frowning.

Archer flushed. “I’ve never made love to you—and I never shall. But you are the woman I would have married—if it had been possible for either of us.”

“Possible for either of us?” She stared at him, astonished. “And you say that—when it’s *you* who made it impossible?”

He gazed at her, lost in darkness pierced by a single beam of light. “*I’ve* made it impossible—?”

“You, you, *you*!” she cried, her lip trembling. “Isn’t it you who made me give up divorcing—because you showed me how selfish and wicked it was? How one must sacrifice oneself to preserve marriage’s dignity, to spare the family shame? And because my family was going to be *your* family—for May’s sake, for yours—I did what you told me. What you proved I should do. Ah,” she laughed suddenly, bitterly, “I’ve never hidden that I did it for *you*!”

She sank onto the sofa, huddled among the folds of her dress like a fallen masquerader. Archer stood by the fireplace, staring at her, unmoving.

“Good God,” he groaned. “When I thought—”

“You thought?”

“Ah, don’t ask me what I thought!”

Still watching her, he saw the same flush creep up her neck, color her face. She sat upright, rigid, facing him.

“I do ask you.”

“Well—there were things in that letter you asked me to read—”

“My husband’s letter?”

“Yes.”

“I had nothing to fear from that letter. Nothing at all. All I feared was bringing notoriety, scandal—on the family—on you and May.”

“Good God,” he groaned again, burying his face in his hands.

The silence settled over them—final, irrevocable. To Archer, it felt like a tombstone pressing down. In all the future ahead, he saw nothing that could lift this weight. He didn’t move. Didn’t raise his head. His eyes remained fixed on utter darkness.

“At least I loved you—” he whispered.

From the other side of the hearth, he heard a soft, stifled sob—like a child’s. He leapt up, rushing to her side.

“Ellen! What madness! Why are you crying? Nothing’s done that can’t be undone. I’m still free. You’ll be too.”

He pulled her into his arms, her face like a wet flower against his lips. All their fears dissolved—ghosts vanishing at dawn. The only thing that shocked him now was that he’d stood arguing across the room for five minutes when just touching her made everything clear.

She kissed him back, but then stiffened. Pulled away. Rose.

“Ah, my poor Newland—this had to happen. But it doesn’t change anything.”

“It changes everything for me.”

“No, no—it mustn’t, it can’t. You’re engaged to May. I’m married.”

He stood too, flushed, resolute. “Nonsense. It’s too late for that kind of lie. We won’t talk of your marriage. But do you see me marrying May after this?”

She remained silent, resting her elbows on the mantel, her profile reflected in the glass. One strand of her chignon had slipped, falling across her neck. She looked weary, almost aged.

“I don’t see you asking May that question,” she said at length. “Do you?”

He shrugged recklessly. “It’s too late to do anything else.”

“You say that because it’s easiest right now—not because it’s true. In reality, it’s too late to do anything but what we both decided.”

“Ah, I don’t understand you!”

She forced a smile—painful, not soothing. “You don’t understand because you haven’t seen how you changed things for me—from the beginning. Long before I knew what you’d done.”

“All I’d done?”

“Yes. I didn’t realize at first how people here avoided me—how they thought I was dreadful. I learned later they refused to invite me to dinner. I found out how you made your mother go with you to the van der Luydens’—how you insisted on announcing your engagement at the Beaufort ball, so I’d have two families to stand by me instead of one—”

At that, he laughed.

“Imagine how blind and unaware I was!” she continued. “New York meant peace, freedom—coming home. I was so happy to be among my own people that everyone seemed kind, glad to see me. But from the start, I felt no one was as kind as you. No one gave me reasons I could understand for doing what felt hard, unnecessary. The good people didn’t convince me—they’d never been tempted. But *you* understood. You knew the world outside tugged at us with golden hands—and yet you hated what it demanded. You hated happiness bought with disloyalty, cruelty, indifference. That was new to me—and better than anything I’ve known.”

She spoke quietly, steadily. Each word landed like molten lead in his chest. He bowed his head, staring at the rug, at the tip of her satin shoe peeking from beneath her dress. Suddenly, he knelt and kissed the shoe.

She bent over him, hands on his shoulders, looking down with eyes so deep he froze under her gaze.

“Ah, don’t let us undo what you’ve done!” she pleaded. “I can’t go back to that old way of thinking. I can’t love you unless I give you up.”

His arms reached for her—but she stepped back. They stood apart, separated by the distance her words had created.

Then, abruptly, his anger erupted. “And Beaufort? Is he to replace me?”

He expected fury. He would have welcomed it. But Madame Olenska only paled, her arms hanging limply, her head lowered as if in thought.

“He’s waiting for you at Mrs. Struthers’s. Why don’t you go to him?”

Archer sneered. She turned and rang the bell.

“I won’t go out tonight,” she said when the maid arrived. “Tell the carriage to fetch the Signora Marchesa.”

After the door closed, Archer looked at her with bitter eyes. “Why this sacrifice? If you’re lonely, I have no right to keep you from your friends.”

She smiled faintly, her lashes damp. “I won’t be lonely now. I *was*. I *was* afraid. But the emptiness is gone. When I turn inward now, I feel like a child entering a room where the light is always on.”

Her voice and presence still held a soft, unreachable grace. Archer groaned again. “I don’t understand you!”

“Yet you understand May!”

He reddened, but held her gaze. “May is ready to give me up.”

“What? Three days after you begged her to hurry the wedding?”

“She refused. That gives me the right—”

“Ah,” she said softly, “you’ve taught me what an ugly word that is.”

He turned away, drained. He felt as if he’d climbed a cliff for hours, only to lose his grip at the top and fall into darkness. If he could have held her again, he might have overcome her logic. But she kept him at bay—with her look, her stillness, and his own awe at her sincerity.

At last, he pleaded again. “If we do this now, it will be worse afterward—worse for everyone—”

“No—no—no!” she nearly screamed, as if he frightened her.

Then—the bell chimed through the house. They hadn’t heard a carriage. They stood motionless, eyes locked.

Outside, Nastasia crossed the hall. The outer door opened. A moment later, she entered, holding a telegram.

“The lady was very happy with the flowers,” she said, smoothing her apron. “She thought it was her signor marito who sent them. She cried a little and called it a folly.”

Madame Olenska smiled, took the yellow envelope, tore it open, and walked to the lamp. When the door closed, she handed the telegram to Archer.

It was dated from St. Augustine, addressed to the Countess Olenska:
> *Granny’s telegram successful. Papa and Mamma agree marriage after Easter. Am telegraphing Newland. Am too happy for words and love you dearly. Your grateful May.*

Half an hour later, when Archer unlocked his front door, he found a similar envelope on the hall table. Inside, a message from May Welland:
> *Parents consent wedding Tuesday after Easter at twelve Grace Church eight bridesmaids please see Rector so happy love May.*

Archer crumpled the paper as if that could erase the news. Then he pulled out his pocket diary, flipped through the pages with trembling fingers—but didn’t find what he sought. He stuffed the telegram into his pocket and climbed the stairs.

A light glowed through the door of Janey’s dressing room. He knocked impatiently. The door opened, and his sister stood before him in her familiar purple flannel robe, hair pinned tightly. Her face was pale, anxious.

“Newland! I hope there’s no bad news in that telegram? I waited—just in case—” (No letter escaped Janey.)

He ignored her. “Look—what day is Easter this year?”

She gasped. “Easter? Newland! Why, the first week in April! Why?”

“The first week?” He turned back to his diary, calculating rapidly. “The first week, you say?”

He threw back his head and laughed—a long, wild laugh.

“For mercy’s sake, what’s wrong?”

“Nothing’s wrong—except that I’m getting married in a month.”

Janey threw her arms around him. “Oh, Newland! How wonderful! I’m so glad! But, dearest, why are you laughing? Hush—don’t wake Mamma.”

Book II

XIX. The day was crisp and bright, the spring wind stirring up clouds of dust as it swept through the city streets. Inside Grace Church, the air was thick with the scent of camphor from the old ladies’ fur coats—faded sables and yellowed ermines—mingling faintly with the sweet perfume of lilies arranged along the altar. Newland Archer stood on the chancel step, his tall hat and pearl-grey gloves held loosely in one hand, waiting for the signal that the bride had arrived. At last, a nod from the sexton confirmed it: the brougham carrying May Welland and her father was approaching.

But there would be delays—always delays. The bridesmaids were already gathered in the lobby like clusters of Easter blossoms, adjusting their trains and whispering nervously. As custom dictated, the groom was expected to stand alone, exposed to the eyes of all, while the guests waited. Archer accepted this ritual with quiet resignation, just as he had once guided others through it, years ago, when he’d been the best man at someone else’s wedding. Now, the roles were reversed, and every detail felt both familiar and strangely distant.

He’d done everything right: the bouquets of lilac and lilies-of-the-valley delivered; the ushers’ sleeve-links and his own scarf-pin carefully chosen; the Bishop and Rector’s fees tucked safely away. His luggage, including the clothes he’d wear after the ceremony, was already at Mrs. Manson Mingott’s house, where the wedding breakfast would take place. A private train compartment had been reserved—part of the unspoken rule that no one should know where the newlyweds would spend their first night together.

“Got the ring?” whispered young van der Luyden, his voice tight with anxiety. He was new to the role of best man and overwhelmed by the weight of tradition.

Archer reached into his waistcoat pocket, fingers brushing the small gold band engraved with *Newland to May, April ——, 187—*. It was there. He returned to his position, heart steady, gaze fixed on the church door.

Above, Handel’s march swelled through the vaulted ceiling, echoing the countless weddings he’d attended before—each one a pageant of elegance and restraint, each bride floating down the aisle like a vision from another world. He watched the faces in the pews, recognizing them all: Mrs. Selfridge Merry with her towering ostrich feathers, Mrs. Beaufort radiant in diamonds. Would they still be here, he wondered, when the Last Trump sounded? And would heaven have its own box seats?

His thoughts drifted to the women in the front rows—their sharp eyes full of curiosity, the men grumbling about having to wear frock coats and fight over food at the breakfast. “Too bad it’s at old Catherine’s,” he imagined Reggie Chivers muttering. “But I hear Lovell Mingott insisted on his chef. Should be good, if you can get to it.”

Sillerton Jackson would likely add: “My dear fellow, haven’t you heard? It’s to be served at small tables, English style.”

Archer glanced toward the left-hand pew, where his mother sat weeping softly beneath her lace veil, her hands tucked into her grandmother’s ermine muff. “Poor Janey,” he thought. “She can only see the people in the front few pews—and most of them are dowdy Newlands and Dagonets.”

Across the white ribbon separating the families, Beaufort sat tall and red-faced, scanning the women with his usual arrogance. Beside him, his wife shimmered in silvery chinchilla and violets. On the other side, Lawrence Lefferts sat stiffly, his polished head guarding the invisible deity of “Good Form” who presided over the ceremony.

Archer smiled faintly. Once, he too had cared deeply about such things—about whether the presents should be displayed, about whether the ritual was perfect. But now, those concerns seemed trivial, almost absurd. A heated debate over whether to “show” the gifts had nearly ruined the final hours before the wedding. Mrs. Welland had put an end to it with tears: “I should as soon let the reporters loose in my house.” And yet, not so long ago, Archer had believed such matters mattered deeply.

Now, he couldn’t help but wonder: “And all the while,” he thought, “real people were living somewhere, real things happening to them…”

Then came the breathless whisper: “There they come!”

But it wasn’t them. Only Mr. Brown, the livery-stable keeper turned sexton, stepped out to survey the scene before ushering the procession forward. The door closed again. Then, slowly, majestically, it opened once more.

A murmur rose through the church: “The family!”

Mrs. Welland entered first, arm-in-arm with her eldest son. Her face was solemn, her plum satin dress with blue panels and ostrich plumes drawing admiration. But everyone strained to see who followed her.

Rumors had flown the day before: would Mrs. Manson Mingott appear, despite her frailty? She had sent her carpenter to measure the pew—could she fit? Could she even walk? The idea of her being wheeled in her massive Bath chair had horrified the family. Too wide to pass under the awning, too visible to the crowd outside—no, it couldn’t happen. And so, the plan was abandoned.

But still, some hoped. When Mrs. Lovell Mingott appeared instead—her cheeks flushed, her eyes glassy from effort—there was a moment of disappointment. Yet her black Chantilly over lilac satin, crowned with Parma violets, made a striking contrast to her mother-in-law’s colors.

Then came the next figure—gaunt, flamboyant, draped in stripes and fringes, floating like a ghost through the nave.

Archer froze.

It was Medora.

He hadn’t expected her. He’d assumed she was still in Washington with Madame Olenska. The sudden appearance of her aunt stirred something deep inside him—a coldness, a tightening in his chest.

For a moment, he forgot everything—the music, the flowers, the voices—straining to see who came behind her. But the procession ended there. The ushers slipped through the side doors, ready to guide the bridal party.

“Newland—she’s here!” the best man breathed.

Archer blinked. Time had stretched. The bride was halfway down the aisle, the Bishop and Rector standing at the altar, the Spohr symphony weaving delicate notes through the air. The scent of lilies, the sight of May’s tulle and orange blossoms drifting closer, the sound of the Rector’s voice—everything was familiar, yet now it felt alien.

“My God,” he thought. “Have I got the ring?”

Again, he reached into his pocket, fingers finding the cool metal.

Then May was beside him, glowing, radiant. Her presence warmed him through the numbness. He straightened, smiled.

“Dearly beloved, we are gathered together here…” the Rector began.

The ring was on her finger. The blessing was given. The bridesmaids poised to return. The organ began to swell toward the Mendelssohn March—the inevitable, unmissable song of every New York wedding.

“Your arm—give her your arm!” the best man hissed.

Archer snapped back into himself. Where had he been? Lost in thought, perhaps. Or in memory. Had he seen, among the crowd, a dark coil of hair beneath a hat—only to realize it belonged to a stranger, a woman with a long nose, laughably unlike the one whose image it had evoked? Was he losing his grip?

Now, he and May walked slowly down the nave, carried forward by the gentle rhythm of the music. Outside, spring beckoned. The doors stood wide open. Mrs. Welland’s chestnuts pranced at the end of the canvas tunnel, their white favours fluttering.

The footman wrapped May’s cloak around her. Archer jumped into the brougham beside her.

She turned to him, smiling triumphantly. Their hands clasped beneath her veil.

“Darling,” he said.

And suddenly, the abyss opened again—deep, dark, endless. He felt himself falling, sinking, while his voice continued smoothly: “Yes, of course I thought I’d lost the ring. No wedding is complete without that little scare. But you did keep me waiting, didn’t you? I had time to think of every horror that might happen.”

She surprised him. In the middle of Fifth Avenue, she turned and threw her arms around his neck.

“But none ever can happen now, can it, Newland? As long as we two are together?”

Everything had been planned perfectly. After the breakfast, they changed into their travelling clothes, descended the grand Mingott staircase between laughing bridesmaids and weeping parents, and climbed into the brougham beneath a shower of rice and satin slippers. They had time to drive to the station, buy the latest weeklies, and settle into their reserved compartment—where May’s maid had already placed her dove-coloured cloak and gleaming London dressing bag.

The du Lac aunts had offered their Rhinebeck house, a gesture welcomed by both couples. Archer was glad to avoid the usual hotel suite. May loved the idea of going to the country, delighted by the bridesmaids’ futile attempts to guess their destination.

It was considered very English to have a country home lent to you. And though no one knew exactly where they were going—not even the parents—the mystery added to the charm.

Once aboard, the train pulled out of the city, shaking off the endless suburbs and entering the pale green landscape of spring. Conversation flowed easier than Archer had expected.

May was still the same girl—eager, innocent, discussing the wedding like a curious observer. At first, he thought her calmness hid fear. But her eyes were clear, her expression tranquil. She was alone with her husband—but he was merely the charming companion of yesterday. There was no one she liked more, no one she trusted more. And the greatest joy of all? Being off with him, alone, like a grown-up, like a married woman.

It amazed him—how such depth of feeling could exist alongside such absence of imagination. He remembered how, even in St. Augustine, she had reverted to girlish simplicity once her conscience was clear. She lived fully in the moment, never looking ahead. Perhaps that was why her eyes were so transparent, her face so timeless—like a statue of civic virtue or a Greek goddess.

Her blood seemed to run close to her skin, not as a fiery force, but as a preserving fluid. Yet she looked neither hard nor dull—just pure, untouched, primitive.

In the midst of this reflection, he found himself staring at her—as if seeing her for the first time.

Then came the moment he’d known was coming.

“Do you know,” she said, “I was surprised—weren’t you—that Aunt Medora came after all? Ellen wrote that neither of them was well enough to travel. I do wish it had been she who recovered! Did you see the exquisite old lace she sent me?”

He had known this moment would arrive. But he’d hoped, by sheer will, to delay it.

“Yes—yes, it was beautiful,” he murmured, eyes unfocused, wondering if hearing those two words would always bring down the walls of his world.

“Aren’t you tired?” she asked, taking his hand. “It will be good to have tea when we arrive. I’m sure the aunts have everything ready.”

Her mind raced to the silver tea service from the Beauforts, which matched uncle Lovell’s trays perfectly.

At dusk, the train stopped at Rhinebeck. They walked to the carriage.

“Oh, how kind of the van der Luydens—they’ve sent their man from Skuytercliff to meet us,” Archer said, as a liveried servant approached.

“I’m extremely sorry, sir,” the man said, “but there’s been a little accident at the Miss du Lacs’. A leak in the water tank. Mr. van der Luyden heard this morning and sent a housemaid to prepare the Patroon’s house. It’ll be quite comfortable, I assure you. And the Miss du Lacs have sent their cook. Everything will be just as if you were at Rhinebeck.”

Archer stared, stunned.

“Exactly the same?” May chimed in, eagerly. “The Patroon’s house? Oh, it will be a hundred thousand times better—won’t it, Newland? How dear of Mr. van der Luyden to think of it!”

As they drove off, the maid beside the coachman, their bridal bags gleaming on the seat, she continued: “Only imagine—I’ve never been inside it. Have you? The van der Luydens don’t show it to many. But Ellen saw it, and she says it’s the only house in America she could truly be happy in.”

“Well—then that’s what we’re going to be, isn’t it?” Archer said, trying to sound cheerful.

She smiled, her eyes bright. “Ah, it’s just our luck beginning—the wonderful luck we’re always going to have together!”

XX. "Of course we must dine with Mrs. Carfry, dearest," Archer said, his voice warm and certain as he leaned back in the breakfast chair. His wife looked up from her plate, her brow furrowed slightly beneath the soft glow of the London morning light filtering through the lace curtains. The Britannia ware on their lodging house table seemed to gleam like polished ivory, a small monument to order in a city that felt more like a damp, gray dream than home.

In all of autumnal London—rain-slicked, fog-wrapped, and strangely empty—the Newland Archers knew only two people. And they had made it a point, ever since their arrival, to avoid them entirely. It was an old New York rule: never force yourself upon acquaintances abroad. To do so would be undignified. And the Archers had followed this principle with quiet pride. During their travels, Mrs. Archer and Janey had moved through Europe like shadows—reserved, polite, almost invisible. They’d passed through cities teeming with Americans, yet spoken barely a word to any but hotel clerks or railway porters. Their own countrymen? Only those already known, or properly introduced. Otherwise, silence. A deliberate one. So complete, in fact, that they might have set a record for never speaking to a “foreigner” unless it was over a train ticket or a missing umbrella.

But even the most careful plans can falter.

It happened at Botzen, in the mountains. One night, a knock came at their door—an English woman from the room across the hall, her voice hushed with concern. She asked if Mrs. Archer had a bottle of liniment. Her sister, Mrs. Carfry, had taken ill suddenly—bronchitis, sharp and fierce—and they were alone, far from help. Mrs. Archer, ever prepared, reached into her travel trunk and produced not just liniment, but a full array of remedies, ointments, and soothing teas. She sent her maid to assist with care, and soon the invalid was recovering under their quiet, attentive watch.

When the Archers left Botzen, they thought nothing more of it. To them, kindness was its own reward. But to Mrs. Carfry and her sister Miss Harle, the gesture had been monumental. They carried gratitude like a sacred flame. Wherever the Archers went—Paris, Geneva, Vienna—they found themselves unexpectedly greeted by the Carfrys, who seemed to know every route, every season, every moment when the American couple would cross paths. They tracked them down with astonishing precision, arriving at hotels just in time, sending notes, offering tea, sharing ferns in glass cases, discussing the latest books, the state of the Church, the proper way to make macramé.

The friendship grew, unbidden but unshakable. In London, Brown’s Hotel became a place of warmth. When the Archers arrived, Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle were already waiting—like sisters, like cousins, like friends who had known them forever. As Mrs. Archer once remarked, “It makes another thing of London.” By the time Newland proposed, the bond between the families was strong enough that a wedding invitation felt natural—almost necessary. The Carfrys sent a bouquet of pressed Alpine flowers, sealed in glass, a delicate tribute to the joy they shared.

And now, standing on the dock before their departure, Mrs. Archer had whispered one final instruction: “You must take May to see Mrs. Carfry.”

Newland and May had no intention of doing so. But fate—or perhaps Mrs. Carfry’s uncanny instinct—had other plans. An invitation arrived, formal and insistent. And now, sitting across from each other at breakfast, May frowned, her fingers curling around her teacup.

“It’s all very well for you, Newland,” she said, “you *know* them. But I don’t. I’ll feel so awkward among strangers. And what shall I wear?”

Archer smiled, leaning back. She looked radiant—more so than ever. The English air had softened her features, deepened the pink in her cheeks. Or perhaps it was simply the glow of happiness, like sunlight beneath ice. He couldn’t tell which.

“Wear?” he repeated gently. “I thought your Paris trunk arrived last week.”

“Yes, of course,” she said, frowning. “But I mean—*which* dress? I’ve never dined out in London. I don’t want to look ridiculous.”

He tried to understand her anxiety. “Don’t Englishwomen dress like everyone else in the evening?”

May blinked. “Newland! How can you say such a thing? They go to the theatre in old ball-dresses and bare heads!”

“Well, perhaps they wear new ones at home,” he offered. “But Mrs. Carfry and Miss Harle won-’t. They’ll wear caps like my mother’s—and shawls. Very soft shawls.”

“Yes, but what about the others?” she insisted.

“Not as well as you, dear,” he replied, half-joking. Then, realizing how much she truly cared, he added, “Why not wear your wedding dress? That can’t be wrong.”

“Oh, dearest!” she sighed, eyes brightening. “If only it were here! But it’s gone to Paris to be remade for next winter. Worth hasn’t sent it back yet.”

Archer stood up, brushing off his coat. “Well then,” he said, “the fog’s lifting. Let’s skip dinner and dash to the National Gallery. We might catch a glimpse of the pictures before they close.”

They walked through the misty streets, arm in arm, heading toward South Kensington. The past three months had been a kind of dream—a wedding tour that May described vaguely in letters to her friends as “blissful.” They hadn’t gone to the Italian Lakes, as she’d once suggested. Archer had imagined her there too vividly—too serene, too still—and decided against it. Instead, they’d spent July in the Swiss Alps, hiking and breathing mountain air, then August along the Normandy coast, at Etretat, a quiet little town praised for its charm.

Once, in the high meadows near Grindelwald, Archer had pointed southward. “There’s Italy,” he said.

May, her feet nestled in a bed of gentian, had smiled. “It would be lovely to go there next winter—if only you didn’t have to be in New York.”

But truthfully, traveling held less interest for her than he’d expected. Once her wardrobe was settled, it was merely an excuse for walking, riding, swimming, and playing lawn tennis—something she found thrillingly new. And now, back in London, where he was to order his clothes, she could barely conceal her eagerness to return home. The theatres? Less exciting than the Paris cafés chantants, where she’d watched “cocottes” from a terrace beneath blossoming horse-chestnuts, while her husband translated songs just loud enough for bridal ears.

Archer, meanwhile, was beginning to fall back into old patterns. Marriage, he realized, was easier when you followed tradition. Treating May like all the other wives he knew—dutiful, graceful, untouched by doubt—was simpler than trying to live up to the romantic ideals he’d once flirted with as a bachelor. He saw no point in freeing a woman who didn’t know she was bound. And May, in her innocence, gave him everything—her loyalty, her admiration, her quiet strength. She didn’t demand freedom; she offered it freely, placing it at his feet without complaint. And because she did, he felt bound to honor her trust.

Her simplicity wasn’t petty—it was pure. Like the lines of her face, clean and fine. She became, in a way, the guardian of his old beliefs, the keeper of the values he’d once thought outdated. She wouldn’t challenge him, not unless something truly outrageous occurred. And the thought of doing anything to break her trust was unthinkable.

So he returned to his old ways—not out of indifference, but out of love. He knew their life would unfold naturally—his intellectual world outside the home, hers within. And when children came, the corners of their lives would fill. He saw it clearly now, as they rode through London in the hansom, her sky-blue cloak edged with swansdown glowing like a vision against the grime.

“Probably nobody will be at Mrs. Carfry’s,” he said lightly. “London’s a desert this time of year. And you’re far too beautiful to risk being seen.”

“I don’t want them to think we dress like savages,” she replied, her tone firm. And he smiled, struck again by the reverence American women held for dress—not just fashion, but armor. A shield against the unknown. A declaration of belonging.

And he understood, finally, why she had taken such care in choosing every gown, every ribbon, every accessory. Not for him—but for herself. For the world.

At Mrs. Carfry’s, the drawing-room was long and chilly, lit by dim lamps. There were only five guests: the hostess, her sister, a vicar with a kind face, a quiet young man named Charles, and a small dark gentleman whom Mrs. Carfry introduced as his tutor—M. Rivière, she said, pronouncing the name with a French lilt.

Into this subdued gathering, May stepped like a swan touched by sunset. She seemed larger, fairer, more luminous than ever. And Archer saw, in her wide, anxious eyes, a child’s fear—of saying the wrong thing, of being misunderstood.

“What on earth will they expect me to talk about?” she whispered, pleading silently.

But beauty, even uncertain beauty, inspires confidence. The vicar and M. Rivière both turned to her, eager to put her at ease. Yet the dinner itself remained quiet, stilted. May, unsure how to engage, clung to familiar ground—American customs, New York society, the names of family friends. Her conversation, though charming, was cool. The vicar drifted away after a glass of port. The nephew, pale and frail, was sent to bed. Only Archer and M. Rivière remained, sipping wine in the quiet aftermath.

And then, slowly, the conversation changed.

M. Rivière spoke of the boy’s illness—consumption, they feared—how he’d left Harrow for Switzerland, spent two years by Lake Leman, and now returned to England under the care of his tutor. He spoke with calm honesty of his own life: a diplomat’s son, meant for the Foreign Office, but drawn instead to literature. He’d lived in Paris, frequented the Goncourt circle, been advised by Maupassant not to write—*a great honor*, he said with a smile—and talked often with Mérimée in his mother’s home.

He was thirty, thin-faced, not handsome, but alive with thought. His eyes sparkled as he spoke of ideas, of books, of conversation as the true air of life. He’d abandoned journalism, authorship, even diplomacy, not for failure—but for freedom. “To keep one’s intellectual liberty,” he said, “is worth everything. To preserve one’s _quant à soi_, one’s self, intact.”

Archer listened, stunned. Here was a man who had lived richly in poverty, who had chosen the garret over the salon, the mind over money. And yet, he confessed, he sometimes dreamed of escape—of a chance in America. “Do you suppose,” he asked, “there might be a place for me in New York?”

Archer stared. New York? For a man who had danced with Flaubert’s circle, who valued conversation above comfort?
