“New York—must it be New York?” he stammered, unable to imagine what opportunity could await such a man in his native city.

A flush rose on M. Rivière’s pale skin. “I thought it your metropolis,” he said quickly. “Is not the intellectual life more active there?” Then, as if fearing he’d asked too much, he added, “One throws out random thoughts—more to oneself than to others. In reality, I see no immediate prospect—”

And rising, he said with ease, “But Mrs. Carfry will think I should take you upstairs.”

On the ride home, Archer sat in silence, thinking. The hour with M. Rivière had stirred something deep within him—old dreams, forgotten passions. He’d wanted to invite him to dinner the next day. But then May spoke.

“The little Frenchman?” she said, yawning slightly. “Wasn’t he dreadfully common?”

Archer paused. “Common—where?”

“Anywhere but in his schoolroom,” she replied, then added with a smile, “Though I suppose I wouldn’t know if he was clever.”

He disliked the word “clever.” He disliked the word “common.” But he also began to understand—this was her world. Her standards. Her inheritance.

“Ah,” he said, laughing, “then I won’t ask him to dine.”

She blinked. “Goodness—ask the Carfrys’ tutor?”

“Well, not on the same day,” he said. “But I did want another talk with him. He’s looking for a job in New York.”

Her surprise grew. “A job in New York? What sort? People don’t have French tutors. What does he want to do?”

“Chiefly,” her husband replied, “to enjoy good conversation.”

She laughed, delighted. “Oh, Newland, how funny! Isn’t that *French*?”

And so it was settled. He was glad she refused. Another talk would have forced the question of New York—of what kind of life M. Rivière could possibly lead there. And the more Archer thought, the more impossible it seemed.

He walked beside her into the house, the hansom clattering behind. The first six months of marriage, he told himself, were always the hardest. After that, they’d smooth each other’s edges.

But the worst part was this—he could already feel her gentle pressure, shaping him, softening him, eroding the very angles he most wanted to keep.

The sunlit lawn stretched out like a ribbon of emerald, leading directly to the shimmering expanse of the sea. Along its edges, scarlet geraniums and deep-red coleus formed a vivid border, while cast-iron vases painted in rich chocolate brown stood at intervals along the winding path—each overflowing with trailing petunias and ivy geraniums that spilled over the neatly raked gravel like living lace.

Halfway between the cliff’s edge and the square wooden house—also chocolate-colored, but with a verandah roof striped in yellow and brown to mimic an awning—two large archery targets were set against a backdrop of dense shrubbery. Opposite them, facing the mark, was a real canvas tent, surrounded by garden benches and chairs. A gathering of ladies in light summer dresses and gentlemen in grey frock coats and tall hats stood or sat on the lawn, chatting softly. Every few moments, a slender girl in crisp white muslin would emerge from the tent, bow in hand, and launch her arrow toward the target, drawing quiet attention from the crowd.

Newland Archer stood on the verandah of the house, watching the scene unfold below. On either side of the polished steps, bright yellow stands held large blue porcelain flowerpots, each filled with a spiky green plant. Beneath the verandah, a wide border of blue hydrangeas edged with red geraniums ran along the ground. Behind him, through the French windows he had just passed, lace curtains swayed gently, framing glimpses of glass parquet floors dotted with chintz poufs, tiny armchairs, and velvet tables adorned with silver trinkets.

The Newport Archery Club always hosted its August meeting at the Beauforts’. Though once rivaling croquet as the season’s favorite pastime, archery was now slowly giving way to lawn tennis. Yet tennis, still considered too rough and informal for refined society, left archery as the preferred spectacle—one where elegance could be displayed through graceful posture and fashionable attire.

Archer gazed down at the familiar ritual with a sense of detachment. It amazed him how life carried on as if nothing had changed, while inside himself, everything had shifted. Newport had been the first place to make that clear. In New York, during the winter, after he and May had settled into their new greenish-yellow home with its bow window and Pompeian vestibule, he’d slipped back into his old routine—the office, the daily work—at first with relief. That steady rhythm had felt like a lifeline to the man he used to be.

Then came the excitement of selecting a striking grey carriage horse for May’s brougham (a gift from the Wellands), and the satisfaction of designing his library exactly as he’d imagined: dark embossed wallpaper, Eastlake bookcases, and "sincere" furniture. At the Century Club, he’d found Winsett again; at the Knickerbocker, the young men of his circle. Between law, dinners, home entertaining, and occasional evenings at the opera or theater, his life seemed solid, predictable—even real.

But Newport offered escape from duty, a world entirely devoted to leisure. He’d tried to convince May they should spend the summer on Mount Desert Island—a remote stretch off the Maine coast, where hardy Bostonians and Philadelphians lived in “native” cottages amid woods and water, far from polite society. But the Wellands always went to Newport, and as their son-in-law, he had no good reason to resist. Mrs. Welland had pointed out, with mild sharpness, that it wouldn’t do for May to wear herself out trying on clothes in Paris only to never wear them. And Archer had no rebuttal.

May couldn’t understand his reluctance. She reminded him that he’d always loved Newport in his bachelor days—and since that was true, he could only say he expected to enjoy it more now, being there with her. But standing on the Beaufort verandah, looking out at the animated lawn, he felt a sudden chill. He realized, with quiet dread, that he wouldn’t enjoy it at all.

It wasn’t May’s fault. Poor dear. When they traveled, they sometimes drifted slightly apart—but returning to familiar surroundings always brought them back into harmony. He’d known she wouldn’t disappoint him. And she hadn’t. He’d married her, like so many young men did, because she was charming, and because he’d reached the end of aimless romantic adventures, weary of fleeting passions. She represented peace, stability, companionship—the comfort of knowing one’s responsibilities.

He couldn’t say he’d made a mistake. She had fulfilled every expectation. To be married to one of New York’s most admired and beloved young women—graceful, sweet-tempered, reasonable—was undeniably rewarding. And Archer had never been blind to such advantages. As for the brief madness that had seized him before the wedding—the thought of marrying the Countess Olenska—he’d trained himself to see it as a relic of youth, a discarded dream. The idea that he might ever, in his right mind, have considered such a union now seemed absurd. She remained, in memory, not a woman, but a ghost—poignant, melancholy, haunting.

Yet these thoughts, these dismissals, left his mind hollow, echoing. And perhaps that was why the cheerful, bustling people on the lawn unsettled him—as if children were playing in a graveyard.

A rustle of silk drew his attention. The Marchioness Manson fluttered out of the drawing-room window, resplendent as ever. Her limp Leghorn hat was anchored by layers of faded gauze, and a tiny black velvet parasol, balanced absurdly on a carved ivory handle, rested over the brim.

"My dear Newland," she trilled, "I had no idea you and May had arrived! You came only yesterday, you say? Ah, business—professional duties—I understand. Many husbands find it impossible to join their wives here except for the weekend." She tilted her head, squinting at him with theatrical languor. "But marriage is one long sacrifice, as I often told my Ellen—"

Archer’s heart gave that same strange lurch it had once before, as if a door had slammed shut between him and the world. But it passed quickly. He heard Medora answer a question he must have asked aloud.

"No, I’m not staying here," she said. "I’m with the Blenkers, in their delightful solitude at Portsmouth. Beaufort kindly sent his famous trotters this morning, so I could catch a glimpse of Regina’s garden party. But tonight I return to country life. The Blenkers—those original souls—have rented an old farmhouse where they gather representative people..." She lowered her head beneath her wide brim, blushing faintly. "This week Dr. Agathon Carver is holding Inner Thought meetings there. A stark contrast to this worldly amusement—but then, I’ve always lived on contrasts! To me, the only death is monotony. I always tell Ellen: Beware of monotony—it’s the mother of all deadly sins. But my poor child is going through a phase of exaltation, of disdain for the world. You know, I suppose, she’s declined all invitations to stay in Newport—even with her grandmother Mingott? I barely managed to persuade her to come with me to the Blenkers’! The life she leads is morbid, unnatural. Ah, if only she’d listened when the door was still open... But shall we go down and watch this match? I hear your May is competing."

Beaufort approached from the tent, striding across the lawn—tall, heavy, tightly buttoned into a London frock coat, with one of his own orchids pinned to the lapel. Archer, who hadn’t seen him in months, noticed the change. In the summer heat, Beaufort’s complexion looked swollen, bloated. Without his proud bearing, he might have seemed merely fat and overdressed.

Rumors about Beaufort circulated freely. Earlier that year, he’d sailed to the West Indies on his new steam yacht—built in the Clyde, equipped with tiled bathrooms and other luxuries—said to have cost half a million. Upon his return, he’d presented his wife with a magnificent pearl necklace, the kind of grand gesture meant to silence scandal. His fortune was strong enough to absorb such extravagances, yet whispers persisted—not only in Fifth Avenue, but in Wall Street. Some said he’d lost money in railroads; others claimed he was being drained by a particularly insatiable woman. Each rumor of financial trouble was met with another act of excess: a new row of orchid houses, a fresh string of racehorses, or a new Meissonnier or Cabanel added to his gallery.

Now he stepped forward with his usual half-sneering smile. "Hullo, Medora! Did the trotters do their job? Forty minutes, eh? Not bad, considering your nerves needed protecting." He shook hands with Archer, then turned and placed himself beside Mrs. Manson, murmuring something low and private that she didn’t catch.

She responded with one of her peculiar foreign gestures and a "_Que voulez-vous?_" which deepened Beaufort’s frown. But he managed a courteous smile as he glanced at Archer and said, "You know May’s going to win the prize."

"Ah, then it remains in the family," Medora chimed, and at that moment they reached the tent, where Mrs. Beaufort greeted them in a cloud of mauve muslin and floating veils.

May Welland was just stepping out. Dressed in white, with a pale green ribbon at her waist and a wreath of ivy on her hat, she had the same ethereal grace she’d shown on the night of her engagement. In the time since, not a single thought seemed to have stirred behind her eyes, not a feeling crossed her heart. Though Archer knew she was capable of both, he marveled anew at how effortlessly experience slipped away from her.

She held bow and arrow. Stepping onto the chalk line, she raised the bow to her shoulder and took aim. The pose was so perfectly classical that a murmur rose among the onlookers. Archer felt a familiar warmth—the pride of ownership—that often tricked him into believing he was content.

Her rivals—Mrs. Reggie Chivers, the Merry girls, various Thorleys, Dagonets, and Mingotts—stood behind her, a lovely cluster of anxious beauty, golden hair and rosy cheeks mingling with pale muslins and floral hats like a soft rainbow. All were young and lovely, glowing in the summer light. But none possessed the effortless elegance of his wife, whose muscles tensed, brow furrowed, as she focused her entire being on the task.

"Gad," Lawrence Lefferts muttered, "not one of them holds the bow like she does."

Beaufort replied, "Yes, but that’s the only kind of target she’ll ever hit."

Archer felt a surge of irrational anger. A husband should want to hear such praise. That a coarse man saw her as lacking appeal only proved her refinement. Yet the words sent a shiver through him. What if such perfection were simply emptiness? What if “niceness,” taken to its highest degree, was merely a veil over nothing?

As May returned, flushed but calm, after hitting the bull’s-eye, he realized he had never truly lifted that curtain.

She accepted congratulations with her usual simplicity—the kind that made jealousy impossible. No one could resent her triumph, because she seemed equally serene whether she won or lost. But when her eyes met his, her face lit up with the pleasure of seeing his joy.

Mrs. Welland’s pony carriage waited. They drove off among the dispersing vehicles, May guiding the reins, Archer seated beside her.

The afternoon sun still lingered on the lawns and hedges. Along Bellevue Avenue, a double line of victorias, dog-carts, landaus, and vis-à-vis carriages rolled past—well-dressed people returning from the garden party or heading home after their daily drive along the Ocean Drive.

"Shall we visit Granny?" May suddenly asked. "I’d like to tell her myself I won the prize. There’s plenty of time before dinner."

Archer agreed, and she turned the ponies down Narragansett Avenue, across Spring Street, and out toward the rocky moorland beyond.

In this less fashionable district, Catherine the Great—always independent and frugal—had built herself a many-peaked, cross-beamed *cottage-orné* on cheap land overlooking the bay. Here, among stunted oaks, her verandahs spread above island-studded waters. A winding drive led up between iron stags and blue glass balls embedded in mounds of geraniums to a front door of highly varnished walnut under a striped roof. Inside, a narrow hall with a black-and-yellow star-patterned floor opened into four small rooms with heavy flock-paper walls and ceilings painted by an Italian artist with gods and goddesses of Olympus.

One room had become a bedroom when Mrs. Mingott’s health declined. The adjacent one was where she spent her days—seated in a large armchair between open door and window, perpetually waving a palm-leaf fan, its motion so distant from her body that it stirred only the fringe of anti-macassars on the chair arms.

Since she had helped bring about his marriage, Mrs. Mingott had treated Archer with warm cordiality. She believed his haste stemmed from uncontrollable passion—and since she admired impulsiveness (so long as it didn’t cost money), she always welcomed him with a twinkle and playful innuendos that May, fortunately, never caught.

Now she examined the diamond-tipped arrow pinned to May’s dress, remarking that in her day a filigree brooch would have sufficed, but that Beaufort certainly did things with style.

"Quite an heirloom, really, my dear," she chuckled. "You must leave it to your eldest daughter." She pinched May’s arm, watching the color rise. "Well, well—what have I said to make you flush like that? No daughters? Only boys? Good heavens—look at her blushing again! What—can’t I say that either? Mercy me—when my children beg me to paint out those gods and goddesses overhead, I always say I’m too grateful to have someone around whom *nothing* can shock!"

Archer laughed, and May joined in, crimson to the eyes.

"Now, tell me all about the party," the old lady continued. "I’ll never get a straight word from that silly Medora." When May exclaimed, "Cousin Medora? But I thought she was going back to Portsmouth?" Mrs. Mingott answered calmly, "So she is—but she has to stop here first to pick up Ellen. Ah—you didn’t know Ellen came to spend the day with me? Such nonsense, her refusing to come for the summer. But I gave up arguing with young people fifty years ago. Ellen—_Ellen_!" she called sharply, trying to lean forward to see the lawn beyond the verandah.

No answer. Mrs. Mingott tapped impatiently with her cane on the shiny floor. A mulatto maid in a bright turban reported that she’d seen "Miss Ellen" walking down the path toward the shore.

"Run down and fetch her," Mrs. Mingott said to Archer. "This pretty lady will describe the party to me."

And Archer stood up—as if waking from a dream.

He’d heard the name Countess Olenska often since their last meeting. He knew the details of her life: how she’d spent the previous summer in Newport, entered society, then abruptly sublet her perfect house and moved to Washington. There, during the winter, he’d heard of her—like any pretty woman in Washington—shining in diplomatic circles, praised for her charm and intelligence. He’d listened to reports of her appearance, conversation, tastes—with the detached interest one gives to a story of someone long gone.

Not until Medora mentioned her name at the archery match had Ellen Olenska become real again. The Marchioness’s careless lisp had conjured up the little fire-lit drawing-room, the sound of wheels retreating down a deserted street. He remembered a tale of Tuscan peasants lighting straw in a cave, revealing ancient images in their tombs...

The path to the shore descended from the bank to a walk above the water lined with weeping willows. Through their branches, Archer saw the glint of Lime Rock, its whitewashed turret and tiny house where Ida Lewis, the heroic lighthouse keeper, lived her final years. Beyond lay Goat Island, the bay stretching northward in golden haze to Prudence Island and the faint shores of Conanicut.

From the willow path projected a small wooden pier ending in a pagoda-like summerhouse. And there, leaning against the railing, her back to the shore, stood a woman.

Archer stopped as if struck awake. That vision was a dream. The reality was waiting above: Mrs. Welland’s pony carriage circling the oval, May under the Olympian gods, glowing with quiet hope; the Welland villa at the end of Bellevue Avenue; Mr. Welland already dressed, pacing the drawing-room, watch in hand, with dyspeptic impatience—because in that house, one always knew what was happening at any given hour.

"What am I?" Archer thought. "A son-in-law?"

The figure at the end of the pier hadn’t moved. For a long moment, he stood halfway down the hill, watching the bay, crisscrossed by sailboats, yachts, fishing boats, and coal barges pulled by noisy tugs. The woman in the summerhouse seemed fixed by the same view. Beyond Fort Adams, the sunset split into a thousand flames. A catboat’s sail caught the light as it beat out through the channel between Lime Rock and the shore.

Archer remembered the scene in *The Shaughraun*, Montague lifting Ada Dyas’s ribbon to his lips without her knowing he was in the room.

"She doesn’t know—she hasn’t guessed. Would I know if she came up behind me?" he wondered. Then, suddenly: "If she doesn’t turn before that sail crosses the Lime Rock light, I’ll go back."

The boat glided out on the receding tide. It passed Lime Rock, obscured Ida Lewis’s house, crossed the turret where the light hung. Archer waited until a wide space of sparkling water lay between the island and the boat’s stern. Still, the figure did not move.

He turned and walked up the hill.

"I'm sorry you didn't find Ellen," May said as they drove home through the dusk. "I’d liked to see her again. But perhaps she wouldn’t have cared—she seems so changed."

"Changed?" Archer echoed, voice flat, eyes fixed on the ponies’ twitching ears.

"So indifferent to her friends," May said. "Giving up New York, her house—spending her time with such odd people. How uncomfortable she must be at the Blenkers’! She says she does it to keep cousin Medora out of trouble—to prevent her marrying dreadful men. But sometimes I think we’ve always bored her."

Archer said nothing.

She continued, with a note of hardness he’d never heard before: "After all, I wonder if she wouldn’t be happier with her husband."

He burst out laughing. "_Sancta simplicitas!_" he exclaimed. Seeing her puzzled frown, he added, "I don’t think I ever heard you say anything cruel before."

"Cruel?"

"Well—watching the contortions of the damned is supposed to be a favorite sport of angels. But even they don’t believe people are happier in hell."

"It’s a pity she ever married abroad then," May said, in the calm tone her mother used when addressing Mr. Welland’s quirks. And Archer felt quietly dismissed—as an unreasonable husband.

They drove down Bellevue Avenue and turned in between the chamfered wooden gateposts topped with iron lamps marking the approach to the Welland villa. Lights glowed through the windows. As the carriage stopped, Archer caught a glimpse of his father-in-law—exactly as he’d imagined him—pacing the drawing-room, watch in hand, wearing the expression of pained impatience he’d long since learned was more effective than anger.

XXII. "A party for the Blenkers—the Blenkers?" Mr. Welland set down his knife and fork, his eyes widening with disbelief as he stared across the luncheon table at his wife. She adjusted her gold-rimmed spectacles and read aloud in a voice laced with theatrical irony: "Professor and Mrs. Emerson Sillerton request the pleasure of Mr. and Mrs. Welland’s company at the meeting of the Wednesday Afternoon Club on August 25th at 3 o’clock punctually. To meet Mrs. and the Misses Blenker. Red Gables, Catherine Street. R.S.V.P."

"Good heavens—" Mr. Welland gasped, as if the absurdity of it required a second glance to fully register.

"Poor Amy Sillerton," Mrs. Welland sighed, lowering her voice. "You never know what her husband will do next. I suppose he's just discovered the Blenkers."

Professor Emerson Sillerton was a persistent thorn in the side of Newport society—unyielding, unremovable, rooted in a family tree both venerable and respected. He had every advantage: a father who was Sillerton Jackson’s uncle, a mother from Boston’s Pennilow lineage, wealth, breeding, and social standing. Yet nothing—nothing at all—compelled him to become an archaeologist, or even a professor, or to winter in Newport, or to host gatherings that flouted tradition. But if he must break from custom, why, then, had he married Amy Dagonet? A woman who deserved more than eccentricity, who had funds enough to maintain her own carriage, who ought to have been spared such a life.

No one among the Mingott circle could fathom how Amy had endured so quietly—a house filled with long-haired scholars and short-haired intellectuals, trips to Yucatan tombs instead of Parisian salons. Still, they were fixed in their ways, unaware of their strangeness. And each year, thanks to the Sillerton-Pennilow-Dagonet connection, every family along the Cliffs drew lots to send a reluctant representative to their dreary garden parties.

"It’s a wonder," Mrs. Welland mused, "that they didn’t pick Cup Race day! Do you remember two years ago, when they hosted a party for a Black man on Julia Mingott’s *thé dansant*? Luckily this time there’s nothing else scheduled—at least, some of us will have to go."

Mr. Welland exhaled heavily. "'Some of us,' my dear—more than one? Three o'clock is terribly inconvenient. I must be back by half-past three for my drops. Bencomb’s treatment requires consistency; otherwise, it’s useless. And if I join you later, I’ll miss my drive." He laid his cutlery down again, a flush creeping up his finely lined cheek.

"There’s no reason for you to go at all," Mrs. Welland said cheerfully, though the tone had grown automatic over years of managing expectations. "I need to leave cards at the other end of Bellevue Avenue. I’ll drop in around half-past three and stay long enough to show poor Amy we haven’t slighted her." She glanced hesitantly toward her daughter. "And if Newland has plans, perhaps May can take you out with the ponies—try their new russet harness?"

It was a sacred principle in the Welland household that every hour should be “provided for.” The very idea of idle time—especially for those who disliked whist or solitaire—was a horror to Mrs. Welland, like the specter of unemployment haunting a philanthropist. Another tenet: parents must never visibly interfere in their married children’s lives. Balancing these ideals meant constant ingenuity, leaving not a moment of her own time unaccounted for.

"Of course I’ll go with Papa," May said gently, reminding her husband of his lack of planning. "Newland will surely find something to do."

It pained Mrs. Welland that her son-in-law showed so little foresight. Already during the fortnight he’d spent under her roof, whenever she asked what he planned for the afternoon, he’d replied with a peculiar nonchalance: “Oh, I think I’ll just save it this time,” or once, after she and May had gone on overdue calls, confessed he’d lain all afternoon beneath a rock on the beach below the house.

“Newland never seems to look ahead,” she once whispered to May.

“No,” May answered serenely. “But you see, it doesn’t matter. When there’s nothing particular to do, he reads.”

“Ah, yes—like his father!” Mrs. Welland conceded, as if accepting a familial quirk. From then on, the subject of Newland’s idle hours was dropped.

Yet as the date of the Sillerton gathering neared, May began to show quiet concern for her husband’s comfort. She suggested a tennis match at the Chiverses’ or a sail on Julius Beaufort’s cutter—anything to make up for her temporary absence. “I’ll be back by six, dear. Papa never drives later than that—” and she wasn’t truly reassured until Archer mentioned hiring a run-about to drive up the island and visit a stud-farm to look at a second horse for her brougham.

They’d been searching for this horse for weeks. The suggestion pleased May so much she glanced at her mother as if to say: *See? He knows how to plan his days like any of us.*

The idea had formed in Archer’s mind the moment the Sillerton invitation surfaced. He’d kept it to himself, as if the plan carried a secret urgency. But he’d already arranged the run-about with a pair of old livery-stable trotters still capable of eighteen miles on flat roads. At two o’clock, he abandoned the luncheon table abruptly and leapt into the light carriage.

The day was perfect. A northern breeze pushed soft clouds across a sky of deep blue, with sunlight glinting off the sea. Bellevue Avenue lay empty at that hour. After dropping the stable boy at Mill Street, Archer turned onto the Old Beach Road and drove across Eastman’s Beach.

He felt the familiar thrill of half-holidays at school—the anticipation of going somewhere unknown. Keeping the horses at a steady pace, he aimed to reach the stud-farm beyond Paradise Rocks before three, so he might examine the horse—and test it if promising—before having four golden hours left to fill.

As soon as he heard about the Sillerton party, he’d known the Marchioness Manson would likely come with the Blenkers. Perhaps Madame Olenska would spend the day with her grandmother again. At the very least, the Blenker house would probably be empty—offering him a chance, without impropriety, to satisfy a strange curiosity.

He wasn’t certain he wanted to see the Countess Olenska again. But ever since he’d glimpsed her from the path above the bay, he’d felt an irrational, indefinable longing—to see the place she lived, to trace the imagined path of her figure through the summer-house, just as he’d watched her real self move beneath the trees.

The desire was constant, restless, like a sick man’s sudden craving for a forgotten taste. He couldn’t imagine what it might lead to. He didn’t wish to speak to her, nor hear her voice. He only knew that if he could carry away the image of the land she walked upon—the way the sea and sky framed it—the rest of the world might feel less hollow.

When he arrived at the stud-farm, a quick glance told him the horse wasn’t right. Still, he took a turn behind it, pretending patience. By three o’clock, he snapped the reins and turned toward Portsmouth.

The wind had died. A faint haze on the horizon signaled fog waiting to creep up the Saconnet with the tide. But around him, fields and woods shimmered in golden light. He passed grey-shingled farmhouses nestled in orchards, hayfields, groves of oak, villages with white steeples piercing the fading sky. Finally, after asking directions from men working in a field, he turned down a narrow lane flanked by high banks of goldenrod and brambles.

At the end, the river sparkled blue. To the left, amid a cluster of oaks and maples, stood a long, dilapidated house—white paint peeling from its clapboards. On the roadside, facing the gate, stood one of the open sheds where New Englanders stored tools and visitors tied their teams.

Archer jumped down, led the horses into the shed, and tied them to a post. Then he approached the house.

The lawn had reverted to a hayfield. To the left, an overgrown box-garden burst with dahlias and rust-colored roses, encircling a ghostly summer-house of weathered trellis, once white, crowned by a wooden Cupid missing his bow and arrow but still aiming uselessly.

Archer leaned against the gate. No one was visible. Not a sound came from the open windows. A grizzled Newfoundland slept before the door—just as ineffective a guardian as the arrowless Cupid.

It was odd to think this silent ruin belonged to the turbulent Blenkers. Yet he was certain he was right.

For a long while, he stood there, soaking in the scene, slowly surrendering to its quiet spell. Then, roused by the passage of time, he hesitated—should he linger, or go?

He suddenly wished to see inside the house—to picture the room where Madame Olenska sat. Nothing stopped him from walking up and ringing the bell. If she were away, as he suspected, he could introduce himself and ask permission to enter briefly to write a message.

Instead, he crossed the lawn and stepped into the box-garden.

As he entered, he caught sight of something bright in the summer-house. A pink parasol.

It pulled him like a magnet. It had to be hers.

He stepped inside, sat on the wobbly bench, picked up the silken umbrella, and studied its carved handle—made of rare wood that released a sweet, aromatic scent. He lifted it to his lips.

Then—rustling of skirts against the bushes.

He froze, hands clasped around the handle, eyes lowered, letting the sound draw closer.

He’d always known this might happen…

“Oh, Mr. Archer!” a loud, youthful voice exclaimed.

He looked up.

Before him stood the youngest and largest of the Blenker girls—blonde, rumpled, in bedraggled muslin. A red blotch on her cheek hinted she’d been sleeping. Her half-awake eyes blinked at him, hospitable but confused.

“Gracious—where did you drop from? I must’ve been fast asleep in the hammock. Everyone else went to Newport. Did you ring?”

Archer’s confusion matched hers.

“I—no—that is, I was about to. I came up the island to see about a horse, and I thought I’d stop by to see if Mrs. Blenker or your guests were here. But the house seemed empty—so I sat down to wait.”

Miss Blenker shook off sleep, her gaze sharpening. “The house *is* empty. Mother’s not here. Neither is the Marchioness. Just me.”

Her expression softened with mild reproach. “Didn’t you know Professor and Mrs. Sillerton are giving a garden party for us this afternoon? It’s too bad I couldn’t go—I’ve had a sore throat, and mother didn’t want me driving home tonight. Isn’t it dreadful? Of course,” she added brightly, “I wouldn’t have minded nearly as much if I’d known you were coming.”

A hint of clumsy flirtation stirred in her. Archer found strength to interrupt: “But Madame Olenska—has she gone to Newport too?”

Miss Blenker looked surprised. “Madame Olenska? Didn’t you know she’d been called away?”

“Called away?” he echoed.

“Oh, my best parasol! I lent it to Katie—because it matched her ribbons—and careless thing dropped it here. We Blenkers are all like that… real Bohemians!” She reclaimed the sunshade with a strong hand, unfurled it, and held it aloft like a banner.

“Yes, Ellen was called away yesterday. She lets us call her Ellen, you know. A telegram came from Boston—she said she might be gone two days. I *love* the way she does her hair, don’t you?”

Archer stared past her, as if she were transparent.

All he saw was the trumpery pink parasol shielding her giggling head.

After a pause, he ventured: “Do you happen to know why Madame Olenska went to Boston? I hope it wasn’t bad news?”

Miss Blenker dismissed the idea with cheerful disbelief. “Oh, I don’t believe so. She didn’t tell us what was in the telegram. I think she didn’t want the Marchioness to know. She’s so romantic-looking, isn’t she? Doesn’t she remind you of Mrs. Scott-Siddons reading ‘Lady Geraldine’s Courtship’? Have you ever heard her?”

Archer’s thoughts raced. His entire future unfolded before him—an endless emptiness, and within it, the shrinking figure of a man to whom nothing would ever happen.

He glanced around—overgrown garden, crumbling house, dusk gathering beneath the oaks.

It had seemed the perfect place to find her. But she was far away. And even the parasol wasn’t hers.

He frowned, hesitated.

“You don’t know, I suppose—well, I’m going to Boston tomorrow. If I could manage to see her…”

He sensed Miss Blenker losing interest, though her smile remained.

“Oh, of course! How lovely of you! She’s staying at the Parker House—it must be awful there in this weather.”

From then on, Archer barely registered their conversation. He only remembered stubbornly refusing her plea to wait for the family’s return and share tea.

Eventually, with his hostess still beside him, he stepped out from beneath the wooden Cupid, unhitched his horses, and drove off.

At the lane’s turn, he saw Miss Blenker standing at the gate, waving the pink parasol.

XXIII. The next morning, when Archer stepped off the Fall River train, he was met by a Boston sweltering under the weight of midsummer heat. The air near the station hung thick with the mingled scents of coffee, beer, and rotting fruit, while shirt-sleeved men and women moved through the streets like guests returning from an evening’s indulgence—unconstrained, unguarded, as if they’d just come from a shared bathhouse. He hailed a cab and rode to the Somerset Club for breakfast. Even in its most refined quarters, the city wore the look of domestic disorder that no amount of warmth could ever impose on European capitals. Caretakers in calico lounged on the doorsteps of grand homes, and the Common sprawled beneath the sun like a neglected garden after a festival. If Archer had tried to imagine Ellen Olenska in any improbable setting, this overheated, half-deserted Boston would have been the hardest to picture her in.

He ate his breakfast with quiet precision—first a slice of melon, then toast and scrambled eggs—while scanning the morning paper. A new energy hummed inside him, born from the decision he’d made the night before: to tell May he was going to Boston on business, take the Fall River boat that night, and return to New York the following evening. It had always been assumed he’d be back early in the week, so when he returned from Portsmouth and found a letter from the office waiting on the hall table—a letter fate seemed to have placed there deliberately—it gave his sudden departure all the justification it needed. He even felt a pang of shame at how easily it had all come together, a fleeting memory of Lawrence Lefferts’s deft maneuvers to free himself from obligation. But the thought didn’t linger; he wasn’t in the mood for analysis.

After breakfast, he lit a cigarette and glanced over the *Commercial Advertiser*. A few familiar faces drifted into the club, exchanging their usual greetings. It was still the same world, yet he felt as though he’d slipped through time and space, arriving in a place both familiar and utterly alien. He checked his watch—half past nine—and rose to go to the writing room. There, he scribbled a short note and sent a messenger with a cab to the Parker House, instructing him to wait for a reply. Then he sat again, newspaper in hand, trying to guess how long the ride might take.

“The lady was out, sir,” came a waiter’s voice beside him.

“Out?” he stammered, as if the word were foreign.

He stood up, heart pounding. It couldn’t be true—she wouldn’t be out at this hour. He flushed with irritation at his own delay. Why hadn’t he sent the note the moment he arrived? He grabbed his hat and stick and stepped back into the street. The city suddenly loomed vast and strange, as if he’d wandered into a distant land. For a moment he hesitated on the doorstep, then decided to walk to the Parker House. Maybe the messenger had been wrong—maybe she was still there.

He crossed the Common and saw her sitting on the first bench, beneath a tree. She held a grey silk parasol over her head—how could he ever have imagined her with a pink one? As he approached, he noticed her stillness, her posture relaxed as if she had nowhere else to be. Her profile drooped slightly, her hair tied low at the nape of her neck beneath a dark hat, her long glove wrinkled where it gripped the parasol. She turned as he neared.

“Oh,” she said.

For the first time, he saw surprise flash across her face—but only for a second. Then it softened into a slow, knowing smile. “Oh,” she murmured again, quieter now, as if she’d been waiting for him. Without rising, she shifted on the bench to make room.

“I’m here on business—just arrived,” he explained, though he didn’t know why he was pretending to be surprised to see her. “But what on earth are you doing in this wilderness?”

He didn’t really know what he was saying. It felt as if he were shouting across miles, afraid she might vanish again before he could reach her.

“I? Oh, I’m here on business too,” she replied, turning her head so they faced each other.

Her words barely registered. All he heard was her voice—the low timbre, the faint roughness on the consonants—something he’d never remembered, something that now struck him as entirely new.

“You do your hair differently,” he said, heart thudding as if he’d spoken something irreversible.

“Differently? No—only because I do it myself without Nastasia.”

“Nastasia? Isn’t she with you?”

“No. For two days, it wasn’t worth bringing her.”

“You’re alone—at the Parker House?”

She looked at him, a spark of old mischief in her eyes. “Does it strike you as dangerous?”

“No—not dangerous—”

“But unconventional? I suppose it is.” She paused. “I hadn’t thought of it, because I’ve just done something far more unconventional.”

A hint of irony lingered in her gaze. “I’ve just refused to take back money that was mine.”

Archer jumped up and took a step back. She had folded her parasol and was absently drawing patterns in the gravel.

He returned slowly, standing before her. “Someone came here to meet you?”

“Yes.”

“With this offer?”

She nodded.

“And you refused—because of the conditions?”

She waited a moment. “I refused.”

He sat beside her again. “What were the conditions?”

“Oh, nothing harsh—just to sit at the head of his table now and then.”

Silence fell between them. His heart had shut down, that peculiar way it did when he was close to her. He fumbled for words.

“He wants you back—at any price?”

“Well—yes. A considerable price. At least, it’s considerable for me.”

Another pause. He circled around the question he knew he had to ask. “Was it to meet him that you came here?”

She stared, then laughed. “Meet him—my husband? _Here_? At this season, he’s always at Cowes or Baden.”

“He sent someone?”

“Yes.”

“A letter?”

She shook her head. “No—just a message. He never writes. I don’t think I’ve received more than one letter from him.”

The mention of it brought color to her cheeks—and then to his. “Why does he never write?”

“Why should he? What are secretaries for?”

He blushed deeper. She said the word as if it carried no weight, no implication.

For a moment, he almost asked: “Did he send his secretary, then?” But the memory of Count Olenski’s single letter to his wife flashed through his mind, and he stopped.

Then he tried again. “And the person?”

“The emissary?” she replied, still smiling. “The emissary might, for all I care, have left already. But he insisted on waiting until this evening… in case… on the chance…”

“And you came out here to think about it?”

“I came out to breathe. The hotel is stifling. I’m taking the afternoon train back to Portsmouth.”

They sat in silence, staring ahead at the people passing along the path. Finally, she turned her eyes to his face. “You’re not changed.”

He wanted to say, “I was—until I saw you again.” Instead, he stood abruptly and looked around at the park, steam rising from the hot ground. “This is terrible. Why shouldn’t we go out on the bay? There’s a breeze—there’ll be cooler. We could take the steamboat down to Point Arley.”

She looked up at him, hesitant. He pressed on. “On a Monday morning, there won’t be anyone. My train doesn’t leave till evening—I’m going back to New York. Why shouldn’t we?”

He leaned down, urgent. “Haven’t we done all we could?”

“Oh,” she murmured.

She stood, reopened her parasol, and glanced around as if confirming the impossibility of staying. Then her eyes met his.

“You mustn’t say things like that to me.”

“I’ll say anything you want—or nothing. I won’t open my mouth unless you tell me to. What harm can it do? All I want is to listen to you,” he stammered.

She pulled out a small gold watch on an enamelled chain. “Oh, don’t calculate,” he broke in. “Give me the day! I want to get you away from that man. When is he coming?”

Her cheeks flushed. “At eleven.”

“Then you must come now.”

“You needn’t be afraid—if I don’t.”

“Nor you—if you do. I swear I only want to hear about you—to know what you’ve been doing. It’s been a hundred years since we met—maybe another hundred before we do again.”

She hesitated, her eyes searching his. “Why didn’t you come down to the beach to fetch me, the day I was at Granny’s?”

“Because you didn’t look round—because you didn’t know I was there. I swore I wouldn’t unless you looked.”

He laughed, realizing how childish it sounded.

“But I didn’t look on purpose.”

“On purpose?”

“I knew you were there. When you drove in, I recognized the ponies. So I went down to the beach.”

“To get away from me as far as you could?”

She repeated softly, “To get away from you as far as you could.”

He laughed again, this time with boyish satisfaction. “Well, you see it’s no use. I may as well tell you—the business I came here for was just to find you. But look—we must start, or we’ll miss our boat.”

“Our boat?”

She frowned, then smiled. “Oh, but I must go back to the hotel first—I must leave a note—”

“As many notes as you please. You can write here.” He pulled out a note-case and a stylographic pen. “I’ve even got an envelope—see how everything’s predestined! Here—steady the paper on your knee, and I’ll get the pen working.”

He tapped the pen against the bench. “It’s like jerking down mercury in a thermometer—just a trick. Now try—”

She laughed and bent over the sheet he’d laid on the case, beginning to write.

Archer walked a few steps away, staring blankly at the passersby, who in turn paused to stare at the sight of a well-dressed woman writing a note on her knee in the middle of the Common.

She slipped the letter into the envelope, wrote a name, and tucked it into her pocket. Then she stood.

They walked back toward Beacon Street, and near the club, Archer spotted the plush-lined cab that had delivered his note. Its driver sat cooling his brow at a hydrant.

“I told you everything was predestined!” Archer said. “Here’s a cab for us. See!”

They laughed, amazed at the miracle of finding a public conveyance at that hour, in such an unlikely place, in a city where cab-stands were still considered foreign.

Looking at his watch, Archer saw they had time to drive to the Parker House before heading to the steamboat landing. They rattled through the hot streets and stopped at the hotel.

He held out his hand for the letter. “Shall I take it in?”

She shook her head and stepped inside.

It was barely half-past ten. But what if the emissary, impatient and unaware of how to fill his time, had already settled among the travelers sipping drinks at the bar—those very travelers Archer had glimpsed as she entered?

He waited, pacing before the cab. A Sicilian youth with eyes like Nastasia’s offered to shine his boots; an Irish matron sold peaches. Every few moments, the doors opened, letting out men in straw hats, their faces weary, their movements sluggish. They all looked alike—like every other overheated traveler in America at that hour, passing endlessly in and out of hotel doors.

Then, suddenly, a face broke the pattern.

He caught only a flash—his pacing had carried him to the far end of his beat—and as he turned back, he saw it among the familiar types: the lank, the tired, the wide-eyed, the mild. This face was different—pale, yes, dimmed by heat or worry, but sharper, quicker, more aware. Or perhaps it seemed so because it stood apart.

Archer clung to a thread of memory—but it snapped, drifting away with the disappearing figure. Just a foreign businessman, looking doubly foreign in that setting.

He vanished into the crowd, and Archer resumed his patrol.

He didn’t want to be seen watching the clock. His own sense of time told him that if she delayed much longer, it could only mean she’d met the emissary and been detained.

At the thought, his anxiety tightened into anguish.

“If she doesn’t come soon, I’ll go in and find her,” he muttered.

The doors swung open again—and she was at his side.

They climbed into the cab. As it pulled away, he checked his watch: she had been gone exactly three minutes.

In the clatter of loose windows, talk was impossible. They bumped over cobblestones to the wharf.

Seated side by side on the nearly empty boat, they found themselves speaking little—what they had to say passed best in silence, in the relief of being alone, in the hush of escape.

As the paddle-wheels began to turn, and the docks and ships faded behind a veil of heat, Archer felt the old world slipping away. He longed to ask her if she felt the same—the sense of embarking on a journey from which they might never return.

But he dared not speak. Not a word that might shake the fragile trust she placed in him.

In truth, he didn’t want to betray it.

There had been nights when the memory of their kiss burned on his lips. The day before, even, on the drive to Portsmouth, her presence had coursed through him like fire.

But now, with her beside him, drifting into this unknown sea, they seemed to have reached a depth of closeness that no touch could disturb.

As the boat turned seaward, a breeze stirred, and the bay broke into long oily waves, then into ripples tipped with spray. The fog still clung to the city, but ahead lay fresh waters and distant headlands, lighthouses gleaming in the sun.

Madame Olenska leaned back against the rail, breathing in the cool air through parted lips. Her veil wound loosely around her hat, leaving her face uncovered. Archer was struck by the calm joy in her expression. She accepted their adventure as natural, neither fearful nor overly excited by the possibility of danger.

In the bare dining-room of the inn—where he’d hoped they’d have privacy—they found a loud group of young teachers on holiday. Archer’s spirits sank.

“This is hopeless—I’ll ask for a private room,” he said.

She made no objection as he went to find one.

The room opened onto a long wooden verandah, the sea visible through the windows. It was plain and cool, with a coarse checkered cloth on the table, a bottle of pickles, and a blueberry pie under a cage.

No more innocent-looking private room could have sheltered a clandestine couple. Archer thought he saw the comfort of it in the faint, amused smile on her face as she sat opposite him.

A woman who had fled her husband—and rumored to have done so with another man—would surely know how to take things for granted.

Yet there was something in her quiet composure that softened his irony. By being so calm, so unsurprised, so simple, she had brushed aside the rules and made it feel natural—natural for two old friends, with so much to say, to seek a moment of solitude.

XXIV. They lunched slowly, each bite a quiet ritual between them, punctuated by silences that stretched like shadows across the table. Once the first words had fallen, they found themselves with so much to say—and yet, at times, silence became its own kind of conversation, rich and unspoken. Archer avoided speaking of himself, not out of calculation, but because he could not bear to lose even a single sentence of her story. Leaning forward, chin cradled in her hands, she told him of the year and a half since their last meeting.

She had grown weary of what people called “society.” New York had welcomed her back with warmth—almost too much warmth. She would never forget how kindly it embraced her return. But after the initial thrill wore off, she realized she was too different, too aware, to care about the things others did. So she had decided to try Washington—where, she hoped, she might meet more kinds of people, more kinds of ideas. And perhaps, she said, she would settle there. It would be a home for poor Medora, who had exhausted the patience of every relative just when she needed protection most—especially from the dangers of marriage.

“But Dr. Carver—aren’t you afraid of him? I hear he’s been staying with you at the Blenkers’.”

She smiled gently. “Oh, the danger is past. Dr. Carver is clever—he wants a wealthy wife to fund his ambitions. Medora is simply good publicity as a convert.”

“To what?”

“To all sorts of new, strange social ideas. But honestly, I find them more interesting than the blind obedience to tradition—someone else’s tradition—that I see among our friends. It feels foolish to have discovered America only to turn it into a copy of another country.”

She looked across the table, her eyes soft with irony. “Do you suppose Christopher Columbus sailed all that way just to go to the opera with the Selfridge Merrys?”

Archer flushed. “And Beaufort—do you talk like this to him?” he asked sharply.

“I haven’t seen him in a long time. But I used to. He understands.”

Ah,” he said, “that’s always been my point—you don’t like us. You like Beaufort because he’s not like us.”

He glanced around the sparse room, then out to the bare beach and the row of white houses lining the shore. “We’re terribly dull. We lack character, colour, variety. I wonder…” he broke off, voice low, “…why you don’t just go back?”

Her eyes darkened, and he braced himself for anger. But she remained still, as if weighing his words. He feared she might agree. Then, quietly, she said: “I believe it’s because of you.”

It was said without emotion, without flattery—so plainly that it startled him. He turned red to the temples, but dared not move. Her words felt like a rare bird perched on the edge of silence—any motion might scare it away, but if left undisturbed, it might draw others.

“At least,” she continued, “you were the one who made me see that beneath the dullness, there are things so delicate, so fine, that even those I loved most in my old life seem cheap now. I can’t explain it properly”—she frowned, pressing her brows together—“but it’s as though I’ve only just learned how deeply the coarse and base can mix with the most exquisite pleasures.”

“Exquisite pleasures,” he thought—“it’s something to have known them!” But the look in her eyes held him mute.

“I want,” she went on, “to be honest—with you, and with myself. For a long time, I’ve hoped for this moment—to tell you how much you’ve changed me, what you’ve made of me…”

Archer sat frozen, brows furrowed. Then he laughed, a short, brittle sound. “And what do you think you’ve made of me?”

She paled slightly. “Of you?”

“Yes. Because I am far more your creation than you ever were mine. I’m the man who married one woman because another told him to.”

Her pallor deepened into a fleeting flush. “I thought… you promised… not to say such things today.”

“Oh,” he said, “how typical of a woman! None of you can ever finish a difficult thing properly.”

She lowered her voice. “Is it really a bad thing—for May?”

He stood by the window, fingers drumming against the raised sash, every nerve tingling with the tenderness in her tone when she spoke her cousin’s name.

“That’s exactly what we must always remember, isn’t it?” she pressed. “By your own account.”

“My own account?” he echoed, still staring at the sea.

“If not,” she continued, “if it’s not worth giving up, if it’s not worth missing things—just so others may avoid pain and disappointment—then everything I came back for, everything that made my old life feel empty and hollow because no one there valued these things… all of it becomes a lie or a dream.”

He turned, still rooted to his place. “Then,” he finished for her, “there’s no reason why you shouldn’t go back.”

Her eyes clung to him, desperate. “Oh, is there no reason at all?”

“None,” he said, “if you’ve staked everything on my marriage. My marriage,” he added fiercely, “won’t be anything worth keeping you here.”

She didn’t answer. He went on: “What’s the point? You showed me a real life, and at the same time asked me to live a lie. That’s beyond human endurance.”

“Oh, don’t say that,” she burst out, tears rising. “When I’m enduring it!”

Her arms fell limply along the table, her face turned toward him, open and exposed—like a soul laid bare. Archer stood stunned, overwhelmed by what it revealed. “You too… oh, all this time, you too?”

For an answer, she let the tears fall, slow and steady down her cheeks.

Half the room separated them. Neither moved.

Archer felt strangely detached from her presence—until one hand, resting on the table, caught his gaze, just as it had once before in that small house on Twenty-third Street. Now, his mind spun around it, as if it were the edge of a whirlpool. Still, he did not step closer.

He had known love that lived in touch and touch alone. But this—this was deeper, closer than bone. His only fear was to act in a way that might erase the echo of her words. His only thought: he would never again be truly alone.

But then, the weight of it crashed over him—the waste, the ruin. They were close, safe, enclosed. Yet bound by fate, as distant as continents.

“What’s the use,” he said suddenly, “when you’ll go back?”

A silent cry beneath his words: *How can I keep you?*

She sat still, eyes lowered. “Oh—I shan’t go yet.”

“Not yet? Then later? Some time you already know will come?”

At that, she lifted her clear, calm eyes. “I promise you—not as long as you hold out. Not as long as we can look at each other like this.”

He sank into his chair.

Her meaning was plain: *If you take a step, if you act, you’ll drive me back—back to all the horrors you know, all the temptations you suspect.*

He understood perfectly. And the knowledge anchored him to his seat, in a kind of hushed reverence.

“What a life for you,” he groaned.

“Oh—as long as it’s part of yours.”

“And mine, part of yours?”

She nodded.

“And that’s all—everything—between us?”

“Well,” she said softly, “isn’t it?”

At that, he rose, forgetting everything but the sweetness of her face.

She stood too—not to flee, not to rush to him, but quietly, as if the hardest part was done, and she only waited.

As he approached, her hands reached out—not to stop him, but to guide him. They fell into his, her arms extended but not stiff, holding him at a distance just enough to let her face speak.

They stood that way—perhaps minutes, perhaps longer. Long enough for her silence to carry all she meant, long enough for him to feel that only one truth mattered: he must not destroy this moment. He must leave their future in her hands, trusting only that she would hold on.

“Don’t—don’t be unhappy,” she whispered, voice breaking, as she drew her hands away.

“You won’t go back,” he said, as if it were the only possibility he could not endure.

“I won’t go back,” she replied.

Then she turned, opened the door, and led him into the public dining room.

The schoolteachers were gathering their things, preparing to scatter toward the wharf. Across the beach, the white steamboat rested at the pier. Over the sunlit water, Boston rose in a haze, golden and distant.

XXV. Once more on the boat, and in the presence of others, Archer felt a quietness within him that both surprised and sustained him. The day, by any ordinary measure, had been a failure—no kiss, no whispered word, no promise of what might come. Yet for a man consumed by unfulfilled longing, parting from the woman he loved with no certainty of ever seeing her again, this calm was almost shameful in its depth. It wasn’t triumph, nor even relief—it was something deeper: a peace born of knowing she had held fast to both duty and truth, not through calculation, but through honesty so pure it trembled in her eyes and faltered in her voice. Now that the moment had passed, he found himself filled with tenderness and awe. He thanked fate that he hadn’t yielded to pride or vanity, that he hadn’t risked their fragile bond for the sake of a dramatic gesture before strangers.

Even after they’d said farewell at the Fall River station, and he walked away alone, the conviction remained: he had gained far more than he had lost. He returned to the club, wandered into the empty library, and sat there, reliving every second of their time together. In his mind’s eye, he saw her face, heard her words, felt the weight of her silence. And as he reflected, one truth became clearer: if she chose to return to Europe, to her husband, it wouldn’t be because she longed for her old life—even under the new terms offered. No. She would go only if she feared becoming a danger to him, a temptation too strong to resist. Her decision would depend on whether she believed he could remain true to the standard they had both quietly set. And that standard rested entirely on him. If he could hold her at a distance—close enough to care, yet far enough to protect—then she might stay. But it was up to him to keep her there.

On the train, these thoughts wrapped around him like a golden veil. The faces around him blurred, distant, unreal. He felt as though speaking to them would be meaningless—they wouldn’t grasp what stirred in his heart. That same hazy clarity followed him into the next morning, when he woke to a stifling September day in New York. The train windows revealed a stream of weary travelers, their faces sunken and drawn; he watched them through the same dreamlike haze. Then, suddenly, one face broke through—the young man he’d seen leaving the Parker House the day before, the one who didn’t look like any American traveler. He remembered now: the sharp eyes, the foreign air, the way he seemed out of place among the bustling crowds.

The man stood still, gazing about him with the bewildered expression of someone cast adrift in a strange world. Then he stepped forward, lifted his hat, and said in English, “Surely, Monsieur, we met in London?”
“Ah, yes—London!” Archer replied, grasping his hand with warmth and curiosity. “So you *did* make it here, after all?” He looked closely at the thin, haggard face of M. Rivière, Carfry’s French tutor.
“Oh, I arrived,” the Frenchman smiled faintly, lips tight. “But not for long—I leave the day after tomorrow.” He held his valise loosely, gloved hands clenched, eyes searching Archer’s face. “I wonder, Monsieur… since I’ve found you, might I—”
“I was just going to invite you,” Archer interrupted. “Come to lunch? Downtown? If you’ll look me up at my office, I’ll take you to a decent place.”
M. Rivière looked touched, startled. “You’re too kind. But I was only asking how to find transport. There are no porters, and no one listens—”
“I know,” Archer said. “Our stations can be shocking. They give you chewing gum when you ask for help. But come along—I’ll get you sorted. And you *must* lunch with me.”
After a brief hesitation, the young man declined, saying he was already engaged. But once outside, in the open street, he asked if he might call later that afternoon. Archer, relaxed in the summer slowness of the office, agreed and scribbled down his address. The Frenchman pocketed it with thanks, tipped his hat grandly, and vanished into a passing horse-car.

At the appointed hour, M. Rivière arrived—shaved, composed, but still pale and serious. Archer was alone in his office. Before accepting the offered chair, the young man began abruptly: “I believe I saw you yesterday in Boston.”
It was a trivial remark—Archer was about to nod, then stopped. There was something in the man’s gaze—intense, probing, charged with meaning.
“It is extraordinary,” M. Rivière continued, “that we should meet under these circumstances.”
“What circumstances?” Archer asked, wondering if the man needed money.
M. Rivière studied him carefully. “I have come—not to seek employment, as I mentioned last time—but on a special mission.”
“A special mission,” Archer repeated, catching the thread instantly. The two encounters now connected in his mind. He paused, letting the revelation settle. M. Rivière waited silently, as if aware that his words were enough.
“A special mission,” Archer echoed.
The young Frenchman raised his palms slightly, as if offering nothing but truth. The two men stared across the desk, neither moving. Finally, Archer said, “Do sit down.”
M. Rivière bowed, took a distant chair, and waited.
“It was about this mission you wished to speak?” Archer asked.
M. Rivière nodded. “Not for myself. On that score, I have already decided. But I wish—may I?—to speak to you about the Countess Olenska.”

Archer had known the words were coming. When they did, they struck him like a blow—a sudden surge of blood to his temples, as if caught in a hidden thicket.
“And on whose behalf?” he asked, voice steady.
M. Rivière met his gaze firmly. “Well… I might say hers, though that sounds presumptuous. Shall I say—on behalf of justice?”
Archer smiled ironically. “In other words—you’re Count Olenski’s messenger?”
He saw the flush deepen on M. Rivière’s sallow face. “Not to *you*, Monsieur. If I come to you, it is for another reason.”
“What right do you have,” Archer snapped, “to stand on any ground but his?”
The young man considered. “My mission is over. As far as the Countess is concerned, it has failed.”
“I can’t help that,” Archer retorted.
“No—but you *can* help,” M. Rivière said, pausing. He turned his hat slowly in his gloves, looked inside it, then back at Archer. “You can help, Monsieur, I am convinced, to make it equally a failure with her family.”

Archer pushed back his chair and stood. “Well—and by God, I will!”
He stood with hands in pockets, glaring down at the small figure before him. M. Rivière rose too, but his face was still lower, pale as ash.
“Why the devil,” Archer exploded, “should you think—since you’re appealing to me because of my connection to Madame Olenska—that I’d take a different view from the rest of her family?”
For a moment, M. Rivière said nothing. His expression shifted—from timidity to deep distress. He looked utterly defenseless.
“Oh, Monsieur—”
“I can’t imagine,” Archer continued, “why you came to me when there are others closer to her. Or why you thought I’d be more open to your arguments.”
M. Rivière accepted this storm with quiet humility. “The arguments I want to present, Monsieur, are mine—my own, not those I was sent with.”
“Then I see no reason to listen.”
M. Rivière looked into his hat again, as if deciding whether to leave. Then he spoke with sudden force: “Monsieur—will you tell me one thing? Is it my right to be here that you question? Or do you believe the matter is already closed?”

That quiet insistence made Archer feel the heat of his own anger recede. M. Rivière had claimed space. Archer reddened slightly, sat down, and gestured for him to do the same.
“I beg your pardon. But why isn’t it closed?”
M. Rivière looked at him with anguish. “You do agree, then, with the rest of the family—that in light of the proposals I brought, it’s hardly possible for Madame Olenska not to return to her husband?”
“Good God!” Archer exclaimed.
A low murmur confirmed it.
“Before seeing her, I saw Mr. Lovell Mingott—at Count Olenski’s request. We spoke several times before Boston. I understand he represents his mother’s view—and Mrs. Manson Mingott’s influence is great throughout the family.”

Archer sat silent, feeling the ground shift beneath him. To be excluded from negotiations, even unaware they were happening—this stunned him. And then he understood: they had stopped consulting him because they knew he was no longer theirs. He recalled May’s careless remark during their drive home from Mrs. Mingott’s: “Perhaps Ellen would be happier with her husband.”
He remembered his sharp protest—and realized she had never spoken of Ellen since. That offhand comment had been a test. The wind had blown in their direction. The family had acted. He had been cut out.

And he admired them for it. May, especially—she would have resisted if her conscience rebelled. But she likely shared the belief that Ellen was better off as an unhappy wife than as a separated woman. And she probably thought it useless to discuss it with him—he had a habit of questioning the most sacred assumptions.

Archer looked up. “Don’t you know, Monsieur—is it possible you don’t know—that the family now doubts whether they have the right to advise the Countess to refuse her husband’s offer?”
“The proposals I brought?”
“The proposals I brought.”

Archer opened his mouth to snap that none of this was M. Rivière’s concern—but something in the young man’s humble courage stayed him. Instead, he asked: “What is your purpose in telling me this?”

The answer came without delay. “To beg you, Monsieur—to beg you with all I have—don’t let her go back. Oh, please, don’t let her!”

Archer stared. There was no doubt—this was real. The man had sacrificed everything to speak this truth.
“May I ask,” Archer said slowly, “if this was the line you took with the Countess?”
M. Rivière flushed, but his eyes held firm. “No, Monsieur. I accepted the mission in good faith. I truly believed—reasons I needn’t explain—that it would be better for her to return, to reclaim her position, her fortune, the respect her husband’s name gives her.”
“I supposed so,” Archer said. “One couldn’t accept such a task otherwise.”
“I wouldn’t have.”
“Well, then—?”
They locked eyes again, in silence.
“Ah, Monsieur,” M. Rivière said at last, “after I saw her, after I listened to her… I knew she was better off here.”
“You knew—?”
“She was good enough to hear me twice. She listened patiently, impartially. And in the course of those talks, I changed. I saw things differently.”
“What led to this change?”
“Simply seeing the change in *her*.”
“The change in her? You knew her before?”
The young man’s color rose. “I used to see her in her husband’s house. I’ve known Count Olenski for years. You can imagine—he would not send a stranger on such a task.”

Archer’s gaze drifted to the calendar on the wall, with the stern face of the President of the United States above it. That such a conversation could happen anywhere within his vast domain felt surreal.
“The change—what kind?”
“Ah, Monsieur—if I could tell you! _Tenez_—the discovery, perhaps, of what I’d never thought of before: that she’s an American. And that if you’re an American of *her* kind—of *your* kind—things that are accepted elsewhere, or tolerated as part of a convenient compromise, become unthinkable. Unbearable. If Madame Olenska’s relations truly understood what these things meant, their opposition to her return would be as absolute as her own. But they seem to see her husband’s desire to have her back as proof of a longing for domesticity.”
He paused. “Whereas it’s far from being that simple.”

Archer looked back at the President’s face, then down at his desk, scattered with papers. For a moment, he couldn’t speak. Then he heard the chair scrape, and saw M. Rivière rise.
When he looked up, the young man was as moved as he.
“Thank you,” Archer said simply.
“There’s nothing to thank you for, Monsieur. It is I, rather—”
M. Rivière broke off. Then, in a firmer voice: “I should like to add one thing. You asked if I’m in Count Olenski’s employ. I am—for reasons of necessity, like anyone who must care for older, ill relatives. But from the moment I came here to speak to you, I consider myself discharged. I will tell him so on my return—and give him my reasons.”
“That’s all, Monsieur.”
M. Rivière bowed, stepped back.
“Thank you,” Archer said again, as their hands met.

XXVI. Every year on the fifteenth of October, Fifth Avenue awoke from its autumn slumber. Shutters flew open, carpets unfurled across polished floors, and heavy drapes were drawn back to reveal windows gleaming like eyes in the crisp morning light. By the first of November, the rituals of high society had settled into their familiar rhythm—dinner invitations piled up, dance calendars filled in, and the opera season burst forth with new productions and glittering premieres. And just as reliably, around this time, Mrs. Archer would declare, with a sigh both weary and wistful, that New York had changed once again.

From her elevated vantage point of quiet disengagement, she watched the city’s social fabric with the keen eye of a historian. With Mr. Sillerton Jackson and Miss Sophy at her side, she traced each subtle fracture in tradition, each new bloom of behavior pushing through the carefully cultivated rows of propriety. It had long been one of young Newland Archer’s amusements to anticipate his mother’s annual lamentations—the way she could spot signs of decay invisible to others. To Mrs. Archer, change was never neutral; it was always a decline. And Miss Sophy Jackson, ever the dutiful companion, echoed her sentiments without hesitation. Mr. Sillerton Jackson, however, remained aloof, listening with amused detachment, though he never denied that something had shifted. And now, in the winter of his second year of marriage, even Newland felt it: New York wasn’t just changing—it was transforming.

These reflections came to the table during Mrs. Archer’s Thanksgiving dinner. At a time when gratitude was expected, she instead took stock of what had been lost. Society, if it could still be called such, seemed less a refuge than a spectacle—one fit only for biblical condemnation. Indeed, everyone knew exactly what Reverend Dr. Ashmore meant when he chose Jeremiah 2:25 for his sermon. The new rector of St. Matthew’s was considered “advanced”—his sermons bold, his language daring. When he spoke against fashionable life, he always referred to its “trend.” And to Mrs. Archer, it was both terrifying and thrilling to feel herself part of a world that was actively trending—like a crack spreading across stone.

“There’s no doubt Dr. Ashmore is right,” she said, as if the trend were something measurable, something visible. “But it was odd to preach about it on Thanksgiving.”

Miss Jackson nodded thoughtfully. “He means us to give thanks for what’s left.”

Newland had once smiled at these yearly pronouncements. But this year, he found himself nodding along. The changes were undeniable.

“The extravagance in dress,” Miss Jackson began, “is staggering. Sillerton took me to the first night of the Opera, and I can only say that Jane Merry’s gown was the only one I recognized from last season—and even that had been altered. Yet I know she brought it out of Worth two years ago. My seamstress always goes in to refashion her Paris gowns before they’re worn.”

“Ah, Jane Merry is one of *us*,” Mrs. Archer sighed, as if belonging to a fading caste were no longer an honor.

“Yes,” Miss Jackson replied. “In my youth, it was considered vulgar to wear the newest fashions. Amy Sillerton told me Bostonians kept their Paris dresses locked away for two years. Old Mrs. Baxter Pennilow imported twelve annually—two velvet, two satin, two silk, six in poplin and cashmere. A standing order. She was ill for two years before she died, so forty-eight Worth dresses were found untouched in tissue paper. When her daughters emerged from mourning, they wore the first batch at Symphony concerts—without looking ahead of the times.”

“Well, Boston is more conservative,” Mrs. Archer conceded. “But I think it’s wise for any lady to wait at least one season before wearing a French gown.”

“It was Beaufort who started this new fashion,” she continued. “Making his wife parade her clothes the moment they arrived. At times, it takes all Regina’s dignity not to look… well…”

Miss Jackson glanced at Janey, who was staring too intently, and murmured something indistinct.

“Like her rivals,” Mr. Sillerton Jackson offered, with the air of delivering a clever remark.

“Oh,” the ladies murmured, shifting their attention.

Mrs. Archer turned to her son. “Poor Regina. Her Thanksgiving hasn’t been cheerful, I fear. Have you heard the rumors about Beaufort’s speculations, Sillerton?”

Mr. Jackson shrugged. Everyone had heard them. He saw no need to confirm what was already common knowledge.

A silence fell over the room. No one truly liked Beaufort, but the idea of him bringing financial ruin upon his wife’s family was deeply unsettling. New York tolerated moral lapses in private life—but in business, honesty was sacred. It had been decades since a prominent banker failed disgracefully, but the memory of the social annihilation that followed such an event lingered. If Beaufort did fall, no amount of Dallas influence would save Regina. Not even her charm or her beauty.

The conversation drifted to safer ground, but every topic seemed to reinforce Mrs. Archer’s sense of inevitable change.

“Of course, Newland,” she said, “I know you let dear May go to Mrs. Struthers’s Sunday evenings.”

May interjected cheerfully, “Oh, everyone does now. And she was invited to Granny’s last reception.”

Archer reflected on this—how New York managed transitions: ignoring them until they were complete, then pretending they’d happened long ago. There was always someone who opened the gates. Once people tasted Mrs. Struthers’s easy hospitality, they couldn’t return to their old ways—even if they remembered her champagne had once been rumored to be transmuted shoe polish.

“I know, dear,” Mrs. Archer sighed. “Such things must happen, I suppose, as long as amusement is the goal. But I’ve never forgiven your cousin Madame Olenska for being the first to support Mrs. Struthers.”

A sudden flush rose in May’s cheeks. Her husband noticed it as much as anyone else. “Oh, *Ellen*,” she murmured, using the same tone her parents might have used when speaking of the Blenkers—accusing yet sorrowful.

It was the tone the family had adopted whenever Ellen Olenska was mentioned, since she refused to return to her husband. But coming from May, it carried a deeper weight. Archer looked at her with a strange sense of distance—this woman who belonged so completely to her world, yet sometimes seemed to exist outside it.

Mrs. Archer pressed on, unaware of the tension. “I always thought people like the Countess Olenska, who’ve lived among aristocrats, should help preserve our standards—instead of abandoning them.”

May’s blush didn’t fade. It seemed to carry a meaning beyond mere social disapproval.

“I suppose we all seem alike to foreigners,” Miss Jackson remarked tartly.

“I don’t think Ellen cares for society,” May said, searching for neutrality. “But nobody knows exactly what she *does* care for.”

Mrs. Archer sighed again. Everyone knew Ellen was no longer welcome in her family’s circle. Even old Mrs. Manson Mingott, her fiercest defender, had given up trying to persuade her to return. The Mingotts hadn’t declared their disapproval aloud—their loyalty ran too deep. Instead, they simply let Ellen “find her own level”—and that level, tragically, was somewhere near the Blenkers, among writers and artists who celebrated their messy lives.

It was incredible, but true: Ellen, despite her privilege, had become “Bohemian.” This confirmed, in many minds, that she had made a fatal mistake by refusing her husband. After all, a young woman’s place was under her husband’s roof—especially when she had left it under circumstances… well, if one cared to examine them…

“Madame Olenska is very popular with the gentlemen,” Miss Sophy observed, as if offering comfort while planting a thorn.

“Ah, yes,” Mrs. Archer agreed gravely. “That’s the danger a woman like her faces.”

And so the ladies retired to the drawing-room, leaving Archer and Mr. Jackson in the Gothic library.

Settling before the fire, Mr. Jackson lit a cigar and leaned back, suddenly expansive. “If Beaufort fails,” he said, “there’ll be disclosures.”

Archer looked up sharply. The image of Beaufort, thick-set and opulent, walking through snow at Skuytercliff, flashed in his mind.

“There’ll be a nasty cleanup,” Jackson continued. “He hasn’t spent all his money on Regina.”

“Oh, well—that’s discounted, isn’t it? I believe he’ll pull through,” Archer said, eager to shift the subject.

“Perhaps,” Jackson allowed. “He was supposed to meet some influential men today. Of course, we hope he can get through this—this time. I wouldn’t want poor Regina ending up in some shabby foreign spa for bankrupts.”

Archer said nothing. It seemed natural—however tragic—that ill-gotten wealth should be paid for in suffering. His thoughts, however, turned elsewhere: to May’s blush, to the memory of the day he and Ellen had spent together four months ago. Since then, he hadn’t seen her. He knew she was in Washington, living in a small house with Medora Manson. He had written once—briefly, asking when they might meet. Her reply: “Not yet.”

No more words followed. And in the silence between them, a sanctuary had formed in his mind—a quiet chamber where Ellen reigned, where his real thoughts and feelings lived. Outside, his life felt hollow, a performance. He moved through it with growing unease, bumping into old beliefs as if he weren’t truly present.

He became aware that Jackson was clearing his throat.

“I don’t know how far your wife’s family knows what people are saying about… well, about Madame Olenska’s refusal to accept her husband’s latest offer.”

Archer remained silent.

Jackson continued, obliquely: “It’s a pity—certainly a pity—that she refused.”

“A pity? In God’s name, why?” Archer asked.

Jackson looked down his leg, adjusting his sock. “Well—on the lowest ground—what’s she going to live on now?”

“Now?”

“If Beaufort—”

Archer stood abruptly, slamming his fist on the writing desk. The inkwell trembled.

“What the devil do you mean, sir?”

Jackson shifted in his chair, his gaze steady. “I have it on good authority—old Catherine herself—that the family reduced her allowance when she refused to return. And by refusing, she forfeits the money settled on her at marriage—money Olenski was ready to transfer if she came back. So, what *do* you mean by asking me what *I* mean?”

Archer walked to the fireplace, knocking ashes into the grate.

“I don’t know her affairs,” he said, voice tight. “But I don’t need to know to be certain that what you suggest—”
