“Oh, I don’t,” Jackson interrupted. “Lefferts, for one, thinks it’s a shame.”

“Lefferts—who made love to her and got rejected!” Archer snapped.

“Ah—did he?” Jackson replied, as if catching a trap.

They stared at each other across the fire.

“Well,” Jackson said slowly, “it’s a pity she didn’t go back before Beaufort’s downfall. If she goes *now*, and he fails, it will only confirm the impression—though it’s not just Lefferts who feels that way.”

“She won’t go back now—less than ever!”

Archer had spoken without thinking. And instantly, he sensed Jackson had been waiting for that.

The old man studied him. “That’s your opinion, eh? Well, perhaps you do know best. But everyone says Medora Manson’s few remaining funds are in Beaufort’s hands. How the two women will survive unless he pulls through, I can’t imagine. Of course, Madame Olenska might still win over old Catherine—she’s been the most opposed. But we all know she hates spending money. And the rest of the family has little interest in keeping her here.”

Archer burned with frustration. He knew he was acting rashly—but he couldn’t stop. He realized Jackson had noted his ignorance of Ellen’s strained relations with her family, and had drawn conclusions about why he wasn’t privy to those secrets.

Yet he remembered: Jackson was a guest. Under the rules of old New York, no argument could take place beneath a host’s roof.

“Shall we go up and join my mother?” he asked curtly.

On the drive home, May remained silent. Through the darkness, he felt her blush like a warning.

They reached the house. He went to the library. She usually followed—but this time, he heard her turn toward her bedroom.

“May!” he called.

She returned, surprised by his tone.

“This lamp is smoking again,” he muttered, irritated. “The servants should see to it.”

“I’m sorry. It won’t happen again,” she said, bright and firm—just like her mother.

It angered him. Already, she was treating him like a younger Mr. Welland.

She bent to adjust the flame. The light caught her shoulders, her face—so young, so pure.

“How young she is,” he thought. “For how many endless years will this life go on?”

He felt the heat of his own blood, the strength of his youth.

“Look here,” he said suddenly. “I may have to go to Washington—soon. Next week, perhaps.”

Her hand stayed on the lamp’s key as she turned slowly.

“On business?” she asked, as if it were the only possible reason.

“On business, naturally. There’s a patent case before the Supreme Court—” He named the inventor, gave details, speaking with the smooth confidence of Lawrence Lefferts.

She listened, nodding. “Yes, I see.”

“When you go,” she said quietly, “you must be sure to visit Ellen.”

She looked at him, smiling—clear-eyed, calm.

It was the only word they exchanged. But in their world, it meant everything:

*Of course I know what people say about Ellen. I understand your role in her defiance. I know you advised her not to return—against the wishes of the whole family, including Grandmother. I know you encouraged her. I know you plan to see her. I know you may be going to Washington for that purpose. And I tell you—because I cannot speak plainly—this is not approved. But I also tell you: I approve of your seeing her. I wish you to go. And I wish you to make her understand the consequences of her choices.*

Her hand remained on the lamp’s key.

She lowered the wick, removed the globe, and blew gently on the flame.

“They smell less if you blow them out,” she said, practical, serene.

At the doorway, she paused. “Goodnight.”

He kissed her—once, briefly—before stepping into the darkened hall.

XXVII. The next day, Wall Street offered more comforting news about Beaufort’s situation. Though the reports were still uncertain, they carried a glimmer of hope. It was widely believed that he had access to powerful allies in times of crisis—and that he’d already called upon them with some success. That evening, when Mrs. Beaufort appeared at the Opera wearing her usual radiant smile and a dazzling new emerald necklace, society collectively exhaled in relief. New York had always been unforgiving when it came to moral or financial transgressions. There had never been a single exception to its unspoken rule: those who betrayed trust must face the consequences. Even Beaufort and his wife were expected to be sacrificed without hesitation to this principle. But the thought of sacrificing them stirred not just sorrow, but discomfort. Their absence would leave a noticeable gap in their close-knit circle. For some, indifferent to the deeper implications, the real tragedy was the loss of what many considered the finest ballroom in the city.

Archer had made up his mind to go to Washington. He only waited for the start of the patent lawsuit he’d mentioned to May, so that his trip could coincide with the court date. But on Tuesday, Mr. Letterblair informed him the case might be delayed for weeks. Still, Archer resolved to leave the very next evening. He didn’t expect May—so detached from his professional life, so untouched by its intricacies—to notice the delay, nor to remember the names involved if they came up. And besides, he could no longer postpone seeing Madame Olenska. There were things he needed to say—things that couldn’t wait.

On Wednesday morning, as Archer arrived at his office, Mr. Letterblair met him with a grim expression. Beaufort, it seemed, hadn’t managed to weather the storm after all. Yet by spreading word that he had, he had calmed his depositors. A flood of withdrawals had poured into the bank until the previous evening, when troubling rumors resurfaced. A run had begun, and the bank was likely to shut its doors before the day ended. The whispers around town were cruel—calling Beaufort’s actions cowardly, deceitful. His downfall was shaping up to be one of the most disgraceful in Wall Street’s history. The weight of it left Mr. Letterblair pale and shaken.

“I’ve seen hard times,” he said, “but nothing like this. Everyone we know will feel the ripple. And what about Mrs. Beaufort? What can possibly be done for her?” He paused, then added, “I feel for Mrs. Manson Mingott as much as anyone. At her age, who knows how this might affect her? She believed in Beaufort—she made him a friend! And there’s the Dallas connection: poor Mrs. Beaufort is kin to all of you. Her only hope would be to leave him—but how can anyone tell her that? Her duty is beside him. Luckily, she’s always been blind to his flaws.”

A knock interrupted them. Mr. Letterblair turned sharply. “What is it? I can’t be disturbed.”
A clerk entered, handed Archer a letter, and withdrew.
Recognizing May’s handwriting, Archer opened it at once:

*Won’t you please come up town as early as you can? Granny had a slight stroke last night. Somehow, she learned about the bank before anyone else. Uncle Lovell is away hunting, and the idea of the disgrace has made Papa so nervous he’s running a fever and can’t leave his room. Mamma needs you terribly. I hope you can get here right away and go straight to Granny’s.*

Archer passed the note to Mr. Letterblair and soon found himself squeezing into a crowded horse-car, switching later to one of the swaying omnibuses along Fifth Avenue. It was past noon when he finally reached old Catherine’s house.

The sitting-room window, where she usually sat like a queen, was now occupied by her daughter, Mrs. Welland, who gave a weary greeting as she saw Archer. At the door, May greeted him with a pale but steady smile. The hall looked unnaturally chaotic—coats and furs piled on chairs, a doctor’s bag and coat on the table, letters and cards piling up unread. Dr. Bencomb had just arrived for the second time, and though the outlook was still uncertain, he offered a hopeful note. Mrs. Mingott’s fierce will to live was already lifting the spirits of those around her.

May led Archer into the sitting-room, where the sliding doors to the bedroom were closed, draped with heavy yellow damask curtains. Mrs. Welland whispered the details of the night’s events. The previous evening, just after dinner, when Mrs. Mingott had finished her game of solitaire, the bell rang. A woman, heavily veiled, asked to see her. The butler, recognizing her voice, opened the door and announced: “Mrs. Julius Beaufort”—then closed it again behind them. They stayed together for nearly an hour. When Mrs. Mingott rang for help, Mrs. Beaufort had already slipped away unnoticed. The old lady, pale and immense, sat alone in her chair, silently signaling the butler to assist her to bed. At first, she seemed fully aware and composed.

But at three in the morning, the bell rang again. The servants rushed in to find her propped against her pillows, a strange crooked smile on her lips, one hand limp at her side. The stroke had been minor—she could speak, express herself, and gradually regain control of her face. But the shock had been terrible.

And worse still—the reason for her distress. From fragments of speech, it became clear that Regina Beaufort had come to beg her—unimaginably bold—to support her husband, to help them through, to prevent the family from “deserting” them. In fact, to make the entire family condone their shame.

“I told her,” Mrs. Mingott had gasped, her voice thick with paralysis, “‘Honour’s always been honour, and honesty honesty, in the Manson Mingott house—and will be till I’m carried out feet first.’ And when she said, ‘But my name, Auntie—my name’s Regina Dallas,’ I replied, ‘It was Beaufort when he covered you with jewels, and it’s got to stay Beaufort now that he’s covered you with shame.’”

Mrs. Welland spoke these words in horrified tones, trembling at having to confront such a scandal. “If only I could keep it from your father-in-law—he always says, ‘Augusta, for pity’s sake, don’t destroy my last illusions.’ How can I shield him from this?”

“After all,” May gently suggested, “he won’t have _seen_ it.”

Mrs. Welland sighed. “Ah, yes—thank heaven he’s safe in bed. And Dr. Bencomb promises to keep him there until Mama improves—and until Regina is sent away.”

Archer sat near the window, staring blankly at the quiet street outside. He understood now why he’d been summoned—not for practical help, but for presence, for strength. Messages had been sent to other family members; Mr. Lovell Mingott had been telegraphed. But for now, there was little to do except talk in hushed voices about the fallout.

Mrs. Lovell Mingott returned from another room, joining the conversation. In their day, the elder women agreed, a man’s disgrace meant his wife should vanish—disappear with him. “There was the case of poor Grandmamma Spicer,” Mrs. Welland recalled. “Your great-grandmother, May. Of course, your great-grandfather’s troubles were private—cards, or signing a note for someone—I never knew exactly, because Mamma wouldn’t talk about it. But her mother had to leave New York after whatever it was. They lived alone up the Hudson, winter and summer, until Mamma was sixteen. Never once did Grandmamma Spicer think of asking the family to ‘countenance’ her, as Regina calls it. Though a private failure is nothing compared to ruining hundreds of innocent people.”

“Yes,” Mrs. Lovell Mingott nodded, “it would be far more fitting for Regina to hide her own face than to demand others cover theirs. I heard the emerald necklace she wore at the Opera Friday was sent on approval from Ball and Black’s that afternoon. I wonder if they’ll ever get it back?”

Archer listened quietly. The idea of absolute integrity was deeply rooted in him—no sentiment could soften it. Men like Lemuel Struthers could amass wealth through dubious means, but in old New York, honor was the duty of the privileged. And Mrs. Beaufort’s fate stirred little sympathy in him. He felt sorry for her, perhaps more than her relatives did—but he believed that marriage, even if fragile in good times, should be unbreakable in ruin. As Mr. Letterblair had said, a wife’s place was beside her husband in trouble. But society’s place was not beside him—and Mrs. Beaufort’s assumption that it was made her seem almost complicit. To ask family to protect a husband’s financial disgrace was unthinkable—it was something the Family, as a whole, could never do.

The mulatto maid called Mrs. Lovell Mingott into the hall. She returned with a troubled look. “She wants me to telegraph Ellen Olenska. I’d already written to Ellen and Medora, but now it seems that’s not enough. I’m to send a telegram immediately—and tell her to come alone.”

Silence fell.

Mrs. Welland sighed. May rose and gathered scattered papers from the floor.
“It must be done,” Mrs. Lovell Mingott said, as if hoping someone would object.

May turned back. “Of course it must be done. Granny knows what she wants, and we must obey her wishes. Shall I write the telegram for you, Auntie? If it goes right away, Ellen can catch tomorrow’s train.”

She pronounced the name with unusual clarity—like two silver bells chiming.

“Well, it can’t go right away,” Mrs. Lovell Mingott said. “Jasper and the pantry-boy are both out with messages.”

May turned to Archer with a gentle smile. “But here’s Newland, ready to help. Will you take the telegram, Newland? There’s just time before lunch.”

Archer stood, murmuring agreement. May sat at her grandmother’s rosewood writing desk and wrote the message in her large, careful script. When finished, she blotted it neatly and handed it to him.

“What a pity,” she said softly, “that you and Ellen will cross paths on the way—Newland,” she added, turning to her mother and aunt, “you’re going to Washington for a patent case before the Supreme Court. I suppose Uncle Lovell will be back by tomorrow night, and since Granny is improving, it doesn’t seem fair to ask you to give up such an important engagement—does it?”

She paused, waiting for confirmation. Mrs. Welland quickly responded: “Oh, of course not, darling. Your Granny would never want that.”

As Archer left the room with the telegram, he heard his mother-in-law whisper, “But why on earth should she want you to telegraph for Ellen Olenska—”

And May’s clear voice replied: “Perhaps it’s to remind her—once again—that her duty lies with her husband.”

The outer door clicked shut behind him. Archer walked quickly toward the telegraph office, the weight of the moment pressing down—of choices, of loyalties, of love and obligation, all converging in the silence of a city caught between scandal and silence.

XXVIII. "Ol—ol—how do you spell it, anyway?" the sharp-tongued young woman behind the brass counter of the Western Union office asked, her eyes narrowing as Archer slid his wife’s telegram across the ledge.

"Olenska—O-len-ska," he repeated, pulling the message back slightly so he could carefully print the foreign syllables above May’s looping handwriting.

"It’s a peculiar name for a New York telegraph office—especially in this part of town," remarked a voice from behind him, calm and unexpectedly familiar. Archer turned to see Lawrence Lefferts standing beside him, tugging at his moustache with studied nonchalance, his gaze fixed somewhere just beyond the telegram.

"Hallo, Newland," Lefferts said, as if catching up on a casual stroll. "Thought I’d find you here. Just heard about old Mrs. Mingott’s stroke. I was heading to the house when I saw you turning down this street and followed along. You’ve come from there, I suppose?"

Archer nodded and slipped the telegram through the lattice.

"Very serious, then?" Lefferts continued, his tone light but probing. "Wiring the family, I imagine. I gather it *is* serious—if you’re including Countess Olenska."

A flash of anger surged through Archer. For a moment, he wanted nothing more than to lash out at Lefferts’ smooth, self-assured face. But he held himself back. To speak of Ellen Olenska with this man—here, now—was unthinkable. He paid for the telegram and stepped out into the street with Lefferts by his side.

Once outside, Archer regained his composure. "Mrs. Mingott is much better," he said. "The doctor says there’s no real cause for concern."

Lefferts sighed with relief, then quickly shifted the subject. "Have you heard? There are terrible rumors again about Beaufort..."

By the next day, the news of Beaufort’s financial collapse had spread across every newspaper. It eclipsed even the report of Mrs. Manson Mingott’s stroke. Only a few who knew the deeper connections suspected that Catherine’s illness might have been stirred not just by age or overindulgence, but by the shock of the scandal.

New York reeled under the weight of Beaufort’s betrayal. As Mr. Letterblair lamented, it was the worst case he’d ever seen—not just in his own memory, but in the long history of the firm bearing his name. The bank had kept accepting deposits for an entire day after its downfall was certain. And since many of its clients belonged to powerful families, the deception felt all the more calculated.

Had Mrs. Beaufort spoken with dignity, perhaps some sympathy might have softened public outrage. But instead, she insisted such misfortunes were “the test of friendship”—a phrase that only deepened the scorn. When it became known why she had visited Mrs. Mingott late at night, her coldness seemed worse than her husband’s. She had no excuse, nor did her critics have the satisfaction of blaming her foreignness.

At least, people comforted themselves, Beaufort *was* foreign. But when a man like Dallas of South Carolina dismissed the disaster with a careless shrug—“He’ll be on his feet again soon”—the argument lost its strength. In the end, society had no choice but to accept the bitter truth: marriage, however grand, was unbreakable.

And so, life would go on without the Beauforts. Except for those left in ruin—Medora Manson, Miss Lannings, and others whose trust had been shattered. Had they listened to Mr. Henry van der Luyden...

"The best thing," Mrs. Archer declared, as if delivering a verdict, "is for them to go live at Regina’s little place in North Carolina. Beaufort’s always kept a racing stable—he should breed trotting horses. I’d say he has all the makings of a successful horsedealer."

Everyone agreed, though no one dared ask what the Beauforts truly planned.

The next day, Mrs. Manson Mingott was indeed much improved. She regained her voice just enough to command that the Beauforts never be mentioned again—and when Dr. Bencomb arrived, she demanded to know why her family was making such a fuss over her health.

"If people of my age *will* eat chicken salad at dinner," she snapped, "what can they expect?"

With the doctor adjusting her diet, the stroke was rewritten as a mere bout of indigestion.

But though she maintained her usual sharpness, something had shifted within old Catherine. Age had begun to distance her from the world—though not from curiosity. Her compassion, never strong, had faded further. She dismissed the Beaufort disaster as easily as she once dismissed gossip.

Yet for the first time, she turned inward. She began to fixate on her own ailments, and to take a sudden, sentimental interest in members of her family she had long ignored.

Mr. Welland, in particular, caught her attention. Of all her sons-in-law, he was the one she had most often overlooked. His wife had tried for years to convince her of his strength and intellect—had he only “chosen.” But her efforts were met only with a dismissive laugh.

Now, however, his frailty made him fascinating. Mrs. Mingott sent a royal summons for him to visit, to compare diets—once his temperature allowed. After all, she now believed, one could never be too careful about temperatures.

Twenty-four hours after Madame Olenska’s telegram, another arrived: she would arrive from Washington the following evening.

At the Wellands’, where the Archers happened to be lunching, the question arose immediately: who would meet her at Jersey City?

The household, already strained by its modest means, debated fiercely. Mrs. Welland couldn’t go—she was needed at her mother’s. The brougham couldn’t be spared, in case Mr. Welland collapsed upon seeing his mother-in-law after her attack. The Welland boys were downtown. Mr. Lovell Mingott was returning from a hunting trip, and the family carriage was already reserved to meet him. And May—no matter how capable—couldn’t be expected to cross the ferry alone in the twilight.

Yet letting Ellen arrive unmet seemed rude, even against old Catherine’s wishes.

“It’s just like Ellen,” Mrs. Welland murmured wearily, “to put us in such a bind.”

“The only thing that makes me think Mamma isn’t as well as Bencomb says,” she added, half-joking, “is this sudden need to see Ellen right away—no matter how inconvenient.”

Her words slipped out before she could stop them.

Mr. Welland froze. His fork clattered onto the plate.

“Augusta,” he said, pale, “do you really think Bencomb isn’t as reliable as he used to be? Have you noticed him being less attentive lately—with my case… or your mother’s?”

Mrs. Welland paled in turn. The implications flooded through her mind—her mistake, the consequences. But she forced a laugh, helped herself to more oysters, and said, regaining her cheerful mask:

“My dear, how could you think such a thing? I only meant—after Mamma took such a firm stand about Ellen’s duty to return to her husband, it seems strange she’d suddenly want her here now. Especially when there are half a dozen other grandchildren she might have called for. But we must remember—Mamma, for all her energy, is very old.”

Mr. Welland remained silent. Then slowly, deliberately:

“Yes. Very old. And for all we know, Bencomb may not be as effective with elderly patients. As you say, my dear—it’s always one thing after another. In ten or fifteen years, I suppose I’ll have the pleasant task of finding a new doctor. Better to make the change before it becomes necessary.”

He picked up his fork again, resolute.

But as Mrs. Welland rose to leave, she paused. “Still,” she said, leading the way into the dimly lit back drawing-room, “I don’t see how Ellen is going to get here tomorrow evening. I like to have things settled at least twenty-four hours ahead.”

Archer, still captivated by a small painting of two cardinals feasting in an ebony frame studded with onyx, looked up.

“I’ll go,” he offered. “I can leave the office early enough to catch the brougham at the ferry—if May sends it.”

His heart pounded.

Mrs. Welland breathed a sigh of relief. May, who had drifted to the window, turned and smiled warmly at him.

“So you see, Mamma,” she said, leaning down to kiss her mother’s forehead, “everything *will* be settled twenty-four hours in advance.”

May’s brougham waited at the door. She would drive Archer to Union Square, where he could catch a Broadway car to the office.

As she settled into her seat, she said, “I didn’t want to worry Mamma by raising obstacles—but how can you meet Ellen tomorrow and bring her back, when you’re going to Washington?”

“Oh, I’m not going,” Archer replied.

“Not going? Why, what’s happened?” Her voice was clear, bright, full of concern.

“The case is off—postponed.”

“Postponed? How odd! This morning I saw a note from Mr. Letterblair to Mamma saying he was going to Washington tomorrow for the big patent case he’s arguing before the Supreme Court. You said it was a patent case, didn’t you?”

“Well—that’s it. The whole office can’t go. Letterblair decided to go this morning.”

“Then it’s *not* postponed?” she pressed, her tone sharper than usual.

Archer felt his cheeks flush. He cursed himself for giving details when he’d announced his plans earlier. He remembered reading somewhere that clever liars give details—but the cleverest say nothing at all.

“No,” he said. “But my going is.”

He added, retreating into sarcasm, “I’m not going till later—luckily for your family’s convenience.”

As he spoke, he felt her gaze on him. He turned to meet her eyes—neither avoiding nor challenging. Their glance locked, briefly, and in that moment, something passed between them—something neither wished to name.

“Yes,” May said brightly, “it *is* awfully convenient that you can meet Ellen after all. You saw how much Mamma appreciated your offering.”

“Oh, I’m delighted to do it.”

The carriage stopped. As he stepped out, she leaned forward and placed her hand on his.

“Good-bye, dearest,” she said, her eyes so blue he wondered later if they had shone with tears.

He walked away, crossing Union Square, repeating to himself in a low chant:

“It’s all of two hours from Jersey City to old Catherine’s. Two hours—and it may be more.”

XXIX. His wife’s dark blue brougham—still gleaming with the fresh varnish of its wedding day—awaited Archer at the ferry, gliding smoothly toward him like a promise kept. It carried him in quiet luxury from the Jersey City terminal, where the air was thick with snow and silence, the gas lamps flickering to life in the vast echoing hall. As he paced the platform, waiting for the Washington express, his mind drifted to the dreams people once whispered about: tunnels beneath rivers, ships crossing oceans in days, flying machines, electric light, voices carried through invisible wires. “I don’t care which one comes true,” he thought, “as long as that tunnel stays unmade.” In the reckless joy of it all, he imagined her stepping down from the train—Ellen Olenska—her face pale against the crowd, her eyes meeting his across the sea of strangers. He saw himself guiding her through the chaos, her hand slipping into his, their slow journey toward the ferry under the weight of falling snow, the world spinning beneath them like a dream.

He had so much to say. The words formed in his mouth like poetry, ready to unfold. But then the train groaned into the station, heavy and monstrous, and he pushed through the throng, scanning each window until—there she was. Her face, close and startled, brought a sudden pang of shame: he had forgotten how she looked. They met. Their hands touched. He slipped her arm through his. “This way—I’ve got the carriage.”

And just like that, everything unfolded as he’d dreamed. He helped her inside, her bags tucked carefully beside her, and in the blur of motion, he recalled reassuring her about her grandmother, brushing aside her worries with a soft, “Poor Regina!” She was fragile, delicate—like something made of glass. The carriage pulled away from the station, navigating the slick incline toward the wharf, dodging coal carts, confused horses, and a disheveled express wagon—and then, an empty hearse. She closed her eyes, gripping his hand tightly. “If only it doesn’t mean… poor Granny?”
“Oh, no—she’s better,” he said quickly, “really well. There—we passed it!”
But her fingers remained locked in his, and when they crossed onto the ferry, he leaned forward, unbuttoned her tight brown glove, and kissed her palm—as if it were a sacred relic. She smiled faintly and pulled back.
“You didn’t expect me today?”
“Oh, no.”
“I meant to go to Washington. I’d planned everything—I almost crossed paths with you on the train.”
“Oh—” she breathed, as if the near-miss terrified her.
“Do you know—I hardly remembered you?”
“Hardly remembered me?”
“It’s always like this. _Each time you happen to me all over again._”
“Oh, yes—I know. I know.”
“Does it… do I too… to you?”
She nodded, gazing out the window. “Ellen—Ellen—Ellen.”
No answer. Only silence. And he watched her profile fade into the snow-dappled dusk, wondering what she’d done in those four months, how little they truly knew each other, despite the closeness that now pressed between them. The moments slipped by, and he realized he’d forgotten every word he’d meant to speak. All that remained was the ache of their proximity—so near, yet so far.

Then she turned, her voice light. “What a pretty carriage! Is it May’s?”
“Yes.”
“So May sent you?”
“How kind of her!”
He didn’t reply at first. Then, sharply: “Your husband’s secretary came to see me the day after we met in Boston.”
He hadn’t mentioned it in his letter. He’d meant to keep it buried. But now, here in his wife’s carriage, he felt a sudden urge to strike back. Would she react any better than he did to the mention of May?
She showed no surprise. No flicker.
“He writes to her, then.”
“M. Rivière went to see you?”
“Yes. Didn’t you know?”
“No.”
“And you’re not surprised?”
She hesitated. “Why should I be? He told me in Boston—he said he knew you. I think he met you in England.”
“Ellen—I must ask you something.”
“Yes.”
“I wanted to ask after I saw him—but I couldn’t put it in a letter. Was it Rivière who helped you leave your husband?”
His heart pounded. Would she answer with the same calm?
“Yes. I owe him a great debt.”
Her voice was steady, quiet—almost indifferent. And in that moment, Archer felt foolish, caught in the trap of his own drama. Once more, her simplicity stripped him bare.
“I think you’re the most honest woman I’ve ever met,” he said.
“Oh, no—but probably one of the least fussy,” she replied, a smile in her tone. “Call it what you like—you look at things as they are.”
“Ah—I’ve had to. I’ve had to look at the Gorgon.”
“Well—she hasn’t blinded you. You’ve seen she’s just another old bogey.”
“She doesn’t blind one—but she dries up one’s tears.”
The words stopped him. They came from a depth he couldn’t reach. The ferry slowed, bumped against the dock, and the carriage lurched, throwing them together. He trembled, feeling the warmth of her shoulder, and wrapped his arm around her.
“If you’re not blind, then, you must see that this can’t last.”
“What can’t?”
“Our being together—and not together.”
“No. You ought not to have come today,” she said, her voice changed. Then suddenly, she turned, threw her arms around him, and kissed him.
At the same instant, the carriage began to move. A gas lamp flashed into the window. She pulled back. They sat still, silent, as the brougham wove through the tangle of vehicles at the landing. When they reached the street, Archer spoke fast, urgently.
“Don’t be afraid of me. You don’t need to shrink back. A stolen kiss isn’t what I want. Look—I’m not even touching your sleeve. I know why you don’t want this to become just another secret affair. I couldn’t have said this yesterday—when I was apart from you, longing for you, every thought burning in fire. But now you’re here—more than I remembered. What I want is more than fleeting moments. More than hunger between them. So I sit still beside you, trusting—just trusting—that it will come true.”
For a moment, silence. Then she whispered, barely audible:
“What do you mean by trusting it to come true?”
“Why—don’t you know it will?”
“Your vision of us together?”
She laughed—a sharp, sudden sound. “You choose your place well to ask me that!”
“Because we’re in my wife’s carriage?”
“Shall we get out and walk? I suppose you don’t mind the snow?”
She laughed again, softer now. “No. I won’t get out. My business is to reach Granny’s quickly. And you’ll sit beside me. We’ll look at realities—not visions.”
“I don’t know what you mean by realities. The only reality to me is this.”
Another long silence. The carriage rolled down a shadowed side street, then into the glow of Fifth Avenue.
“So—is it your idea that I should live with you as your mistress—since I can’t be your wife?”
The word stunned him. Women of his world avoided it, even in whispers. But she said it plainly, as if it belonged in her tongue. Had it been spoken freely in the life she fled?
He floundered. “I want—to get away with you into a world where words like that… categories like that… don’t exist. Where we’re just two people who love each other. Who are everything to each other. Nothing else matters.”
She sighed deeply, then laughed again. “Oh, my dear—where is that country? Have you ever been there?”
When he stayed silent, she continued: “I know many who’ve tried. And they all got off at wrong stations—Boulogne, Pisa, Monte Carlo. But it wasn’t different from the world they left. Just smaller. Duller. Messier.”
He had never heard her like this. He remembered her earlier words.
“Yes—the Gorgon _has_ dried your tears,” he said.
“She opened my eyes too. It’s a lie to say she blinds people. She opens their eyelids wide—so they can never return to darkness. Isn’t there a Chinese torture like that? There should be. Believe me—it’s a miserable little country.”
They crossed Forty-second Street. May’s strong horse carried them northward, swift and sure. Archer felt the minutes slipping away, the words wasted.
“So—what exactly is your plan for us?”
“For _us_? There’s no _us_ in that sense. We’re near only when we stay far apart. Then we can be ourselves. Otherwise, we’re just Newland Archer, husband of Ellen Olenska’s cousin, and Ellen Olenska, cousin of Newland Archer’s wife—trying to be happy behind the backs of those who trust us.”
“Ahh—I’m beyond that,” he groaned.
“No, you’re not. You’ve never been beyond. And I have,” she said, her voice strange. “And I know what it looks like there.”
He sat in silence, overwhelmed. Then, in the dark, he found the bell. He remembered May rang twice to stop. He pressed it. The carriage halted.
“Why are we stopping? This isn’t Granny’s,” she said.
“No—I’m getting out here,” he stammered, opening the door and stepping onto the pavement.
By the streetlight, he saw her face—startled, reaching for him. He shut the door, leaned in.
“You’re right. I ought not to have come today,” he murmured, low enough that the coachman wouldn’t hear.
She bent forward, as if to speak. But he already called out, “Drive on.”
The carriage moved away. He stood alone on the corner. The snow had stopped. A cold wind lashed his face. Suddenly, he felt something stiff and icy on his lashes. He realized—he’d been crying. The wind had frozen his tears.
He thrust his hands into his pockets and walked briskly down Fifth Avenue, toward home.

That evening, when Archer descended the stairs before dinner, he found the drawing-room empty. He and May were dining alone—every family engagement having been postponed since Mrs. Manson Mingott’s illness—and though May was usually the more punctual of the two, he was surprised she hadn’t arrived first. He knew she was home; during his dressing, he had heard her moving quietly in her room. Yet something had detained her. He had grown accustomed to such small mysteries, using them as anchors to keep his thoughts grounded in reality. Sometimes, he felt he understood his father-in-law’s obsession with trifles: perhaps Mr. Welland, long ago, had known dreams too vivid to bear, and so built a fortress of routine to shield himself from them.

When May finally appeared, she looked weary. She wore the low-necked, tightly laced dress required by Mingott tradition—even for informal evenings—and her fair hair was coiled into its usual elaborate style. But her face was pale, almost washed out. Still, she greeted him with her familiar warmth, her eyes holding that same bright blue sparkle from the day before.

“What happened to you, dear?” she asked. “I was waiting at Granny’s. Ellen came alone and said she’d dropped you on the way because you had urgent business. Is everything all right?”

“Just some letters I forgot,” he replied. “Wanted to send them off before dinner.”

“Oh,” she said softly, then after a pause, “I’m sorry you didn’t come to Granny’s—unless they really were urgent.”

“They were,” he answered, startled by her concern. “Besides, I didn’t know you were there.”

She turned toward the mirror above the mantel, lifting one arm to adjust a loose puff in her hair. As she stood there, Archer noticed a stiffness in her posture, a kind of quiet fatigue. Was the endless rhythm of their life beginning to weigh on her too? Then he remembered: earlier that morning, as he left the house, she had called down the stairs that she would meet him at her grandmother’s so they could return together. He had cheerfully replied, “Yes!” but once absorbed in his own thoughts, he had forgotten entirely.

Now, guilt pricked him—but also irritation. How could such a small oversight be held against him after nearly two years of marriage? He was tired of living in a perpetual, tepid honeymoon—no fire, no passion, yet all the expectations of love. If May had voiced her frustrations (and he suspected she harbored many), he might have laughed them away. But she had been trained to hide pain behind a smile, to wear sorrow like a well-tailored dress.

To mask his annoyance, he asked about her grandmother.

“She’s still improving,” May said, “but she was quite upset by the latest news about the Beauforts.”

“The Beauforts?” he echoed.

“It seems they’re staying in New York. He’s going into insurance or something. They’re looking for a small house.”

The absurdity of it needed no comment. They went in to dinner.

Their conversation remained within its usual narrow circle. But Archer noticed May made no mention of Madame Olenska—or of old Catherine’s reception of her. He was grateful, yet oddly uneasy. After dinner, they moved to the library for coffee. Archer lit a cigar and pulled down a volume of Michelet. Since May had begun asking him to read aloud whenever he picked up poetry—something he now found stifling—he had taken to history instead. Not that he disliked his voice, but he could predict her reactions: thoughtful, sometimes critical, always dissecting what he read. During their engagement, she had simply reflected his opinions back to him. Now, without his guidance, she offered her own—often ruining the pleasure of the work itself.

Seeing he’d chosen history, she fetched her embroidery basket, pulled an armchair close to the green-shaded lamp, and uncovered a cushion she was stitching for his sofa. She wasn’t skilled with needlework—her hands were strong, meant for riding and rowing—but she did it anyway. Other wives embroidered cushions; she wouldn’t be left behind in the ritual of devotion.

As she sat, her head bent over the frame, her sleeves slipping back from her firm arms, the betrothal sapphire glinting beside her gold wedding band, Archer watched her in silence. The lamplight caught her clear brow, and suddenly he felt a pang of dread: he would always know what lay beneath it. Never again would she surprise him—with a new thought, a hidden emotion, a flicker of rebellion. Her romance had been spent in their brief courtship; now it was gone, used up. She was becoming her mother—quiet, dutiful, composed—and in doing so, somehow trying to turn him into Mr. Welland.

He set the book aside and rose abruptly. At once, she looked up.

“What’s wrong?”

“The room feels stifling. I need some air.”

He had insisted the library curtains slide open and closed on a rod—unlike the fixed, lace-draped ones in the drawing-room. Now he pulled them back and raised the sash, leaning out into the cold night. The simple act of not seeing her—of looking beyond the house, across rooftops and chimneys, into other lives, other cities—cleared his mind. For a moment, he breathed freely.

After a few minutes, she spoke.

“Newland! Shut the window. You’ll catch your death.”

He lowered the sash and turned back.

“Catch my death?” he repeated, half-laughing. And he almost added: *But I already have. I’m dead—been dead for months.*

Then, like lightning, a wild idea struck him: What if it were *she* who died? If she passed soon—suddenly—wouldn’t that free him?

The thought was shocking, intoxicating. To stand here, in this warm, familiar room, staring at her, wishing her gone—it felt both monstrous and thrilling. His soul, sick with longing, clung to the possibility. Yes, May could die. Young people died. Healthy people like her. She might die—and leave him unchained.

She glanced up, sensing something strange in his expression.

“Newland… are you ill?”

He shook his head and walked toward his chair. She returned to her embroidery, and as he passed, he touched her hair.

“Poor May,” he murmured.

“Poor? Why?” she asked, laughing faintly.

“Because I’ll never be able to open a window without worrying you.”

She smiled, but it was strained.

“I’ll never worry—if you’re happy.”

“And I’ll never be happy unless I can open the windows.”

“In *this* weather?” she protested.

He sighed and buried his face in his book.

Days passed. No word from Madame Olenska. Her name was avoided in conversation. Archer didn’t try to see her—not while she stayed at old Catherine’s side. In the uncertainty, he drifted, yet beneath it all, a decision had formed—the one he’d made leaning into the icy night.

It gave him strength. He waited. He did nothing.

Then May told him Mrs. Manson Mingott wished to see him.

It wasn’t unusual—Catherine was recovering, and she had always favored Archer above the others. May delivered the message with pride; she loved knowing her grandmother admired her husband.

There was a pause. Then Archer said, “All right. Shall we go together this afternoon?”

Her face lit up—but instantly she replied, “Oh, you’d better go alone. Granny gets bored seeing the same people too often.”

His heart pounded. He had wanted to go alone precisely for this reason: to speak privately with Ellen. He had waited for the chance—now it was here.

Behind the door, in the yellow damask room beside the hall, she must be waiting. In moments, he’d see her. Just one question—he needed only that. When would she return to Washington? A simple question, impossible to refuse.

But when he entered, it was the mulatto maid who met him. Her white teeth gleaming like piano keys, she slid open the doors and led him to the old woman.

Mrs. Mingott sat in a throne-like armchair near her bed. Beside her, a mahogany stand held a bronze lamp with a glass globe shaded in green paper. No books, no knitting—only conversation had ever mattered to her. She seemed unchanged, save for paleness and deeper shadows under her massive form. Her fluted mob-cap, tied with a starched bow between her chins, and her purple dressing-gown draped with muslin, made her look like a wise, indulgent ancestor.

She held out a small hand—delicate, resting in the hollow of her lap like a pet—and called to the maid: “Don’t let anyone else in. If my daughters come, say I’m asleep.”

The maid vanished. Mrs. Mingott turned to Archer.

“My dear, am I perfectly hideous?” she asked gaily, gesturing toward her chest. “My daughters say it doesn’t matter at my age—as if hideousness matters less the harder it is to hide!”

“You’re handsomer than ever,” he replied, matching her tone.

She threw back her head and laughed. “Ah, but not as handsome as Ellen!” she teased, twinkling at him. “Was she awfully beautiful the day you drove her up from the ferry?”

He laughed. She continued: “Was it because you told her so that she had to put you out on the way? In my youth, young men didn’t abandon pretty women unless they were forced!”

She chuckled, then added, almost plaintively: “It’s a pity she didn’t marry you. I always told her so. It would’ve spared me all this trouble. But who ever thought of sparing their grandmother worry?”

Archer wondered if illness had dulled her mind. Then she burst out:

“Well, it’s settled—she’s staying with me, whatever the rest say! She hadn’t been here five minutes before I’d have fallen to my knees to keep her—if only I’d known where the floor was these past twenty years!”

He listened in silence.

“She’d talked me over,” she went on. “Lovell, Letterblair, Augusta Welland—all of them. They said I must cut off her allowance until she saw her duty to return to Olenski. Even the secretary brought forth proposals—handsome ones, I admit. Marriage is marriage, and money is money—both useful things... and I didn’t know what to say—”

She paused, breathing deeply.

“But the minute I saw her, I said: ‘You sweet bird! Shut you up in that cage again? Never!’ And now it’s decided: she’ll stay and nurse her Granny as long as there’s a Granny to nurse. It’s not gay, but she doesn’t mind. And I’ve told Letterblair she’ll get her proper allowance.”

Archer felt his blood surge. But confusion clouded his joy. He had planned one path—now, the world shifted.

Yet slowly, a deep relief took hold. Difficulties delayed. Opportunities granted. If Ellen chose to stay, it must mean she couldn’t give him up. This was her answer—her refusal to take the final step, but a yielding to something in between.

He sank into the comfort of safety—dangerously sweet.

“She couldn’t have gone back,” he exclaimed. “It was impossible!”

“Oh, my dear,” she said, “I always knew you were on her side. That’s why I sent for you today—and why I told your lovely wife, when she offered to come: ‘No, my dear, I’m pining to see Newland. I don’t want anyone sharing our joy.’ Because you see—” she leaned back as far as her chins allowed, locking eyes with him—“you see, we’ll still have a fight. The family doesn’t want her here. They’ll say it’s because I’m weak, because I’m ill. But I’m not strong enough to face them yet. You must do it for me.”

“For me?” he stammered.

“You. Why not?” she snapped, her round eyes sharp as blades. Her hand darted from the armrest and gripped his—tiny, pale nails like bird claws. “Why not?”

Under her gaze, he regained composure.

“Oh, I don’t count. I’m too insignificant.”

“Well, you’re Letterblair’s partner, aren’t you? You’ve got to reach them through him. Unless you’ve got a reason,” she pressed.

“Oh, my dear,” he said gently, “I believe you can stand alone. But I’ll help if you need it.”

“Then we’re safe,” she sighed, smiling with ancient cunning. “I always knew you’d support us—because they never quote you when they talk about her duty to go home.”

He winced. He longed to ask: “And May—do they quote her?”

But he held back.

“And Madame Olenska?” he said instead. “When may I see her?”

The old lady chuckled, crinkling her eyes, playing the part of mischief.

“Not today. One at a time, please. Madame Olenska has gone out.”

He flushed with disappointment.

“She’s gone out,” she continued, “in my carriage to visit Regina Beaufort.”

She paused, letting the words sink in.

“That’s what she’s reduced me to already. The day after she arrived, she put on her best bonnet and told me, cool as a cucumber, that she was going to call on Regina Beaufort. ‘I don’t know her,’ I said. ‘Who is she?’ ‘She’s your grand-niece,’ she replied. ‘A most unhappy woman.’ ‘She’s the wife of a scoundrel,’ I said. ‘Well,’ she answered, ‘so am I—and yet all my family want me to go back to him.’”

She stopped, then added: “That floored me. I let her go. Then one day she said it was raining too hard to walk, and she wanted to borrow my carriage. ‘For what?’ I asked. ‘To see cousin Regina’—_cousin_! I looked out the window. It wasn’t raining a drop. But I understood. I gave her the carriage…”

She paused, then said softly: “After all, Regina’s a brave woman. And so is she. I’ve always liked courage above all.”

Archer bent down and kissed the small hand still resting on his.

“Eh-eh-eh!” the old lady snapped, mocking him. “Whose hand did you think you were kissing, young man? Your wife’s, I hope?”

As he rose to leave, she called after him: “Give her my love. But don’t tell her about our talk.”

XXXI. Archer stood frozen, the news from old Catherine still echoing in his mind like a thunderclap. It was natural enough that Madame Olenska had rushed to her grandmother’s side when summoned—she had always been devoted to Mrs. Mingott. But that she would choose to stay, especially now that the older woman was recovering, puzzled him deeply. He knew full well that Ellen’s financial situation hadn’t improved. Her husband had granted her only a meager allowance after their separation, and without Mrs. Mingott’s support, it wouldn’t have sustained even a modest existence by New York standards. And now, with Medora Manson—her companion and confidante—ruined, the two women were left with barely enough to survive.

Yet Archer didn’t believe money had driven her decision. He knew Ellen too well. She carried herself with the careless grace of someone who’d never known want, whose taste for luxury came not from necessity but habit. She could live simply when needed, though her relatives often remarked on how little she cared about appearances or “how things were done.” Still, he remembered: months had passed since her allowance ended, and yet she hadn’t once reached out to reconcile with her family. So why now?

The answer was clear, if painful. On the ferry ride back from Jersey City, she had whispered that they must remain apart—her head resting against his chest, her breath warm on his skin. He knew those words weren’t meant to charm or provoke; they were born of desperation. She was fighting a war no one else could see, clinging to the idea that they could honor their obligations, preserve dignity, and still hold onto each other. But perhaps, in the ten days since her return, she had sensed his silence. The absence of visits, the lack of urgency—these might have signaled to her that he was preparing to act, to make a choice that couldn’t be undone. And fear, cold and sudden, might have seized her then. Not fear of losing him, but of losing herself—of becoming just another woman caught in a secret life, half-truths, and silent betrayals.

Earlier that evening, as he rang Mrs. Mingott’s bell, Archer had felt certain of his path. He intended to speak alone with Ellen, or at least learn when she was leaving for Washington. Then he would follow her—not just to Washington, but wherever she went. His heart leaned toward Japan, a distant dreamland where they could begin anew. He planned to leave a note for May—final, decisive, shutting down any possibility of retreat. He believed himself ready, even eager, for this leap into the unknown.

But when he heard that Ellen had decided to stay, his first emotion wasn’t anger or sorrow—it was relief. A strange, quiet relief. Yet as he walked home through the winter night, that relief curdled into something heavier. He realized he was no longer excited by the idea of running away. The path ahead wasn’t unfamiliar—he’d seen others walk it before, men who played the game of love and duty with practiced hands. They called it “protecting a woman’s honor,” a phrase he’d learned from novels and dinner-table talk. He’d watched Sillerton Jackson do it with quiet pride, watching Mrs. Thorley Rushworth smile through lies, laugh through pretense, maintain a perfect façade while living a double life.

Now, standing in the cold dark, he saw the truth: that role was not one he could wear lightly. It demanded constant deception—a lie in every glance, every touch, every word. A lie that would stretch across years, poisoning joy and turning intimacy into performance. Women could play such roles more easily, society allowed them more leeway. They could claim nerves, moods, exhaustion. Men, however, were judged harshly. In Archer’s world, a man who continued affairs after marriage was despised. There was a season for wild oats—but only one.

He had always believed this. He thought Lefferts contemptible. But loving Ellen Olenska changed everything. She was unlike any other woman. He was unlike any other man. Their situation was unique, unbound by convention. They owed allegiance not to society, but to themselves. And yet… tomorrow he would step through his own door. There would be May, waiting. Habit, duty, honor—everything he’d ever believed in would stand between him and the future he dreamed of.

At the corner of Fifth Avenue, he paused. Ahead loomed a great house, dark and silent, its windows shuttered save for one faint glow upstairs. He remembered it lit up, alive with music and laughter—the place where he’d kissed May for the first time, under a canopy of candles, where she’d appeared like a goddess of silver light. Now it was empty, almost tomblike. And there, at the door, stood Mrs. Mingott’s carriage.

A flicker of irony crossed his mind—what a chance for gossip if Sillerton Jackson happened by! But he pushed it aside. He’d been deeply moved by Catherine’s account of Ellen’s visit to Mrs. Beaufort. It made the moral outrage of New York seem petty, almost cruel. Still, he knew what people would say. That Ellen was flirting with scandal, playing games with the elite. Rumors already whispered that Beaufort had fled to Fanny Ring. But Ellen’s behavior suggested otherwise—she was choosing restraint, not recklessness.

He looked up at the window. Perhaps the two women sat together, talking quietly. Beaufort had likely gone elsewhere. As he approached, the door opened—and she stepped out.

“Ellen,” he said softly, his voice barely above a whisper.

She stopped, startled. Behind her, a dim light guided her down the steps. Two young men approached from the opposite side of the street—Lefferts and Chivers, stylish and polished, heading to a theater party. For a moment, Archer felt a pang of shame—didn’t she know the danger of being seen? But then her hand touched his, warm and real, and all hesitation vanished.

“I shall see you now—we shall be together,” he blurted, his words tumbling out.

“Oh,” she replied. “Granny told you?”

They stood in silence, the city breathing around them. The two young men had turned away, discreetly crossing the avenue. That kind of complicity—men protecting secrets, shielding reputations—was familiar to Archer. But now it repulsed him.

Could they really live like this? Was this all she imagined? He couldn’t bear the thought.

“Tomorrow,” he insisted, “we must meet—somewhere we can be alone.”

She hesitated, glancing toward the carriage. “I’ll be at Granny’s—for now,” she added, as if needing to justify her change of plan.

“Somewhere alone,” he repeated, his voice sharp with frustration.

She laughed faintly, a sound that grated on him. “In New York? There are no churches… no monuments.”

“The Art Museum,” he said quickly. “In the Park. Half-past two. I’ll be at the door.”

She turned without answering, climbed into the carriage, and as it rolled away, he thought he saw her wave—once, briefly, into the dark.

He stared after her, torn between hope and despair. It felt as though he’d spoken to a stranger—a woman who gave pleasure but offered nothing more. He despised the worn-out phrases, the hollow promises. “She’ll come,” he muttered, almost bitterly.

At the museum, they avoided the crowded galleries and wandered into the quiet room housing the Cesnola antiquities—broken shards of glass, clay, bronze, relics of forgotten lives. No one else was there. They sat on the divan beside the radiator, facing the glass cases filled with fragments of Ilium.

“It’s odd,” Ellen said, “I’ve never been here before.”

“Ah, well—some day it may become a great museum.”

She stood and drifted across the room. Archer watched her—how the furs draped her slender figure, the delicate curl framing her cheek, the way the veil framed her face like a mask. He was lost in the details that made her uniquely herself.

Then he rose and joined her at the case.

“It seems cruel,” she murmured, “that after a while nothing matters—just like these little things, once so important to people long dead, now labeled ‘Use unknown.’”

“Yes,” he said. “But meanwhile…”

“Meanwhile…” she echoed.

She stood there—long coat, muff, veil drawn low, violets trembling in her breath. To him, she seemed untouched by time, pure harmony of form and color. Impossible to imagine she could ever fade.

“Meanwhile, everything matters—that concerns you,” he said.

She looked at him, thoughtful, then returned to the divan. He sat beside her, waiting. But soon, a distant footstep echoed through the empty halls. A guard in braided cap passed slowly, ghost-like, through the rooms of mummies and sarcophagi.

“What is it you wanted to tell me?” she asked, as if sensing the same urgency.

“What I wanted to tell you?” he replied. “That I believe you came to New York because you were afraid.”

“Afraid?”

“Afraid of my coming to Washington.”

She lowered her eyes, fingers twitching in her muff. “Well?”

“Well—yes,” she admitted. “You knew?”

“Yes,” he said. “I knew.”

“So… this is better, isn’t it?”

“Better?” he echoed.

“We’ll hurt fewer people. Isn’t that what you always wanted?”

“To have you here—close, but not too close? To meet in secret, on the sly? That’s the opposite of what I want. I told you what I wanted.”

She hesitated. “And you still think this—worse?”

“A thousand times worse.” He paused. “It would be easy to lie to you. But the truth is—I think it detestable.”

“Oh, so do I!” she breathed, relieved.

He sprang up. “Then why—what do you think is better?”

She bowed her head, clenching and unclenching her hands. The guard passed again, vanishing into the shadows.

Instead of answering, she whispered, “I promised Granny I’d stay because it seemed safer here.”

“Safer from me?”

“Safer from loving you?”

Her profile didn’t move. But a tear slipped from her lashes, catching in the mesh of her veil.

“Safer from doing irreparable harm. Don’t let us be like all the others!”

“What others? I’m not claiming to be different. I feel the same wants, the same longing.”

She glanced at him, a flush rising in her cheeks. “Shall I—once come to you… and then go home?”

The blood surged to his forehead. “Dearest,” he whispered, unmoving.

It felt as though he held his heart in his hands, fragile and full. Then her last words struck him.

“Go home? What do you mean by going home?”

“Home to my husband.”

“And you expect me to say yes to that?”

She raised her eyes, troubled. “What else is there? I can’t stay here and lie to those who’ve helped me rebuild my life.”

“But that’s exactly why I ask you to come away!”

“And destroy their lives? When they’ve given me mine?”

Archer stood, staring down at her, speechless with despair. He could have said yes—“Come, just once.” He knew the power she would give him. Persuading her to stay would have been easy. But something stopped him. Her honesty, her refusal to play the game—made it impossible to lure her into the trap of temporary escape.

“If I let her come,” he thought, “I must let her go again.” And that was unthinkable.

But he saw the tear on her cheek, and wavered.

“After all,” he began again, “we have our own lives. There’s no use pretending the impossible. You’re so open-minded about some things, so used to facing the Gorgon—why are you afraid to look at our case honestly? Unless you think the sacrifice isn’t worth it.”

She stood, lips tightening. “Call it that—then I must go.” She pulled out her watch.

As she turned to leave, he caught her wrist.

“Then come to me once,” he said, his voice breaking. “Just once.”

For a moment, they stared at each other—like enemies, yet bound by love.

“When?” he pressed.

“Tomorrow?”

She hesitated. “The day after.”

“Dearest—”

She freed her wrist, but they held each other’s gaze. Her face had grown pale, yet radiated an inner fire. He felt awe—this was love made visible.

“Oh, I shall be late—goodbye. Don’t come farther,” she said, hurrying away.

At the door, she turned, waved once, and disappeared.

Archer walked home alone. Darkness fell as he entered his house. He looked around the hall as if seeing it from beyond death. The parlour-maid ran up to light the gas.

“Is Mrs. Archer in?”

“No, sir. She went out after lunch and hasn’t returned.”

Relief washed over him. He entered the library, sank into his chair, and sat motionless—elbows on knees, chin in hands, eyes fixed on the dying fire.

He sat in silence, unaware of time, consumed by a deep, grave amazement. “This was what had to be… this was what had to be,” he repeated, as if chained to fate.

The dream he’d held—of escape, of freedom—had been shattered. The rapture was tainted with chill.

The door opened. May stepped in.

“I’m dreadfully late—were you worried?”

She placed her hand on his shoulder, rare and tender.

“Is it late?” he asked.

“After seven. I thought you were asleep.”

She laughed, tossed her hat on the sofa. Pale, but animated, her eyes bright.

“I went to Granny’s. Just as I was leaving, Ellen came in from a walk. So I stayed. We talked—really talked. It’s been ages…”

She settled into her chair, smoothing her hair.

“She was so dear—just like the old Ellen. I haven’t been fair to her lately. I’ve sometimes thought—”

Archer rose, leaning against the mantelpiece, out of the lamp’s reach.

“Yes, you’ve thought—?”

“Well, perhaps I haven’t judged her fairly. She’s so different—on the surface. She takes up odd people. She likes to stand out. Probably from her European life. We must seem dull to her. But I don’t want to judge unfairly.”

She paused, breathless. Her cheeks flushed.

He saw the effort in her—like that moment in St. Augustine, when she’d reached beyond herself.

“She hates Ellen,” he thought. “And she’s trying to overcome it. Trying to win me over.”

The thought stirred him. For a moment, he nearly confessed everything.

“You understand, don’t you,” she continued, “why the family has been annoyed? We did what we could. But she never seemed to grasp it. And now—going to Mrs. Beaufort, in Granny’s carriage! I’m afraid she’s alienated the van der Luydens…”

“Ah,” Archer said, with a weary laugh.

The door had closed again.

“It’s time to dress. We’re dining out, aren’t we?”

She rose, lingered near the fire. As he passed, she stepped forward—eyes meeting, blue and swimming, like the ones he’d left behind.

She threw her arms around his neck, pressed her cheek to his.

“You haven’t kissed me today,” she whispered.

And he felt her tremble in his arms.

XXXII. "At the court of the Tuileries," said Mr. Sillerton Jackson, smiling with the air of one recalling a distant, slightly scandalous past, "such things were pretty openly tolerated." The words hung in the quiet air of the van der Luydens’ black walnut dining room on Madison Avenue, where the evening light fell softly over polished silver and delicate porcelain. It was the night after Newland Archer’s visit to the Museum of Art—a quiet moment between moments, when the city still hummed beneath its surface of propriety.

Mr. and Mrs. van der Luyden had returned from Skuytercliff, their country retreat, summoned back by the sudden collapse of Beaufort’s fortune. The news had sent shockwaves through society, and it was felt—by those who mattered—that the old guard must reassert themselves. “It will never do,” Mrs. Archer had said firmly, “to let people like Mrs. Lemuel Struthers think they can step into Regina’s shoes.” And so, with solemn duty in their hearts, the van der Luydens had come to town, reopened their doors, and issued invitations for dinners and receptions, as if the world could be restored simply by proper attendance at the Opera.

That evening, they were hosting a small gathering: Sillerton Jackson, Mrs. Archer, Newland, and his wife May. They would go together to see *Faust*, newly performed that winter. The dinner began precisely at seven, as always—no matter how few the guests—so that each course might unfold with measured grace before the men settled into cigars. Archer had not seen May since the previous night. He’d left early for the office, buried in routine work, then detained by an unexpected visit from a senior partner. By the time he arrived home, May had already gone ahead to the van der Luydens’, sending the carriage back without him.

Now, across the table, she looked pale and tired, yet her eyes sparkled with a forced brightness. She spoke quickly, almost too animatedly, as though trying to fill the silence with sound. The conversation had turned—perhaps deliberately—to the Beaufort affair, a topic still fresh in the drawing rooms of New York. After a thorough moral reckoning, Mrs. van der Luyden turned to May with a look of mild inquiry. “Is it possible, dear, that what I’ve heard is true? I was told your grandmother Mingott’s carriage was seen at Mrs. Beaufort’s door.”

There was a subtle shift in tone—the name “Mrs. Beaufort” now spoken without familiarity. May flushed. Mrs. Archer stepped in swiftly. “If it was, I’m certain it was without Mrs. Mingott’s knowledge.” But Mrs. van der Luyden paused, sighed, and glanced toward her husband. “I’m afraid,” he said gently, “Madame Olenska’s kind heart may have led her into imprudence—calling on Mrs. Beaufort.”

“Or her taste for peculiar people,” Mrs. Archer added dryly, her gaze lingering on her son.

“I’m sorry to think it of Madame Olenska,” Mrs. van der Luyden murmured. Mrs. Archer replied softly, “Ah, my dear—and after you’d had her twice at Skuytercliff!”

And then, as if waiting for this moment, Mr. Jackson leaned forward. “At the Tuileries,” he repeated, his voice low but carrying, “the standard was excessively lax in some respects. If you’d asked where Morny’s money came from—or who paid the debts of some of the Court beauties…” He let the implication hang.

“I hope, dear Sillerton,” Mrs. Archer interjected, “you’re not suggesting we adopt such standards?”

“I never suggest,” Jackson returned, unruffled. “But Madame Olenska’s foreign upbringing may make her less particular—”

“Ahh,” the two older women sighed, exchanging glances.

“Still,” Mr. van der Luyden murmured, “to have kept her grandmother’s carriage at a defaulter’s door!” He seemed to regret something unspoken—perhaps the hampers of carnations he’d once sent to the little house on Twenty-third Street.

“Of course,” Mrs. Archer said, “I’ve always said she looks at things quite differently.”

A flush rose in May’s cheeks. She turned to her husband. “I’m sure Ellen meant it kindly.”

“Imprudent people are often kind,” Mrs. Archer observed, as if kindness did not excuse folly.

“If only she had consulted someone,” Mrs. van der Luyden whispered.

“Ah, but she never does!” Mrs. Archer replied sharply.

At that, Mr. van der Luyden glanced at his wife, who inclined her head slightly toward Mrs. Archer. Then, with silent understanding, the three ladies rose and swept out of the room, their trains glimmering behind them, leaving the men to their cigars.

Archer, after the first act at the Opera, slipped away from the group and found himself standing at the back of the club box. From there, he watched the stage—the same scene he had seen two years earlier, the night he first met Ellen Olenska. He half-expected to see her in her grandmother’s box, but it remained empty. He sat motionless, staring at the vacant seat, until suddenly Madame Nilsson’s pure soprano rang out: “*M’ama, non m’ama…*”

He turned to the stage, where the familiar tableau unfolded: the large blonde Marguerite yielding to the small brown Faust, surrounded by giant roses and pansies painted like pen-wipers. His eyes drifted to the horseshoe-shaped balcony, where May sat between two older ladies—just as she had that night, between Mrs. Lovell Mingott and her foreign cousin. She wore white again—white as snow, just as she had on her betrothal evening. Archer hadn’t noticed her dress at first, but now he recognized the blue-white satin and lace of her wedding gown. In old New York, brides wore it for the first year or two of marriage. His mother still kept hers wrapped in tissue paper, hoping Janey might one day wear it—even though poor Janey was nearly grown, and pearl grey poplin was now considered more appropriate.

Archer realized, with a pang, that May had rarely worn this dress since their return from Europe. Seeing her in it now stirred something deep within him. Her figure had filled out slightly, as her graceful build had predicted, but her posture remained upright, her expression still girlish and transparent—except for a new softness, a faint languor he had lately noticed. Without that, she was exactly the girl he had watched that night, playing with her bouquet of lilies-of-the-valley, full of innocent joy.

The sight moved him—not just with nostalgia, but with pity. Such innocence was fragile, like a child’s trust. And then he remembered—her quiet strength, the depth beneath her calm. He recalled her glance when he had insisted their engagement be announced at the Beaufort ball; he heard again her voice in the Mission garden: “I couldn’t have my happiness made out of a wrong—a wrong to someone else.” A longing surged through him, uncontrollable: to tell her the truth, to throw himself on her mercy, to ask for the freedom he had once refused.

Newland Archer was not a man given to drama. Conformity was his nature, shaped by years of living within the strict rules of society. To act impulsively, to break form—was unthinkable. Yet now, the walls of convention crumbled. He walked along the curved passage behind the theater and opened the door of Mrs. van der Luyden’s box as if stepping into a world unknown.

“*M’ama!*” sang Marguerite triumphantly. The occupants looked up in surprise as Archer entered—breaking one of the unwritten laws: no one entered a box during a solo.

He slipped between Mr. van der Luyden and Jackson, leaned over his wife. “I’ve got a beastly headache,” he whispered. “Don’t tell anyone, but come home, won’t you?”

May gave him a glance—understanding, quiet. She whispered to her mother, who nodded sympathetically. Then she murmured an excuse to Mrs. van der Luyden and stood just as Marguerite sank into Faust’s arms.

As Archer helped her on with her opera cloak, he saw the older ladies exchange a knowing smile.

They drove home in silence. May laid her hand gently on his arm. “I’m so sorry you don’t feel well. I’m afraid they’ve been overworking you again.”

“No—it’s not that,” he replied, confused. “Do you mind if I open the window?” He lowered the pane on his side.

He stared out at the passing lights, feeling her presence beside him like a silent question. At the door, she caught her skirt on the step and stumbled against him.

“Did you hurt yourself?” he asked, steadying her.

“No—but my poor dress—see how I’ve torn it!” she exclaimed, bending to gather up the mud-stained fabric.

They climbed the stairs. The servants hadn’t expected them so early—only a dim gaslight flickered on the landing. Archer lit the brackets on either side of the library mantel. The curtains were drawn, and the room glowed warmly, like a familiar face seen in secret. He noticed how pale she was.

“Should I get you some brandy?” he asked.

“Oh, no,” she said, flushing slightly as she removed her cloak. “But hadn’t you better go to bed?”

He opened a silver case and took out a cigarette. Then, abruptly, he dropped it. “No—my head isn’t that bad.” He paused. “There’s something I want to say. Something important—I must tell you now.”

She sat down in an armchair, lifting her head. “Yes, dear?”

Her voice was so gentle, so untroubled, that he wondered at her composure.

“May—” he began, standing a few feet away, as if distance could protect him. “There’s something I’ve got to tell you… about myself…”

She remained still, her lashes unmoving. Pale, yes—but her face held a strange serenity, as if she drew strength from somewhere deep within.

Archer stopped himself from speaking the usual words of guilt and apology. He wanted to speak plainly, without excuses. “Madame Olenska—” he started.

But at the name, she raised her hand—softly, as if to stop him.

The gaslight caught the gold of her wedding ring.

“Oh, why should we talk about Ellen tonight?” she asked, with a slight pout of impatience.

“Because I ought to have spoken before.”

Her face stayed calm. “Is it really worth while, dear? I know I’ve been unfair to her at times—perhaps we all have. You’ve understood her better than we did. You’ve always been kind to her. But what does it matter, now it’s all over?”

He stared at her, stunned. “All over—what do you mean?”

She looked at him with clear, steady eyes. “Why—since she’s going back to Europe so soon. Since Granny approves and understands, and has arranged for her to live independently—she’ll be with Aunt Medora, who’s coming with her. She’s hurrying back to Washington to pack. We sail next week.”

She paused. “I suppose you were kept at the office this evening about the arrangements. It was settled this morning, I believe.”

She lowered her eyes under his gaze, and a fleeting blush crossed her cheeks.

He realized then—his own eyes must be unbearable. Turning away, he rested his elbows on the mantelpiece and covered his face.

Something drummed inside his head—blood? Or the clock ticking on the shelf?

May sat motionless. The clock marked five minutes.

A lump of coal shifted in the grate. She rose to push it back.

Archer turned slowly to face her.

“It’s impossible,” he said.

“Impossible—?”

“How do you know—what you just told me?”

“I saw Ellen yesterday. I told you I’d seen her at Granny’s.”

“It wasn’t then she told you?”

“No. I had a note from her this afternoon. Do you want to see it?”

He couldn’t speak. She left the room and returned almost at once.

“I thought you knew,” she said simply.

She placed a sheet of paper on the table. He picked it up.

It read:

> *May dear, I have at last made Granny understand that my visit to her could be no more than a visit; and she has been as kind and generous as ever. She sees now that if I return to Europe I must live by myself—or rather with poor Aunt Medora, who is coming with me. I am hurrying back to Washington to pack up, and we sail next week. You must be very good to Granny when I’m gone—as good as you’ve always been to me. Ellen.*
>
> *If any of my friends wish to urge me to change my mind, please tell them it would be utterly useless.*

Archer read it twice, then flung it down and burst out laughing.

The sound startled him—like Janey’s midnight fright when he’d rocked with laughter over May’s telegram announcing their marriage date had been advanced.

“Why did she write this?” he asked, forcing the laugh down.

May met his eyes with her quiet honesty. “I suppose because we talked things over yesterday—”

“What things?”

“I told her I was afraid I hadn’t been fair to her—hadn’t always understood how hard it must have been for her here, alone among people who were relations but strangers, who felt free to judge but didn’t know the truth.”

She paused. “I knew you’d been the one friend she could count on. I wanted her to know that you and I—we’re the same—in all our feelings.”

She hesitated, as if waiting for him to respond. Then she added slowly, “She understood my wanting to tell her this. I think she understands everything.”

She stepped toward him, took his cold hand, and pressed it gently against her cheek.

“My head aches too,” she said softly. “Good-night, dear.”

Then she turned and walked to the door, her torn and muddy wedding dress trailing behind her across the floor.

It was, as Mrs. Archer smiled to Mrs. Welland, a momentous occasion for a young couple to host their first grand dinner.

Since the Newland Archers had established their household, they’d entertained informally—Archer fond of inviting a few friends for supper, May welcoming them with the same warm grace her mother had shown in her own marriage. He often wondered if she would have invited anyone at all without tradition’s guidance, but long ago he’d stopped trying to uncover the real woman beneath the carefully shaped façade society had molded her into. It was expected that well-to-do young couples in New York should entertain freely, and a Welland married to an Archer carried double the weight of expectation.

But this dinner—this was different. With a hired chef, borrowed footmen, Roman punch, roses from Henderson’s, and menus printed on gilt-edged cards—it was not merely a gathering, but a declaration. As Mrs. Archer observed, it was the Roman punch that truly made the difference—not because of the drink itself, but because it signaled everything else: canvas-backs or terrapin, two soups, a hot and cold dessert, decolletage and short sleeves, and guests of proper standing.

There was always something thrilling about a young couple sending out their first formal invitations in the third person. Few refused, even among the most seasoned and sought-after. Yet it was still considered a triumph when the van der Luydens, at May’s request, delayed their departure just to attend Ellen Olenska’s farewell dinner.

The two mothers-in-law sat in May’s drawing room on the afternoon of the event. Mrs. Archer wrote out the menus on thick, gold-edged Tiffany paper, while Mrs. Welland supervised the placement of palms and standard lamps.

Archer arrived late from his office to find them still there. His mother-in-law had moved on to name-cards for the table, and Mrs. Welland was considering whether to reposition the large gilt sofa to create another conversational corner between the piano and the window.

May, they told him, was in the dining room, inspecting the mound of Jacqueminot roses and maidenhair ferns at the center of the long table, and checking the arrangement of Maillard bonbons in openwork silver baskets between the candelabras. On the piano sat a large basket of orchids sent by Mr. van der Luyden from Skuytercliff. Everything, in short, was exactly as it should be on the eve of such an important evening.

Mrs. Archer ran her eye over the guest list, ticking off names with her sharp gold pen.

"Henry van der Luyden—Louisa—the Lovell Mingotts—the Reggie Chiverses—Lawrence Lefferts and Gertrude—(yes, I suppose May was right to include them)—the Selfridge Merrys, Sillerton Jackson, Van Newland and his wife. (How time flies! It seems only yesterday you were my best man, Newland)—and Countess Olenska—yes, I believe that’s everyone."

Mrs. Welland looked at her son-in-law with affection. "No one can say, Newland, that you and May aren’t giving Ellen a fitting send-off."

"Ah," said Mrs. Archer, "I understand May wants her cousin to tell people abroad we’re not quite savages."

"I’m certain Ellen will appreciate it," Mrs. Welland replied. "She was supposed to arrive this morning. It’ll make a lovely final impression. The night before sailing is usually so dreary."

Archer turned toward the door, and his mother-in-law called after him: "Do go see the table. And don’t let May overexert herself." But he pretended not to hear, and hurried up the stairs to his library. The room stared back at him like a stranger’s face, politely composed. It had been ruthlessly tidied—ash trays placed strategically, cedar boxes arranged neatly—as if ready for gentlemen to smoke.

"Ah, well," he thought, "it won’t last long—" and walked on to his dressing room.

Ten days had passed since Madame Olenska left New York. In that time, Archer had received no word from her—only the return of a key wrapped in tissue paper, delivered to his office in a sealed envelope bearing her handwriting. This quiet refusal might have seemed like a familiar move in a game they both knew, but he chose to interpret it differently. She was still resisting her fate. She was going to Europe. And she wasn’t returning to her husband. That meant nothing stood in the way of his following her. Once he took that step—once he proved it irrevocable—he believed she wouldn’t turn him away.

This belief gave him strength. It kept him silent, steady, unbroken. He didn’t write. He showed no sign of pain or humiliation. To him, the silence between them was a deadly game, and he still held the trumps. He waited.

Yet there had been moments that tested him—like when Mr. Letterblair summoned him the day after Madame Olenska’s departure to discuss the trust Mrs. Manson Mingott wished to establish for her granddaughter. For hours, Archer reviewed the legal terms with his senior partner, all the while sensing that his consultation was less about kinship than about something else—something he feared would be revealed at the end.

"Well," Mr. Letterblair concluded after reviewing the settlement, "the lady can’t deny it’s a generous arrangement. In fact, I must admit she’s been treated very well overall."

"All round?" Archer echoed, with a hint of irony. "Do you mean her husband’s offer to return her money?"
